In the softer later years of the century that followed, a flood of bright young newly-ordained curates would descend like spring rain across the heather-cloaked hills of northern England, settling thick in every parish, brimming with untapped zeal to serve their flocks. But we do not turn to those dust-drenched, sun-scorched later days. We slip back instead, to the crisp, woodsmoke sharp dawn of 1811, in the West Riding of Yorkshire, where wool looms clatter from cottage to mill, and the air hums with unspoken anger, quiet longing, and the sharp, sweet thrill of change waiting just over the moor horizon.

Do not mistake this prelude, dear reader, for a gilded romance of moonlit reverie and grand, sweeping passion, not yet. The first course set before you is plain, unvarnished, as unglamorous as a Monday morning mill whistle rousing half the parish from bed: cold lentils and bitter herbs before the feasting begins. The warmth, the thrills, the soft, unexpected turns of the heart will come later—we only lay the groundwork now.

Back in 1811, that flood of curates had not yet risen. No Pastoral Aid Society stretched out purses to help worn old rectors hire bright young graduates fresh from Oxford or Cambridge. The pious, Puseyite successors to the apostles we know in later years were still swaddled in frilled net caps, being christened in washbasin water, no one guessing that the squalling babe kicking in its long linen nightgown would one day stand in a pulpit flourishing a starched surplice never before seen higher than a parish reading desk.

Yet even in that scarcity, three rare "blossoming rods of Aaron" clustered within twenty miles around the villages of Whinbury, Briarfield, and Nunnely, and we are about to meet them. Step through the gate of the neat white cottage garden on Whinbury's edge, push open the parlour door, and there they sit, halfway through dinner, guests of their fellow curate Mr. Donne, who lodges here with John Gale, a small local clothier. They are young, bursting with restless energy that their staid old vicars keep trying to funnel into school inspections and sick calls—dull, thankless work, as far as the three lads are concerned.

Instead, they chase each other in a never-ending triangle of visits all year round, blundering through snowdrifts and rain-slick mud just to share a dinner, a pot of tea, a late supper. It is not friendship, not exactly—they bicker the whole time they are together. Not piety, for they never speak of it. Not even the food, for each could get as good a roast and suet pudding in his own lodgings. Their three landladies, Mrs. Gale, Mrs. Hogg, and Mrs. Whipp, all sigh that the boys do nothing but run them ragged for no good reason.

Mrs. Gale is particularly frazzled this Thursday. The week is not half done, and on Monday Mr. Peter Malone, the fiery Irish curate of Briarfield, turned up for breakfast and stayed through dinner. On Tuesday, Malone and the shy little flutist curate of Nunnely, Mr. Sweeting, came for tea, stayed for supper, camped in the spare bed, and lingered through Wednesday's breakfast. Now they are both back again, and she knows full well they will stay the night again. The roast beef is tough, the beer flat, and the young parsons do not even bother to be polite about it, sniffing at Yorkshire ways and sneering at their landlady for working her own house without a hired servant, as her mother did before her. Mrs. Gale mutters that the old parsons are worth ten of these college lads, who have no manners at all.

"More bread!" barks Mr. Malone, his thick Irish brogue ringing through the small parlour. Tall, broad-shouldered, with a sharp, high-featured face that looks carved out of Connaught granite, he makes Mrs. Gale's blood run a little hot. She would happily have sliced him along with the loaf if she dared, so sharp is the edge to his command.

The three young men eat as if half starved: the tough roast vanishes, the flat beer disappears, the Yorkshire pudding and heaped vegetable tureens melt like leaves before locusts, the spiced dessert cake that Mrs. Gale's six-year-old son Abraham had been saving for himself vanishes so fast there is not a crumb left. The little boy weeps bitterly in the kitchen when he sees the empty platter.

Upstairs, the curates sip their cheap port wine. Mr. Malone, who has already helped himself to two glasses to his friends' one, grows boisterous and teasing, jibing Mr. Donne about his thin frame, his silly turned-up nose, his frayed chocolate overcoat he wears every time it looks like rain, and his dandy Cockney accent. Next he teases tiny Mr. Sweeting about his small stature, his habit of playing the flute and singing hymns so sweet that all the young girls in his parish dote on him, and the fact that he still writes to his mamma and sisters back home.

Sweeting laughs it off, easygoing, but soon the jabs go too far, and the other two turn on Malone, teasing him about all the village boys that yell "Irish Peter!" after him in the lane, asking if he really carries loaded pistols and a blackthorn shillelagh on his pastoral visits, mocking his thick brogue that makes "veil" sound like "vele" and "storm" sound like "storrum".

Malone explodes, yelling and thumping the mahogany table so hard the decanters jingle, roaring that the English are all snobs, declaring he will fight for his conquered Irish homeland. The other two laugh at him, the little parlour in such an uproar that the Gales in the kitchen half expect the neighbours to send for the constable. But they know the score: the curates' fights never come to anything, no matter how loud they get, and they will be thick as thieves again by breakfast tomorrow.

Just then, a sharp rap raps on the front knocker. John Gale opens the door to find Mr. Helstone, the sharp, stern old vicar of Briarfield, standing in the dark, his shovel hat pulled low over his sharp, hawk-like face.

"Who've you got up in the parlour?" he demands, his nasal voice cutting sharp through the noise. When Gale tells him the three curates are there, dining and carrying on, Mr. Helstone marches straight through the kitchen and up the stairs, throwing the parlour door open without a knock.

The three young men freeze, mid-yell, their mouths hanging open. Mr. Helstone folds his arms across his chest, surveying them like a drill sergeant catching his recruits brawling. "What's this?" he booms, his voice deep and cavernous. "Has Pentecost come back? I could hear seventeen different languages of gibberish all the way down the lane, sounded like the Tower of Babel fallen over in here. Three Babylonish masons, that's what you are, not apostles."

Mr. Donne stammers out an invitation to sit, have a glass of wine, says they were only "settling the Dissenters" together. Mr. Helstone snorts. "Sounded more like you were trying to tear each other's heads off. The Methodist revival down the lane makes less noise than the three of you do, quarrelling over half a pint of port. No wonder the Dissenters are drawing all the weaver girls to their chapels, when the three of you can't even conduct a service to more than the clerk and the organist half the time."

He turns to Malone, his gaze sharp. "I came for you. Do you have your weapons on you? The pistols I gave you, your blackthorn stick?" Malone says he does. "Good. You're coming with me out to Hollow's Mill tonight."

Malone blinks. "What's happening there?"

"Nothing yet, but it might. The mill-owner Moore is bringing two wagonloads of new labour-saving machinery in tonight from Stilbro', and the Luddite weavers are furious. He sent all his trusted workmen out to fetch the machines, and there's barely two women left in the whole mill with him. The countryside is seething with anger at these new frames that will throw half the villagers out of work. You've heard of Mike Hartley, that raving Antinomian weaver who's half drunk half the time, spouting regicide and visions? He's got it into his head that Moore, the cleverest mill-owner in Yorkshire, should be made a sacrifice, a warning to all the rest. Last Friday Mike turned up at my door at midnight, ranting about a wild vision he'd seen in Nunnely Park, of blood running down Fieldhead Lane. It would be foolhardy to leave Moore alone there tonight. You're coming with me to stand guard, just in case."

Sweeting leans forward, eyes wide, full of that childish curiosity the other two lack. "What was the vision Mike saw, sir? Tell us!"

The night hung thick as pitch: not a star pricked through the cloud curtain, not a sliver of moon broke the murk. By daylight those clouds would have been soft, rain-heavy gray, but now they draped the world in sable, swallowing all light save for the tremulous, lurid orange glow leaking from the Stilbro’ ironworks furnaces far to the east, painting the horizon like a smoldering wound.

Malone, the broad-shouldered Irish curate, never spared much thought for nature’s small shifts. He could tramp miles across a capricious April day and never notice the soft, fleeting kiss of sunbeams on hilltops, turning the grass to glistening green, nor see a summer shower drape the peaks in cloud tresses like loose, disheveled hair. He did not pause to contrast that black, streaming, rain-soaked sky above him with the crisp, frosty, unbroken dark of a clear winter night. He did not wonder where the constellations had vanished, nor mourn the deep, velvet black-blue of the open sky, now smothered beneath a low, rolling sea of cold rainwater. He just doggedly put one foot in front of the other, leaning forward against the wind, his hat pushed back off his forehead in the easy, careless Irish way. Tramp, tramp, he went along the flagged causeway where the road was well-kept; splash, splash, he sloshed through mud-caked cart ruts where the paving ran out entirely. He only looked for two landmarks: the sharp spire of Briarfield Church, and farther on, the warm lantern glow of the Redhouse inn.

When he reached that roadside tavern, the golden spill of firelight through a half-curtained window, the glint of glass tumblers on a scrubbed round table, and the low roar of revellers huddled on an oaken settle nearly lured him off his path entirely. He ached for a tumbler of hot whisky and water. In any strange town he would have darted through the door before he had time to think, but the men drinking in that kitchen were all Mr. Helstone’s parishioners — every last one of them knew their curate by sight. He heaved a loud, disappointed sigh, and trundled on past.

Soon he left the main highroad entirely, taking a short cut across flat, monotonous fields that would cut his remaining walk to Hollow’s Mill nearly in half. He loped straight across the grass, vaulting low hedges and loose stone walls without breaking stride. Only one large, irregular building stood between him and the valley below: a sprawling, half-hidden pile with a high gable, a long weathered front, another lower gable, and a thick, towering stack of chimneys, ringed by shadowy old oak trees. No candle glowed in any window. The whole place was dead still, save for rain dripping off the eaves, and a thin, wild little whistle of wind curling around the chimneys and through bare branches. It was Fieldhead, the old estate half-lost to the night, holding its secrets close — secrets not even Malone, who knew every lane and byway in the parish, had guessed at yet.

Once he passed that dark house, the flat fields sloped sharply downward into a vale, and he could hear the gurgle of a swollen mill stream rushing far below. One tiny pinprick of light glimmered in the hollow, and he steered straight for it.

It led him to a little white cottage — bright enough that even through the thick, rain-choked dark he could make out the whitewash on its walls — and he rapped hard on the door. A fresh-faced young servant named Sarah opened it, holding a guttering tallow candle that lit up a narrow passage ending in an equally narrow stair. Two doors draped in soft crimson baize, and a strip of matching crimson carpet running down the steps, popped vivid against pale plaster walls and white scrubbed floor, making the whole tiny house feel warm and clean.

“Mr. Moore at home, I suppose?” Malone asked, shaking rain off his coat sleeves.

“Ah, sir, he’s near, but not in this part of the house,” the girl replied. “He’s down at the mill, in his counting-house.”

Just then one of the crimson baize doors swung open. “Have the wagons come yet, Sarah?” a woman’s voice called, and a head poked out, two little screws of curl paper sticking out on either side of her temples, making it very clear she was half way through preparing for bed. It was no Gorgon’s head, not by a long shot, but big as he was, Malone flinched back bashfully into the rain anyway, as if he’d caught a woman mid-dressing, and stammered “I’ll go find him,” before hurrying at a near-run down a short muddy lane, across a shadowy yard, straight for the huge, black bulk of the mill.

Work hours were long over. All the hands had gone home for the night, the great water-powered machinery was still and silent, and the main mill doors were locked tight. Malone tramped all the way around the soot-stained outer wall until he spotted another thin sliver of light peeking through a crack in a side door, and banged on the wood hard with the thick end of his shillelagh, beating out a loud, rousing tattoo. A key turned in the lock, and the door creaked open.

“Is that you, Joe Scott? What news of the wagons, Joe?” the voice that spoke was sharp, tight with tired expectation.

“Nah, it’s me. Mr. Helstone sent me, to check you’d got in safe,” Malone rumbled, stepping over the threshold, rain dripping off his cuffs onto the dirty floor.

“Oh! Mr. Malone.” The voice held the faintest, flicker of disappointment, so subtle you might have missed it if you weren’t listening close. After a short pause, it continued, polite but a little formally distant: “Do come in, Mr. Malone. I’m very sorry Mr. Helstone thought he needed to drag you all this way on such a night. I told him there was no need for it at all — but do walk forwards.”

Malone followed the speaker through a dark, unlit store room where he could make out nothing but stacked bales of cloth, and into a bright, warm counting house beyond. After an hour of picking his way through fog and pitch dark, the room felt almost dazzling to his eyes: its only light came from a crackling, roaring fire on the hearth, and a sleek, bright lamp burning on the centre of the plain wooden table. The floor was bare, uncarpeted boards. Three or four stiff-backed, green-painted chairs looked like they had been salvaged from an old farm kitchen, and a heavy, solid oak desk, plus a few framed sheets on the stone-coloured walls holding building plans, garden layouts, and new machinery designs, made up the rest of the furniture.

Plain as it was, Malone could not have loved it more. He hung up his soaking wet overcoat and dripping hat by the door, dragged one of those stiff old chairs right up to the hearth, and held his knees so close to the red-hot grate they nearly touched the bars.

“Comfortable little quarters you’ve got here, Mr. Moore, all snug and private,” he said happily.

“They do well enough. My sister would be glad to have you in the cottage, if you’d rather sit somewhere warmer,” Moore offered.

“Not at all! Ladies are far better left to themselves. I was never much of a ladies’ man, you know. You don’t mistake me for my friend Sweeting, do you?”

“Sweeting? Which one is that? The gentleman in the chocolate overcoat, or the tiny little one?”

“The little one, curate of Nunnely! The man who’s half in love with all six of the Misses Sykes, ha ha!”

“Better be half in love with all six than head over heels for one of them, I’d have thought, in that family,” Moore said, a faint, dry smile tugging at his mouth.

“But he is head over heels for one of them, on top of all that. When me and Donne teased him to pick a favourite out of the whole lot, he named — guess who?”

“Dora, of course,” Moore said, still smiling quiet, “or Harriet.”

“Ha! You’ve got an excellent guess! What made you hit on those two?”

“They’re the tallest, the handsomest, and Dora at least is the sturdiest. Your friend Mr. Sweeting is such a slight little fellow, I just figured he’d go for the complete contrast, as men so often do.”

“You’re dead right, it’s Dora. But he hasn’t got a chance, has he, Moore?”

“What has Mr. Sweeting, besides his tiny curacy, to offer her?”

The question tickled Malone so much he laughed for three full minutes before he could catch his breath enough to answer. “What has Sweeting? Why, David had his harp or his flute, same sort of thing. He’s got a pinchbeck watch, a pinchbeck ring, a pinchbeck eyeglass. That’s all he’s got, the daft lad.”

“How does he plan to keep Miss Sykes in new gowns, then?”

“Ha ha! That’s excellent. I’ll roast him for that presumption next time I see him. But he must be expecting old Christopher Sykes to settle something handsome, right? The man’s rolling in it, isn’t he? They live in that great big grand house.”

“Sykes runs an enormous cloth manufacturing concern, that’s true.”

“So he must be wealthy, eh?”

“So he’s got every penny of his capital tied up in keeping that concern running. These days, he’s about as likely to pull money out of his business to give dowries to six daughters as I am to tear down that little cottage outside the mill, and build a house twice the size of Fieldhead on its ruins.”

“Wait, you know what I heard people saying about you the other day?” Malone leaned forward, grinning.

“Nothing good, I’ll wager. Your Briarfield gossips are capable of saying far sillier things than that.”

“They say you’re going to take Fieldhead on a long lease — I swear it looked like the loneliest, dismalest place on earth when I passed it tonight — and that you’re going to install one of the Misses Sykes there as mistress, that you’re getting married, ha ha! It’s Dora, isn’t it? You said she was the prettiest.”

Moore huffed a quiet, exasperated little laugh, staring into the fire. “I wonder how many times the good people of this parish have decided I’m getting married in the two years I’ve been here. They’ve assigned me every single unmarried woman within ten miles. First it was the two Miss Wynns, dark one then the fair one, then the red-haired Miss Armitage, then the middle-aged Ann Pearson. Now I’m supposed to marry the whole Sykes tribe. God knows where all this nonsense comes from. I visit no one. I avoid female company just as much as you do, Mr. Malone. If I ever drive over to Whinbury, it’s only to knock on Sykes or Pearson’s counting house door, and all we talk about is cloth we can’t sell, workers we can’t pay, mills we can’t run, not courtships, settlements, dowries. Those things take up all our minds right now, pushing out all that silly romantic nonsense entirely.”

“Couldn’t agree with you more, Moore. If there’s one thing I hate it’s that whole silly idea of marriage being just sentiment — two daft beggars agreeing to tie their penniless lives together over some stupid feeling. Humbug! But a good, solid advantageous match, built on shared interests and sensible stability? That’s not half bad, now is it?”

Moore only nodded absently, as if he was barely listening. The whole topic held no interest for him at all. He sat staring at the flames for a long minute, his forehead furrowed, before he suddenly tilted his head.

“Hark!” he said. “Did you hear wheels?”

He stood up, crossed to the window, and threw the sash open, listening hard to the dark, rain-soaked yard outside. A second later he closed it again, shaking his head. “Only the wind picking up, and the mill stream swelling, rushing down the gully. I expected those wagons at six o’clock. It’s nearly nine now.”

“Seriously, do you think bringing this new machinery in is going to land you in danger?” Malone asked. “Helstone seems convinced the frame breakers will come after you.”

“I only wish the new frames were here safe, locked inside these mill walls. Once I get them installed, I defy every last frame breaker in the shire to try and touch them. My mill is my castle. Let them come, and they’ll get what’s coming to them.”

“Ugh, those low scoundrels aren’t worth wasting fear on,” Malone said, pensive. “I almost wish a whole party of them would turn up tonight! But the road was dead quiet when I walked in. I didn’t see a single soul stirring.”

“You came along the Redhouse road, didn’t you?” Moore asked.

“Aye.”

“Then you wouldn’t have seen a thing. The risk all lies on the Stilbro’ side of the valley.”

“So you do think there’s real danger, then?”

“They’ve done the exact same thing to every other manufacturer in the district. The only difference is, most of them turned into rabbits the second they got attacked. Sykes, for example, when his dressing shed got burned to the ground, when his cloth got torn off the tenters and left in rags out in the fields? He never even tried to find who did it, never punished a single one of the miscreants. He gave up meeker than a rabbit in a ferret’s jaws. I’m not like that. If I know myself, I’ll stand by my trade, my mill, and my machinery, come what may.”

“Helstone says those three things are your gods,” Malone chuckled. “That the Orders in Council are just another name for the seven deadly sins to you, that Castlereagh is your Antichrist, and the whole war party is his army of devils.”

“And he’s not wrong. I abhor all of it, because every last bit of it is ruining me. It stands in my way. I can’t move forward with my plans, I get blocked at every single turn.”

“But you’re rich and thriving, aren’t you, Moore?”

“I’m rich in bales of cloth I can’t sell. Come look in my warehouse sometime — it’s piled all the way up to the rafters. Roakes and Pearson are in the exact same mess. America used to be our main market, and the Orders in Council cut it right off, no warning.”

Malone didn’t have much patience for talk of trade laws and blocked markets. He started tapping the heels of his boots together on the hearth stones, and yawned so wide his jaw ached.

“Just think,” Moore went on, too wrapped up in his own worries to notice his guest’s boredom, “all those silly gossips in Whinbury and Briarfield keep badgering me about getting married! As if there’s nothing else to do with a whole life but hang around paying ‘attention’ to some young lady, march her down the church aisle, go off on a bridal tour, do the rounds of all the neighbourhood calls, and then settle down to have a house full of brats. To hell with it all!” He caught himself mid-outburst, and softened his tone. “I do believe women think of nothing else all day long, so naturally they assume men’s heads are full of the same nonsense.”

“Right, right, exactly,” Malone nodded, fidgeting in his chair, clearly wanting something the conversation wasn’t giving him. This time Moore noticed his restlessness, and understood at once.

“Mr. Malone, you must be starving after that long wet walk. I’ve been such a bad host I entirely forgot hospitality.”

“Not at all,” Malone protested — though his face lit up like a lantern, making it very clear he had been waiting for exactly that. Moore stood up and opened a large oak cupboard tucked against the wall.

“I make a point of keeping everything I need stocked right here,” he explained. “I don’t like running to the cottage for every bite to eat or sip to drink. Half the time I spend the whole evening and sleep right here in the mill with Joe Scott, no need to disturb anyone. Sometimes I work as my own night watchman. I don’t need much sleep, and on fine nights I love wandering around the hollow with my musket for an hour or two. Mr. Malone, do you know how to cook a mutton chop?”

“Try me. I did hundreds of them back at college, no bother.”

“Then there’s a plate full of chops right there, and a gridiron. Turn them fast, you know the trick to keep all the juice in, right?”

“Never you fear. Hand me a knife and fork, will you?”

Malone rolled up his coat cuffs, and threw himself into cooking the chops with enormous vigour. Moore set plates, a loaf of crusty bread, a black glass bottle and two tumblers on the table, then pulled out a small copper kettle, filled it with water from a big stone jar in the corner, set it on the hearth beside the hissing gridiron, and fetched out lemons, sugar, and a tiny china punch bowl. He was just stirring the sugar into the hot water when a knock rapped on the outer door.

“Sarah?” he called.

“Yes, sir. Will you be coming in to supper tonight, sir?” the servant called back.

“No, I won’t be in the cottage at all. I’m sleeping in the mill tonight. Lock all the doors, and tell your mistress to go straight to bed.”

He came back to the counting house, just as Malone flipped the last chop, his face ruddy from the fire, a brown, juicy sheen on the meat.

“You run a tight ship, no mistake about it,” Malone said approvingly. “You’re not under petticoat rule like poor Sweeting. That lad’s going to spend his whole life being bossed around by women. Now us two, Moore — that’s a perfect chop, look at all that gravy — us two, we’ll never let a woman run our households if we ever do get around to marrying.”

“I don’t know, I never think about it. If the woman in question was handsome, and easy to get along with, what would be the harm?”

“The chops are done. Is the punch brewed yet?”

Cheerfulness, it turns out, relies just as much on the fire burning in a man’s breast as it does on the sun in the sky above him. I note this obvious little truth only because I happen to know full well that Messrs. Helstone and Moore trotted out of the mill-yard gates at the head of their tiny rescue party in the highest of spirits. When the glow of one of the three pedestrian party’s lanterns caught Mr. Moore’s face, you could spot a rare, lively spark dancing in his dark eyes, a new, bright vivacity warming his sharp, dusky features. And when the light fell on the rector, his normally hard, severe face was split wide with a grin, all ashine with unrepentant glee.

You’d think a drizzling, bone-chilling night and a foolhardy, perilous expedition wouldn’t be circumstances to liven up men soaked to the skin and riding straight into trouble. Any of the rioters who’d been working the frame-breaking job on Stilbro’ Moor would have been only too happy to shoot both leaders dead from behind a stone wall, and both men knew it perfectly. The fact of it, instead of damping their mood, stoked their high spirits all the higher: they were both made of steely nerves and steady, unshaking hearts, and the risk of it all made them feel more alive than they had in months.

I know, reader—you needn’t remind me that a parson is meant to be a man of peace, not a warrior. I haven’t forgotten a clergyman’s mission, who he serves, whose message he carries. But if you hate parsons, don’t expect me to follow you down that bitter, narrow road of blind rage against the cloth. Mr. Helstone was not diabolical, not by a long shot. He had simply missed his calling: he ought to have been a soldier, and circumstance had made him a priest instead. For the rest, he was conscientious, hard-headed, brave, stern, loyal to a fault, and almost entirely without soft, yielding sympathy—ungentle, prejudiced, rigid, but unwaveringly true to his principles, honourable, sharp, and sincere. Men aren’t always carved perfectly to fit their professions, and there’s no sense cursing a man because his vocation sits a little awkwardly on his shoulders.

Helstone was cursed by half his parishioners, though, and adored by the other half—the common fate of men who love fiercely and hate fiercely, who cling as hard to their prejudices as they do to their core beliefs. You’d think two men in such good spirits, united in a single cause, would ride side by side chatting amicably. Not these two. Both with sharp, bilious natures, they rarely came into contact without chafing each other’s tempers raw. Their favourite point of contention was the war. Helstone was a staunch high Tory (Tories were still thick on the ground in those days), and Moore was a bitter, unapologetic Whig, at least on the question of the war that was ruining his mill and starving his workforce—he cared nothing for other British political debates otherwise. He loved to infuriate Helstone by declaring Bonaparte unbeatable, taunting England and Europe for their useless efforts to stand against him, arguing it made more sense to yield sooner than later when Bonaparte would inevitably crush all his enemies and rule the continent.

Helstone could barely stand to hear the words. The only reason he didn’t cane Moore on the spot was the knowledge that Moore was a half-foreign outcast, only half British, the rest of his blood made sharp and strange by the foreign roots he couldn’t shake. He also had a sneaking, grudging respect for the dogged, unyielding tone with which Moore stated his unpopular opinions.

As the party turned onto the Stilbro’ road, the wind hit them full in the face, rain lashing hard against their cheeks. Moore had been teasing his companion all the while, and now, braced by the raw chill of the breeze, he began to goad him in earnest.
“Does your Peninsular news still sit well with you?” he asked.
“What do you mean?” the rector snapped, surly.
“I mean, do you still have faith in that Baal you call Lord Wellington?”
“And what on earth are you on about now?”
“Do you still believe that wooden-faced, pebble-hearted idol of England has the power to send fire down from heaven to burn up the French army you’re so eager to throw yourself against?”
“I believe Wellington will flog Bonaparte’s marshals straight into the sea the day he lifts a finger to do it,” Helstone growled.
“My dear sir, you can’t be serious. Bonaparte’s marshals are great men, guided by a master spirit that’s omnipotent. Your Wellington is nothing but a plodding, commonplace martinet, his slow, mechanical movements further crippled by an idiot home government back in London.”
“Wellington is the soul of England. He is the champion of a just cause, the perfect representative of a powerful, resolute, sensible, honest nation.”
“Your just cause, as far as I can tell, is just putting that filthy, useless Ferdinand back on a throne he disgraced. Your representative of an honest people is a dull-witted drover, taking orders from a duller-witted farmer—and against them you have victory and unbeatable genius arrayed on the other side.”
“Against legitimacy stands usurpation. Against modest, righteous, brave resistance to greed stands boastful, double-tongued, selfish ambition to steal what is not theirs. God defends the right!”
“God often defends the powerful.”
“What? Do you think the handful of Israelites standing dryshod on the far side of the Red Sea were more powerful than Pharaoh’s army drawn up on the other? More numerous? Better armed? They were a ragged, overwrought band of bondsmen, oppressed for four hundred years, their ranks thinned by women and children. Their masters, roaring to follow them through the divided flood, were pampered, brutal men, armed, horsed, riding chariots, while the Hebrews had nothing but shepherds’ crooks and masons’ tools. Their own great leader carried nothing but a rod. But right was on their side. The God of battles was on their side. Pharaoh’s army drowned to the last man. ‘The depths covered them, they sank to the bottom as a stone.’ The Lord’s right hand dashed the enemy to pieces.”
“You’re all right—only you got the parallel backwards. France is Israel. Napoleon is Moses. Europe, with its bloated old empires and rotten dynasties, is corrupt Egypt. Gallant, young France is the Twelve Tribes, and her vigorous ‘usurper’ is the shepherd of Horeb.”
“I scorn to answer you.”
Moore answered himself anyway, muttering under his breath. “Oh, in Italy he was as great as any Moses! He was exactly the man to organize the regeneration of nations. It still puzzles me how the conqueror of Lodi could stoop to become a petty, stupid emperor, a vulgar fraud. And how a people who once called themselves republicans could sink back so shamelessly into slavery. I despise them. If England had marched as far towards civilization as France did, she’d never have retreated that far.”
“You don’t mean to say that besotted imperial France is any worse than bloody republican France?” Helstone demanded, furious.
“I don’t have to say anything,” Moore smiled dryly. “I can think what I please, you know—about France, about England, about revolutions, regicides, restorations, the divine right of kings you love to preach about, the duty of non-resistance, the utter insanity of this whole war, and—”

His sentence was cut short by the rapid rumble of a gig rolling up fast, stopping dead in the middle of the road. Both men had been so wrapped up in their argument they hadn’t heard it approaching until it was almost upon them.
“Nah, maister—did th’ wagons hit home all safe?” a voice called from the vehicle.
“Is that Joe Scott?”
“Ay, ay!” a second voice called back. The three pedestrians with lanterns had fallen far behind, the two riders having outpaced them easily, and the gig’s lamp cast a warm glow over two men huddled inside. “Ay, Mr. Moore, it’s Joe Scott, and I’m bringing him back to you in a right bonny pickle. I found him up on the moor, him and three of his lads. What’ll you give me for restoring him safe and sound?”
“Why, my heartfelt thanks,” Moore laughed. “I can ill afford to lose a man as good as Joe. That’s you, Mr. Yorke, isn’t it? I’d know that voice anywhere.”
“Ay, lad, it’s me. I was coming home from Stilbro’ market, whipping the pony on as fast as he’d go—they say these aren’t safe times, thanks to a useless government!—when I heard a groan right out of the heather. Some folks would have spurred faster, but I’ve naught to fear. Not a lad in these parts would harm me, and if they tried I’d give them as good as I got. I called out, ‘Is there something wrong there?’ ‘Deed there is,’ someone answered, like he was speaking out of the ground. ‘What’s happened? Speak sharp,’ I told them. ‘Nobbut four of us lying in a ditch,’ says Joe, as calm as you like. I told them they ought to be ashamed of themselves, bid them get up and move or I’d lash them with my gig whip, for I could see they weren’t hurt bad. ‘We’d ha’ been gone an hour sin’, but our legs are tied tight with bands,’ Joe says. So I got down, cut all their bonds loose with my penknife, and Scott rode back with me to tell me the whole tale. The other three are trundling along behind as fast as their feet can carry them.”
“Well, I’m deeply obliged to you, Mr. Yorke.”
“Are you, my lad? You know you’re not, but no matter. Look, here come the rest of your lads. And by the Lord, there’s another group coming with their lights bobbing like Gideon’s army, and we’ve got the parson with us—good evening, Mr. Helstone—we’ve got eleven strong men between us, horses and a gig and all. If we run into those starved ragamuffin frame-breakers, we could win a grand victory. Every last one of us could be a Wellington, just like you want, Mr. Helstone! We’d write such a grand spread for the *Stilbro’ Courier* that Briarfield would be famous. I dare say we’ll get a full column and a half out of this night’s work at the very least.”
“And I’ll promise you no less,” the rector grinned. “I’ll write the article myself.”
“Sartain you will! And mind you recommend that the lot who broke your frames and tied Joe Scott’s legs up hang without benefit of clergy. It’s a hanging offence, no two ways about it.”
“If I was judging them, I’d give them short shrift,” Moore put in. “But this time, I mean to leave them well alone, give them enough rope to hang themselves before long.”
“Leave them alone, do ye, Moore? Do you promise that?”
“Promise? No. I only mean I won’t go out of my way to hunt them down. But if one of them happens to stumble across my path—”
“You’ll snap him up, of course. You’d rather they do something worse than just stop a wagon before you settle scores. Well, we’ll say no more about it now. Here we are at my door, gentlemen. You and your lads will all step in and have a bite and a warm drink, won’t you?”

Moore and Helstone tried to protest, but Yorke pressed the invitation so warmly, the night was so raw and miserable, and the soft golden glow spilling out of his muslin-curtained windows looked so inviting, that they gave in at last. Mr. Yorke hopped down from the gig, left the pony in the care of a man who came out of an outbuilding, and led the way inside.

You might have noticed Mr. Yorke slips between broad Yorkshire brogue and crisp, perfect formal English without even noticing. His manners flip just as fast: one moment blunt and rough as a moor stone, the next warm, affable, and refined. His station wasn’t easy to guess from his speech, but the inside of his house told the whole story. He sent the workmen off to the kitchen, promising them a hearty supper, then led Helstone and Moore through the front door into a matted hall lined almost to the ceiling with paintings, then into a great parlour with a magnificent roaring fire blazing in the grate. There was no gaudy show of wealth, but taste was everywhere—the kind of taste belonging to a travelled man, a scholar, a gentleman. Italian pastoral landscapes decked the walls, each a genuine, carefully selected work of art, their sunny skies and soft blue distant hills glowing even in candlelight. A guitar and a stack of sheet music rested on the sofa, cameos and beautiful miniatures lined the mantel, a set of elegant Grecian vases sat at either end, and two polished bookcases held rows of well-loved, neatly arranged books.

Yorke bade them sit, rang for wine, and gave the servant orders to feed the men in the kitchen. The rector stayed standing, stiff as a board, clearly not comfortable in his host’s home, and refused the glass of wine offered him.
“Suit yourself,” Yorke shrugged. “I reckon you’re thinking of old Eastern customs, Mr. Helstone, that you won’t eat or drink under my roof for fear we’d be forced to be friends. But I’m not that superstitious. You could drink that whole decanter, give me a bottle of the very best claret from your own cellar, and I’d still fight you tooth and nail at every vestry meeting and magistrate’s session we happen to cross paths at.”
“That’s exactly what I’d expect of you, Mr. Yorke.”
“Does it suit you, a man your age, to be tramping out after rioters on a wet night like this?”
“Doing my duty always suits me. And this particular duty is a pleasure. Hunting down vermin is a noble occupation, fit even for an archbishop.”
“Fit for you, at any rate. But where’s your curate? Did he run off to visit some sick parishioner, or is he out hunting vermin in another direction?”
“He’s on garrison duty down at Hollow’s Mill.”
“I hope you left him a dram of wine to keep his courage up, Bob,” Yorke said, turning to Moore, who had flopped into a deep old oak chair by the fireside. “Move, Robert! Get up, lad! That seat’s mine. Take the sofa, or any three other chairs you like, but not that one. It belongs to me, and nobody else.”
“Why are you so fussy over that one chair, Mr. Yorke?” Moore laughed, lazily standing up to oblige him.
“My father sat in it before me. That’s all the answer you’ll get, and it’s a better reason than Mr. Helstone has for most of his silly little opinions.”
“Moore, are you ready to leave?” the rector asked.
“Nay, Robert’s not ready, and I’m not ready to let him go. He’s a bad lad, and needs a good talking to.”
“Why, sir? What have I done?”
“Made enemies of every last soul within ten miles of you.”
“What do I care? What difference does it make if your Yorkshire louts hate me or like me?”
“Ay, there it is. The lad’s a foreigner through and through, outcast among us. His father would never have talked like that. Go back to Antwerp, where you were born and bred, mauvaise tête!”
“Mauvaise tête vous-même; je ne fais que mon devoir; quant à vos lourdauds de paysans, je m'en moque!”
“En revanche, mon garçon, nos lourdauds de paysans se moqueront de toi; sois en certain,” Yorke replied, his French almost as perfect and natural as Moore’s own, the accent spotless.

They bantered back and forth in that quick, rolling tongue, old, half-forgotten grievances from Moore’s childhood in Antwerp rising to the surface, until Yorke fell quiet, leaning back in his heavy carved three-cornered oak chair. And now, while he sits there, warm firelight gilding his sharp, witty face, I will pause to paint the full portrait of this contradictory, French-speaking Yorkshire gentleman.

MR. YORKE (_continued_)

He was the very paragon of a Yorkshire gentleman, through and through. At fifty-five, silver hair streaked no softer than new-fallen frost, he looked older at first glance still, his broad, low forehead crisscrossed with lines earned from a lifetime of hard work and sharper wit. The harsh, bracing bluntness of the north was written into every line of his face, just as it laced his speech; not a single soft, delicate Norman aristocratic line lingered in his features, his visage carved from unpolished, unclassic, thoroughly English stock. Fancy folk might dismiss it as vulgar, but anyone with half a brain would call it unforgettable, every furrow thrumming with shrewdness, sagacity, and a raw, unapologetic originality no polished town gentleman could ever fake. It was a face set against being led, and utterly impossible to drive — indocile, scornful, sharp-edged with sarcasm, belonging to a man who answered to no one. He stood well over average height, wiry and straight-backed, carrying himself with a quiet, unyielding integrity that banished any hint of the bumpkin anyone might have expected from his rough-hewn features.

Painting his character is far trickier than painting his face, though. If you, reader, are hoping for some perfect, benevolent, wise old philanthropist, you will be disappointed. He spoke with sense and a spark of genuine kindness to Robert Moore a little while back, but that does not mean he was always just, or gentle.

For one thing, he had not a scrap of what phrenologists call the organ of veneration — a gap that warped his judgement on every matter that called for awe. For another, he barely possessed the organ of comparison, that little nudge that lets a man step into another’s shoes, stripping him of easy sympathy. And last, his nature held far too little benevolence and ideality, draining most of the soft, glowing warmth out of his soul, and for him dimming every trace of that soft, divine grace the rest of the world stumbles across now and then.

His total lack of veneration made him unsparingly intolerant of anyone who claimed rank over him: kings, nobles, priests, dynasties, parliaments, every stuffy institution, most of their laws, all their silly forms, their hoary old claims to special privilege — to him they were all rubbish, plain and simple. He saw no use, no joy in any of it, and was fully convinced the world would be far better off if all those lofty high places were razed to the ground, and the men perched on top crushed in the fall. That same missing reverence left him numb to that electric, thrumming delight of admiring something truly beautiful for its own sake; it dried up a hundred quiet, pure sources of joy, withered a thousand small vivid pleasures no one else thinks twice about. He was no atheist, though he belonged to no chapel or church, but his faith could never be the soft, awe-struck kind known to people who know how to venerate what is greater than themselves. He believed in God and a heaven beyond this life, but his God and his heaven belonged to a man who never knew the hush of awe, the flight of imagination, the soft pull of tender affection.

His stunted powers of comparison made him a walking contradiction: he would spout fine, noble speeches about mutual tolerance and forbearance, all the while nurturing a bigoted, unreasoning hatred for entire classes of people. He spoke of “parsons” and everyone connected to them, of “lords” and all their hangers-on, with a harshness, even an insolence, that was as unjust as it was unbearable. He could not imagine what it was like to stand in the shoes of the people he railed against, could not weigh their faults against the temptations they faced, their flaws against the disadvantages they were born into. He could never picture how his own circumstances would warp him if he was placed in their position, and he would often roar out ferocious, tyrannical threats against people he thought were tyrants themselves. If you judged him by his words alone, he would have used arbitrary, even cruel methods to bring about the freedom and equality he ranted about so often. Equality! Yes, Mr. Yorke talked a great deal about equality, but at heart he was proud as Beelzebub — kind as a father to his workpeople, generous to everyone beneath him who never presumed to push for power over him, but haughty and unyielding to any man the rest of the world dared call his superior. Rebellion ran in his blood: he would not be ruled, his father before him would not be ruled, and his children after him would never bear control either.

That thin streak of general benevolence in his nature made him wildly impatient of foolishness, and every small weakness that grated against his sharp, shrewd temperament. There was no check at all on his biting sarcasm; he was no merciful man, and he would cut and cut again, never pausing to notice how deep his thrusts went, never caring how much pain he left in his wake.

As for his near-total lack of poetic imagination? That can barely be called a flaw. He had a fine ear for music, a sharp, true eye for colour and form, so his taste was impeccable — and who, after all, cares much for imagination anyway? Most folk think it a dangerous, silly trait, close to weakness, verging on frenzy, a kind of sickness rather than a gift.

Only the people who have it, or think they do, would ever disagree. Listen to them talk, and you would believe their hearts would turn cold if that sweet elixir of imagination did not flow through their veins, their eyes dim if that inner flame did not light up the world for them, their lives turn barren and lonely if that strange, gentle companion ever abandoned them. You would think it gave spring an extra gilded hope, summer a soft magic, autumn a tranquil warmth, winter a comfort no ordinary man could ever know. It is an illusion, of course, but the people who live by that dream cling to it tighter than they would cling to solid gold.

Since Hiram Yorke had no poetic imagination of his own, he considered it a thoroughly useless quality in anyone else. Painters and musicians he could tolerate, even encourage, since he could enjoy the fruit of their work — he could see the beauty of a great painting, feel the thrill of a fine melody — but a quiet poet, no matter what fire burned in his breast, no matter what genius churned under his quiet exterior? If that poet could not add up a ledger in a counting house, or strike a sharp bargain at the Piece Hall, he could have lived despised and died scorned right under Yorke’s nose.

And since there are a hundred Hiram Yorkes in the world, it is a mercy that the true poet, for all his outward meekness, often hides a tough, unyielding spirit under his placid surface, sharpens his wits even as he cultivates his gentleness, and can measure every inch of the small-minded men who look down on him, and rightly judge how little their fancy worldly achievements are worth. It is a happy thing that he can find his own joy, his own deep communion with the great wild natural world he loves, completely independent of the men who see no value in him, and for whom he feels no fondness at all. It is only just that when the world turns its cold, dark face to him — as it often does, for he gives it that same careless, distant coldness first — he can keep a bright, warm glow burning in his own chest that makes all the world feel soft and genial to him, even while strangers think his life is nothing but a frozen polar winter with no sun to ever warm it. The true poet is never to be pitied, and he often smirks in secret when some misguided do-gooder whines about his supposed wrongs. Even when utilitarians sit in judgement over him, declaring him and his art entirely useless, he hears their sentence with such broad, unyielding, merciless contempt for the self-righteous Pharisees who condemn him, that he deserves to be chided for his arrogance far more than he deserves any sympathy. But none of these thoughts belonged to Mr. Yorke, and it is Mr. Yorke we are concerned with here.

I have laid out his faults for you, reader — but his good points are equally clear. He was one of the most honourable, capable men in the whole of Yorkshire; even the people who disliked him could not help but respect him. The poor adored him, for he was unfailingly kind, fatherly and generous to everyone who lived in his cottages. To his workpeople he was considerate and warm: if he had to lay them off, he would move heaven and earth to find them new work elsewhere, and if he could not, he would help them move their whole families to a district where jobs could be found. It must also be said that for all his hatred of being ruled himself, he knew how to rule with steady, unyielding strength; if any of his hands showed signs of insubordination, he crushed that unrest in the bud, pulled it up like a noxious weed before it could ever spread across his mill. With his own affairs running so smoothly, he saw no reason not to speak with harsh, unvarnished severity of other mill owners who struggled with unrest, dismissing all their troubles as their own fault, and happily disavowing the other masters’ interests to openly advocate for the workers.

His family was the oldest, most established line in the whole district, and while he was not the richest man for miles around, he was one of the most influential. He had a fine education, and as a young man, before the French Revolution broke out, he had travelled all across the Continent. He spoke French and Italian like a native, and during a two-year stay in Italy he collected a trove of fine paintings and beautiful rare artefacts that lined the walls of his home. When he chose to, he carried himself with the polished, easy grace of an old-school gentleman, and when he wanted to please, his conversation was wildly entertaining and utterly original. He deliberately spoke in his thick, rolling Yorkshire burr instead of the more refined standard English, and declared that “A Yorkshire burr is as much better than a Cockney lisp as a bull’s bellow is better than a rat’s squeak.”

He knew everyone for ten miles around, and everyone knew him — but he had almost no close intimates. A man who was utterly original himself, he hated all dull, ordinary, commonplace people; a rough, sharp, vivid character, high or low, would always win his favour, while a refined, insipid person, no matter how grand their title, made him actively averse. He would happily spend an hour chatting freely with a sharp-witted weaver in his mill, or a queer, shrewd old widow living in one of his cottages, but would grudge a single minute of his time to a posh commonplace gentleman, or the most elegant, fashionable frivolous lady. He carried this preference to an extreme, forgetting that there could be amiable, even admirable people who had no gift for being eccentric and original. Still, he made exceptions: he was peculiarly at ease with plain, guileless souls who cared nothing for refinement, barely had a scrap of intellectual curiosity, and could not even begin to understand the sharp, brilliant parts of his own mind. Those people never winced at his rudeness, never got hurt by his sarcasm, never picked apart his words or his choices to judge him. He was the undisputed lord of that kind of company, and while they followed his lead without question, they never even noticed his superiority, so they were tractable without ever being servile. Their unthinking, easy, unselfconscious indifference was as comfortable to him as the solid oak chair he sat on, or the worn stone floor under his boots.

It was clear he was not unkind to Robert Moore, and there were a handful of small reasons he held a faint partiality for the man. It sounds silly, but the first of them was that Moore spoke English with a distinct foreign lilt, and French with a perfect, unaccented purity, his dark, gaunt face with its fine, worn lines looking nothing like a typical British or Yorkshire man’s. Those small, trivial details tugged at old, happy memories: they brought him back to his travelling days, his long-lost youth. He had seen faces just like Moore’s wandering the streets of Italian cities, heard voices just like his in Parisian cafes and theatres. When he looked at the foreign mill owner and listened to him speak, he felt twenty years old again.

Second, he had known Moore’s father, and had done business with him for years — a less happy tie, admittedly, since his own firm had been tied up with the elder Moore’s, and had lost a fair bit of money when that business collapsed. Third, he had seen that Robert Moore was a sharp, shrewd man of business, and he was sure the man would eventually turn his fortunes around, no matter what it took; he respected his grit, his determination, and even his unapologetic hardness. The fourth link between them was even more practical: Mr. Yorke was one of the guardians for the young minor who owned the estate that Hollow’s Mill was built on, so Moore had to consult him constantly for permission for all the alterations and improvements he planned to carry out.

As for the other guest sitting in Yorke’s parlour that night, Mr. Helstone? There was a double, unyielding antipathy between the host and the parson — the natural dislike of two completely opposite characters, stoked by years of old history. The free-thinking, liberty-loving Yorke hated the rigid formalist, the disciplinarian parson. On top of that, rumour said they had once been rivals for the same woman’s hand, long ago.

As a young man, Mr. Yorke had always been drawn to lively, dashing, high-spirited women: a bold, graceful figure, a sharp wit, a quick tongue, that was what had always attracted him before. He never once proposed to any of those brilliant belles he had chased around local balls, though — until the day he fell hard, head over heels, for a girl who was the exact opposite of every woman he had ever admired before. Her name was Mary Cave, and she had the face of a marble Madonna, stillness made flesh. It did not matter that she only answered him in monosyllables when he spoke to her, that his sighs seemed to go unheard, his glances went unreturned, that she never agreed with his opinions, barely smiled at his jokes, and gave him no respect or attention at all. For some unnameable reason that he never could have explained, Mary Cave was perfect to him, and he loved her more than he had ever loved anything.

Mr. Helstone, then the new young curate of Briarfield, loved her too — or at least, he thought he did. He had none of the humble, reverent adoration that subdued every other suitor of hers; he saw her far more clearly, far more plainly, than anyone else did, so he kept control of his own feelings, and of her. She accepted his first proposal, and they were married.

Nature never intended Mr. Helstone to be a good husband, least of all to a quiet, soft, gentle wife like Mary. He thought that if a woman never complained, she could never be unhappy. If she never spoke up to say she was bored, solitude could never irk her. If she never stated a preference for this thing or an aversion to that, she had no preferences or aversions at all, so there was no point in ever asking what she wanted. He made no pretence of understanding women, or seeing them as equal to men; they were a different, almost certainly far inferior order of being. A wife could never be her husband’s companion, let alone his confidante, let alone his support. A year or two into their marriage, she was barely relevant to him at all. So when she faded away and died one day — so gradually that he barely even noticed her decline, while everyone else in the parish saw her wasting away for months — he felt his loss barely at all, or so it seemed. He grieved dry-eyed, sober and unemotional, which scandalized his old housekeeper and Mary’s lady’s maid, who had sat with her through her illness and knew far more of the deep, tender heart hidden under her quiet stillness than her husband ever had. The two gossiped over her corpse, embellishing stories of her slow decline, working themselves up into a fury against the austere little parson sitting in the next room poring over his papers, entirely unaware of how everyone was judging him.

Before Mary was even buried, rumours were flying all across the parish that she had died of a broken heart. Those rumours swelled, growing into tales of cruel mistreatment, into lurid made-up details of harsh abuse at her husband’s hands — lies, all of them, but lies the neighbourhood ate up greedily. Mr. Yorke heard the stories, and he believed every word. He was already no friend to his successful rival, and even though he was married now to a woman who was the complete opposite of Mary in every possible way, he never forgot the great, crushing disappointment of his youth. When he learned that the woman who would have been his whole world had been neglected, even abused, by the man who stole her from him, he conceived a deep, bitter, unshakable hatred for Helstone that would never fade.

Helstone was barely aware of how deep that hatred ran. He never knew how fiercely Yorke had loved Mary Cave, how gutted he had been when she married another man, and he had no idea of the cruel lies about his treatment of her that everyone in the parish knew by heart. He thought his disagreement with Yorke was purely political, purely religious. If he had ever learned the real truth, no force on earth could have convinced him to step across his old rival’s threshold.

Mr. Yorke never went back to lecturing Robert Moore. The conversation soon picked up again, growing more general, but still sharp and disputatious. The unrest spreading across the country, the recent spate of frame-breaking raids on local mills, gave them endless material to bicker over, and all three men in the parlour held wildly different views on the crisis. Mr. Helstone thought the mill owners were the injured party, the workpeople ungrateful and unreasonable. He condemned the growing widespread disaffection to the government, the unwillingness of the poor to patiently bear hardships he saw as unavoidable. His solution? Stronger government interference, stricter vigilance from local magistrates, and if needed, swift, unflinching military coercion.

Mr. Yorke laughed in his face, and demanded to know if all that interference, vigilance and coercion would put bread in the mouths of the starving hungry, or give jobs to the men no mill would hire. He scoffed

The light, unruly good humour that had carried Robert Moore through the long, fraught night after the frame attack still clung to him when he roused at dawn. He and his overlooker Joe Scott had bunked down on makeshift beds cobbled from spare blankets and mill crates tucked in the nooks of the front and back counting houses, and Moore—who was always up before the first workman stirred—rose even earlier than usual that morning, humming a lively French folk tune under his breath as he laced his boots, loud enough to rouse Joe where he snored on his pallet.

"Ye haven't gone and lost all your brass then, maister?" Joe squawked, blearily pushing himself up.

"Not a stiver, mon garçon—my lad, that means. Up with you, now. We'll do a turn round the mill before the hands show their faces, and I'll lay out the whole of my new plan for you. We'll get the new machinery running yet, Joseph. You've heard of Bruce, haven't you? The Scottish king and the spider?"

"Th' arrand? Aye, I've read me history o' Scotland, know it as well as thee, I reckon. And I take your meaning: ye'll stick it out, no quitting."

"That's exactly what I mean."

"Is there many of your sort back in your country?" Joe asked, tucking his fold-up bed away in its cupboard.

"My country? And which do you think that is?"

"Why, France, isn't it?"

"Not a bit of it. The French seized Antwerp, sure, but that doesn't make me French.

"Holland then?"

"Don't muddle Antwerp and Amsterdam, man, I'm no Dutchman."

"Flanders?"

"Joe, you insult me! A Fleming? Me? Clumsy great nose sticking out, sloped-back thin forehead, pale blue eyes sitting right flat on my face? All body and no legs, like a Fleming is built? You don't know those Netherlanders, do you? I'm an Anversois, born and bred. My mother was Anversoise too, though her line was French, that's why I speak the language so easy."

"But your father were Yorkshire, so that makes you half one of us. Any fool can see it—ye're that keen to turn a profit, to get forrards, ye fit right in."

"You're an impudent rascal, Joe, but I grew up around Belgian factory workers who spoke their minds far rougher than you, so your bluntness doesn't ruffle me. The classe ouvrière, the working folk there, were brusque as anything to their employers."

"We allus speak our minds here in the north, that's the truth. Them soft young parsons and grand London folks get all shocked at our 'incivility'—and we love giving 'em something to gasp over, watching 'em roll their eyes and wave their hands like they've been stung by nettles, stammering 'Dear me! What dreadful vulgarity! How very coarse!'"

"You're a pack of savages, Joe. Don't tell me you think you're civilized?"

"Middling, maister, middling. Us manufacturing lads up north are a deal sharper than them slow farming folk down south. Trade sharpens wits, and a mechanic like me? You've got to think, ain't you? Working round the machinery, you see an effect, you hunt straight for the cause, and nine times out of ten you find it. I read a fair bit too, curious to know what the folks that run this country think they're doing with us. There's hundreds smarter than me—greasy mechanics reeking of machine oil, dyers with blue-stained hands that know more about what makes a fool of a law tick than soft ninnies like Christopher Sykes of Whinbury, or that strutting Irish curate Peter over in Helstone."

"Go on, I know you think you're a genius, Scott."

"Aye, I'm fair clever. Can tell cheese from chalk, and I've made the most of what chances I had, better than a whole lot of folks that reckon themselves above me. But Yorkshire's full of lads as good as me, and a few a damn sight better."

"Oh, you're a sublime, magnificent fellow—just a conceited prig with it all. Don't go thinking because you picked up a bit of practical math, or scraped a little chemistry knowledge out of the bottom of a dye vat, that you're some neglected unappreciated man of science. Don't go convincing yourself that because trade hits a rough patch sometimes, and you and your mates go short of work and bread, that all you working folk are martyrs, and the whole government's rotten to the core. And don't you dare try and tell me all the virtue lives in cottages and none at all in slated big houses. Human nature's the same no matter if you live under tiles or thatch—vice and virtue mixed up in every single soul, rich or poor, no exceptions. I've met villains with gold, villains with nothing, and villains sitting pretty on a modest little income exactly like Agar's old wish. The clock's about to strike six. Go ring that mill bell, Joe, sharp."

It was mid-February, so the dawn that crept up was thin, pale, and slow, just a faint silver ray piercing the dark brown night, turning the thick opaque shadows of the dale soft and half-translucent. No colour tinged the east, no warm flush touched the hills. The sun's fire felt all but quenched, as if last night's rain had drowned it. The wind that stirred was raw and biting, peeling back layers of night cloud to leave a pale silvery ring all round the horizon, no blue sky to be seen, just a thin stratum of pale grey vapour. The rain had stopped, but the earth was sodden through, every puddle and little rivulet brim full.

The mill windows glowed with lamplight, the bell clanged loud across the yard, and soon the little factory children came running, hurrying so fast we can hope they barely felt the bite of the sharp wind—especially since that winter they'd trundled to work through blizzards, torrential rain, and hard killing frost so bad it turned the puddles to ice.

Mr. Moore stood in the mill entrance to watch them file past, counting each head as they went. He called out a sharp, quiet reprimand to the stragglers, which Joe repeated a little more firmly once they reached the work floor. Neither master nor overlooker was cruel, even if they were strict: a boy who turned up far too late got fined a penny, which Moore made him hand over before he crossed the threshold, warning him the next offence would cost him twopence.

Rules are necessary for a mill, of course, and cruel masters make cruel rules—rules that were often enforced tyrannically in this era—but there are no child-beating slave-drivers here. I will not sully these pages with tales of whippings and torture. Neither Mr. Moore nor Joe Scott ever laid a hand to a child in their mill. Joe had once thrashed his own son hard for lying and sticking to it, that was true, but both men were too calm, too reasonable, to use corporal punishment for anything but the rarest of exceptions.

Moore wandered the mill, the yard, the dye house, and the warehouse until that sickly grey dawn thickened into full day. The sun even peeked over the dark ridge of the far hill: a white, disc of light, almost as chill and colourless as ice, gilding the top edge of the clouds above it, and staring solemnly down the whole length of the narrow little dale. It was eight o'clock. All the mill lamps were blown out. The whistle blew for breakfast, and the children poured out of the work rooms for their half hour off, clustering round their little tin cans of coffee and small wicker baskets of bread. Let us hope every last one of them had enough to eat. It would be a sad, shameful thing if they did not.

At last Moore left the mill yard, and turned his steps towards his little cottage home, a short walk away down a lane banked high with hedges that gave the small whitewashed place a quiet, secluded air. A green wooden porch arched over the front door; the garden beds at its feet poked up bare brown stalks, budless and flowerless now, but promising climbing roses and honeysuckle for the summer ahead. The lawn and borders were nothing but black mud right then, save for a few sheltered nooks where the very first emerald green snowdrop and crocus shoots were peeking up through the earth. Winter had been long and harsh, the last deep snow only melted three days prior in yesterday's rains, patches of white still lingering on the high hill hollows and peaks. The lawn grass was not green yet, bleached pale and pale from frost. Three graceful, small trees stood clustered beside the cottage, tall enough to feel imposing with no other trees nearby, framing the little white house perfectly. It was a snug, peaceful nest for quiet reflection, but far too small to hold all of Moore's ambition and restless energy for long.

He did not go inside straight away. He fetched a spade from the little tool shed by the back door, and started turning the cold soil in his garden, digging for a full fifteen minutes undisturbed, before an upstairs window creaked open, and a woman's voice called out to him.

"Eh, bien! Tu ne déjeûnes pas ce matin?"

"Breakfast ready, Hortense?" Moore called back, speaking in their shared native French.

"Of course it is. It has been sitting hot for half an hour."

"Then I'm ready too. I'm ravenous enough to eat a dog."

He threw down his spade and stepped inside. The narrow front hall led straight to a tiny parlour, where breakfast was laid out: coffee, bread and butter, and the very un-English addition of slow-stewed pears. Hortense Moore, his older sister, presided over the table. She was a tall, sturdy woman of thirty-five, with thick black hair twisted up in curl papers, high colour in her cheeks, a small neat nose, and bright little black eyes. The lower half of her face was broader than the upper, her forehead small and slightly lined, her expression a little fretful but not unkind, the kind of look you half want to tease and half feel sorry for. She was dressed for her morning housework in a simple woven petticoat and striped cotton camisole, the petticoat cut short enough to show a pair of stocky ankles and feet that were far from elegant.

Do not mistake her for a slattern. Hortense was the most orderly, thrifty housekeeper alive. This was the customary morning dress she had worn her whole life back in Antwerp, and she refused to adopt English conventions just because she was living in a remote Yorkshire dale, convinced sticking to her old Belgian ways was a mark of virtue. She thought very highly of herself, and not without reason—she was loyal, hardworking, and unshakably honest, even if she overestimated her own wisdom, and never noticed her little flaws of being narrow-minded, overly touchy about her own dignity, and quick to take offence over tiny trifles. Still, when no one threatened her pride or her family's honour, she was warm and generous. She adored both her brothers, as the last remaining members of their once wealthy old family, and she thought Robert, the older of the two, was the greatest man alive. Any opposition to him, or to her, was nothing short of monstrous injustice.

So the moment Robert sat down at the breakfast table, and she had served him his stewed pears and cut him a thick slice of buttered Belgian tartine, she launched into a full tirade of horror about the previous night's attack on the new frames.

"The idea! To destroy them! Disgraceful, shameful behaviour! The working people here are both stupid and cruel, exactly like the English servants, especially that girl Sarah. She is unbearable!"

"She looks clean and hard-working enough," Moore murmured.

"Looks! I don't care how she looks, I'm not saying she's dirty or lazy—but she is insolent! She argued with me for a full quarter of an hour yesterday about how to boil beef. Said I boiled it to rags, that no English person would eat our bouilli, that the broth was nothing but greasy warm water. And that barrel of sauerkraut I prepared with my own hands down in the cellar? She called it a tub of pig slop! I'm half driven mad by that girl, and I can't even fire her, because I'll only end up with someone worse. You're just the same with your workmen, poor dear brother."

"I'm afraid you're not very happy in England, Hortense."

"It is my duty to be happy where you are, of course, but otherwise there are a thousand things that make me long for Antwerp. Everyone here is ill-bred. The mill girls snicker when they walk in the kitchen and find me in my jupon and camisole making dinner—you know I can't trust Sarah to cook a single dish properly. The last time I was invited out to tea, they treated me like I was invisible, no respect at all for my family name, the Gérards and the Moores, we are perfectly entitled to deference! Back in Antwerp everyone treated me with the utmost distinction, here I think they must laugh at my accent, though I know I pronounce English perfectly."

"Hortense, in Antwerp everyone knew us as rich. Here, everyone only ever knew us as poor."

"See? That's how mercenary everyone is! And last Sunday, it was pouring rain, so I walked to church in my neat black sabots—perfectly respectable for muddy country roads in winter, we all wear them at Mons and Leuze outside Brussels. I walked up the aisle as calm as you please, and four ladies and four gentlemen hid their faces behind their prayer books and laughed at me!"

"Then don't wear the sabots again. I told you before, they don't suit this country."

"They are not the rough peasant clogs! They are clean, proper black sabots! Let anyone try and walk the muddy Flemish chaussées in delicate Paris boots, they'll change their tune fast!"

"Forget Mons and Leuze and the Flanders roads. When in Rome, do as the Romans do. And I'm not so sure about the camisole and petticoat either. No English lady dresses like that in the morning. Ask Caroline Helstone."

"Caroline? *I* ask Caroline for fashion advice? *I* consult her? She is the child who should be consulting me! She is nothing but a baby."

"She's seventeen, almost eighteen. Old enough to know all about gowns and shoes."

"Do not spoil her, Robert. Do not make her think she is more important than she is. She is modest and unassuming now, let us keep her that way."

"Fair enough. Is she coming this morning, like usual?"

"She will be here at ten for her French lesson."

"She doesn't sneer at you, does she?"

"Of course not. She respects me more than anyone else here, she sees that I am educated, intelligent, well-bred. I do not care for her deeply, of course, I do not take sudden silly fancies, my regard for her is steady. She is my cousin, after all, and her position is lonely, her uncle is far too strict with her. She is a good pupil, though of course she is not perfect. There is much that needs correcting in her."

"Pour me one last cup of coffee, then, and tell me what those faults of hers are."

"I am so glad you have a good appetite after that terrible night. Well, Caroline is sometimes quiet and reserved in a way that is not properly girlish and submissive, and she has little bursts of wild, unsettled hurry that quite unsettle me, though most of the time she is far too quiet, far too thoughtful, pensive for no reason I can name. I will train her, of course, give her a proper system for her thoughts, full control over her silly feelings. The other day I set her to memorize some short light French poetry to practice her pronunciation, and she was turning the pages so fast I could see her curl her lip in absolute scorn. She said 'All this bores me to death.' And then she suddenly stopped, memorized one little piece by Chénier, *La Jeune Captive*, and came and recited it to me so passionately, so full of feeling, you would have thought that scribbler Chénier was better than Racine and Corneille put together. That unruly excess of feeling? That is the 'unsettled hurry' I mean. She has no regulated mind at all, but I will fix that for her."

"Quite right, Hortense. Wait, isn't that her shadow passing the window? She's here."

"Ah, yes. She is half an hour early. My child, what brings you here before your lesson time?"

The door had swung open to reveal Caroline Helstone, wrapped in a heavy winter mantle gathered gracefully around her slight, slender frame, cheeks pink from the sharp cold wind, eyes bright and worried.

"I ran all the way here to make sure you two are alright. I only heard about the attack on the frames this morning at breakfast, my uncle told me. I knew you would both be so grieved."

"Unspeakable, the whole thing," Hortense sighed. "You sympathize with us? Your uncle sympathizes?"

"He was furious! He rode out with you last night, didn't he, to Stilbro' Moor? I thought he went with you to meet the wagons?"

"Aye, we set out ready for a fight, Caroline," Robert smiled, the faint ghost of amusement on his face. "But the lads we went to rescue met us halfway, no battle needed."

"No one got hurt, then? No one was injured?"

"Not a scratch. Only Joe Scott's wrists a little sore, they tied them behind his back too tight."

"You were not there, were you? Not when the wagons got attacked?"

"No. You hardly ever get to be on the spot for the kind of fight you actually want to help with."

"Where are you going this morning, then? I saw Murgatroyd saddling your horse out in the yard."

"To Whinbury. It's market day."

The golden mid-morning sun slanted through Hollow's Cottage parlour window, wrapping Caroline Helstone in its soft warmth as she sat at Hortense Moore's side, her mind drifting far from the French grammar lesson unfolding before her. Again and again she missed the point of Mademoiselle's explanations, earning gentle chidings that she bore with a smile unclouded by frustration—for that sunlight seemed to carry with it a quiet, thrumming joy that had settled in her chest since first light, making her feel soft, bright, and brimming with quiet goodwill. In that mood, she looked her very best: a vision of gentle, unforced charm that caught the eye even at first glance.

Beauty had never been denied to Caroline. Her slight, girlish frame was lithe and pliant, every curve neat, every limb softly proportioned; her face was gentle and alive with expression, her brown eyes bright with a winsome, warm glow that slipped unawares into a person's heart, speaking quiet affection before her mouth could frame a word. Her lips were full and sweet, her skin delicate as rose petal, and her thick, wavy brown hair fell in loose, artful curls that she arranged with quiet, unshowy taste. Her dress matched her hair's soft warm brown merino for the winter, a simple collar edged with a narrow pink ribbon fastened at her throat with a tiny pink knot—no other trinkets, no costly adornments, and yet every stitch suited her perfectly.

That was Caroline Helstone for the world to see. Her character, her quiet hungers and hidden hopes, would reveal themselves soon enough.

Her story was simple, and tinged with quiet loneliness. She was born to two parents who separated almost immediately after her birth, their tempers too ill-suited to ever make a home together. Her mother was the half-sister of Hortense, Robert and Louis Moore's father, making her their distant cousin, no shared blood between them but a soft, gentle affection that had grown between her and the Moore siblings since they had arrived at Hollow's Mill. Her father, the dissolute brother of her uncle the rector, had made his wife's life a misery, and died young when Caroline was still a small child. She had never known her mother, taken away from her in infancy and never permitted to see her again, and for years her only guardian had been the gruff, sharp rector, a man ill-suited to raising a quiet young girl. He had paid no mind to her education, and if Caroline had not pushed for it herself, hungry for every scrap of learning she could get, she would have grown up almost entirely untaught. Even so, she carried a quiet, constant fear that she was less educated than other girls of her station, which made her jump at Hortense's kind offer to teach her French and fine needlework the moment the older woman had settled into the cottage.

Hortense, for her part, adored the task: it gave her a sense of importance, and she delighted in lording gently over her docile, sharp little pupil. She dismissed Caroline's quick progress as proof only of her own superior teaching methods, never once crediting the girl's sharp mind and eagerness to learn, even when Caroline knew things about subjects Hortense had never studied—she was entirely convinced Caroline had gleaned every last scrap of knowledge from her own conversation, a silly, unshakable little conviction that no amount of evidence could shift.
