Proud of her "positive intellect" and fond of dry, rigid studies, she kept Caroline nose-deep in French grammar as much as possible, assigning endless interminable "logical analyses" that Caroline found tedious beyond measure: hours spent picking apart principal and subordinate clauses, distinguishing determinative and applicative incidents, sorting full, elliptical and implicit propositions that tangled her thoughts into knots.
When she got stuck, she would sneak her book down to the counting-house while Hortense rummaged endlessly upstairs, rearranging her drawers for the tenth time that day, and Robert Moore would untangle the puzzle for her in two minutes flat, his clear, calm brain cutting through the jargon like a knife through butter. Caroline would leave the counting house glowing with gratitude, smiling after him like he had handed her a precious treasure, and daydream constantly that nature had made her a boy instead of a girl, so she could be his clerk and sit by his side in the counting house all day, instead of stuck in the parlour doing endless grammar drills with Hortense.

Very rarely, she was invited to spend the whole evening at the cottage. Half the time Robert was away at market, or calling on Mr Yorke, or shut away with a business visitor, but sometimes he was home, free and unoccupied, and those evenings passed so fast they were gone before she could count the hours. There was no room in all of England as perfect as that tiny parlour when the three cousins sat together: Hortense would soften as the day wound down, no longer scolding or fussing over cooking, and if Caroline cajoled her into picking up her guitar to play, she would grow perfectly good-natured, her warm skilled voice filling the room. Robert, released from the weight of his business worries, would sit near her, a soft, willing audience for all her chattering, gentle and friendly, and those hours were the brightest points in her quiet, uneventful life. The only flaw was that by the next morning, he would always retreat back into his quiet, distant work-focused self, and never planned those pleasant evenings on purpose, letting weeks pass before inviting her over again. Caroline, inexperienced and soft-hearted, puzzled over that habit constantly: if she had a source of that much happiness, she would use it often, never let it sit gathering dust on a shelf.
She never invited herself over, no matter how much she longed to come—even when Hortense pressed her to visit, she would refuse if Robert did not second the invitation. Which was why that morning, when he had invited her of his own free will, speaking so kindly that her chest had felt fit to burst with joy, she had carried that warmth with her every minute of the day.

The morning unfolded as usual. Hortense bustled back and forth between kitchen and parlour, scolding their maid Sarah one minute, checking Caroline's exercises the next. No amount of perfect work ever earned a word of praise from her; she was convinced praise undermined a teacher's dignity, and that constant, mild scolding was the only way to maintain her authority. If Caroline got her lesson perfectly right, Hortense would find some fault with her posture, her dress, or the way she held her head to correct instead.
Dinner brought its usual small skirmish: Sarah slammed the dish down on the table with a look that said plainly she thought the meal was not fit for a dog, but it was surprisingly savoury all the same: dried pea puree that Hortense had fussed over for an hour, lamenting the lack of proper haricot beans in "this desolate English country", a spiced baked meat and breadcrumb mould, oddly bruised greens, and a fruit pâté made from her grandmother's old Belgian recipe, tasting faintly of molasses instead of sugar. Caroline liked the strange foreign food well enough, and she was wise to: any hint of distaste for Hortense's cooking would have been a far worse sin in the older woman's eyes than any outright crime.

After dinner, Caroline coaxed Hortense upstairs to dress, navigating the task carefully: if she had ever dared say the sight of her cousin in curl papers, camisole and petticoat was unbecoming, Hortense would have worn that outfit for the rest of the day just to be stubborn. Instead, she pretended she was bored of the parlour and wanted to stretch her legs, and once they were in the bedroom, she persuaded Hortense it was far more efficient to dress for the evening right away, and fussed over her, arranging her collar and her hair while Hortense delivered a long, solemn speech about how frivolous fashion was. Even then, Hortense insisted on tying a thick ficher handkerchief tight around her throat and putting on a huge, servant-like black apron, ruining the neat effect entirely. To her, the ficher was a point of decency, and the apron the mark of a good housewife, a garment that somehow saved Robert untold amounts of money. She had tried to force Caroline to wear matching ones, and their only serious quarrel had been over that very demand—until Robert had overheard the dispute, and ruled that Caroline's small neat aprons and high collars were perfectly sufficient, especially with her long curls falling almost to her shoulders. There was no arguing with Robert, so Hortense had yielded, though she still thought Caroline's pretty, neat dresses were far from "beaucoup plus convenable" as a solid, homely outfit would have been.

All afternoon, the two women sewed, Hortense working on fine embroidery and lacework that strained her eyes, devoting entire days to mending two tiny holes in a stocking, a skill she had been forced to learn when she was barely old enough to hold a needle, at the age of five. She had been horrified to discover Caroline had no idea how to do the perfect, invisible stitch darning that duplicated the stocking fabric exactly, and had dug out a pair of stockings with the heels completely worn away for Caroline to mend—two years later, Caroline was still chipping away at a few rows a day, the task hanging over her like a tedious penance. Robert had once offered to sneak the stockings down to the counting house and burn them, but Caroline had known that would only result in a new, even more ruined pair of hose for her to mend, so she had stuck it out.

By late afternoon, rain was pouring down in torrents, the sky darkening completely long before twilight. Caroline's heart sank: what if Robert stayed over with Mr Sykes or Mr Yorke in Whinbury, waiting out the rain? Five o'clock struck, then six, the wind sighing through the trees outside, the parlour fire glowing ruddy as if it was already deep night.
"It will not clear until the moon rises," Hortense declared. "My brother will not come back until then. We will have coffee. There is no point waiting for him."
Caroline agreed meekly, and trundled off to the kitchen to tell Sarah to bring the tea things in, leaning her head against the mantel, her spirits drooping.
Sarah noticed her at once. "You look fit to cry, miss! All that work Cousin Moore puts you through, it's a downright shame."
"Nothing of the sort," Caroline said, then leaned forward, voice soft. "Sarah, does your master ever come home early from market when it's raining?"
"Hardly ever—but he's home now, as it happens. I saw Murgatroyd lead his horse round to the back yard five minutes ago, and I heard him talking to Joe Scott in the counting house about new frames for the mill, and four soldiers from Stilbro barracks to guard the next supply wagon."
Caroline's face lit up. She offered to help Sarah finish cutting out her new gown, and gave her a length of matching narrow satin ribbon for trimming, barely noticing when she snipped a little mistake in the fabric.
A moment later, the kitchen door opened, and Robert walked in, cold and damp from the rain. Caroline turned away for a second, trying to smooth the giddy smile off her face, but when she looked up at him her whole expression was glowing, bright with joy.
"We all thought you would not come back," she said.
"But I promised I would return soon. You expected me, didn't you?"
"I dared not, with it pouring so hard. You are wet and chilled—go change all your wet clothes, or you will catch your death!"
"My riding coat is waterproof. I only need dry shoes," he said, stepping close to the hearth to warm himself. He stood staring at the polished brasses on the mantelshelf, until he glanced down and caught sight of her face, upturned, flushed, smiling, her curls soft around her cheeks. Sarah had already slipped off to the parlour, detained by a scolding from Hortense over her "cafe au lait" that Sarah insisted was a waste of perfectly good coffee. Robert rested his hand for a light moment on Caroline's shoulder, leaned down, and pressed a soft, warm kiss to her forehead.
"Oh!" she breathed. "I was so miserable when I thought you would not come. I am almost too happy now. Are you happy, Robert? Are you glad to be home?"
"For tonight, at least, yes."
"Are you not fretting about your frames, your business, the war?"
"Not just now."
"Are you sure you don't think this cottage is too small, too narrow and dull for you?"
"Not this moment, no."
"Are you not bitter that the rich and great people around here forget you?"
He laughed, a little rough. "No more questions. I don't care about currying favour with them. I just want a livelihood, a position, a career."
"Your talent and your goodness will win it for you. You are meant to be great, you will be great."
"Lina, you judge me with your heart, not your head," he said, his dark face flushing softly, his lips twitching into a smile even as he tried to look serious. "Men are not as good as you think. I am no better than the rest of them."
"But you are better," she protested. "I feel it, here in my chest. I am your sincere friend, Robert."
He opened his mouth to reply, but before he could speak, Hortense and Sarah came bustling back into the kitchen, still arguing loudly over the merits of coffee made with milk instead of water. Robert only had time to meet Caroline's eyes, and answer her unspoken question if he could ever be her enemy, with a soft, quiet, "Could I be?" before he led her into the parlour, and settled her at his side at the table, the soft glow of the fire wrapping around both of them, the heavy rain beating soft against the windows outside.

Caroline Helstone was just eighteen, and at that age, the true story of your life has barely even turned its first page. Before you hit that milestone, you spend all your time listening to a tale half stitched from fantasy: sometimes delightfully soft, sometimes sad, but almost never real. Your world at seventeen is all heroic grandeur: the people in it are half angels, half demons, the woods darker and more enchanted than any real copse, the skies brighter, the streams more glittering and dangerous, the flowers sweeter than any you’ll find in a garden tended by mortal hands. The moon you gaze up at then hangs heavy with unnameable beauty, the silver light trembling in your bones like a secret the whole universe is sharing only with you. Your sun is a burning hearth stoked by the gods themselves.

At eighteen, you draw right up to the edge of that misty, dream-kissed fairyland you’ve lived in your whole life. Elfland recedes behind you, and the blue, soft-looking shores of Real Life rise up on the horizon, still far enough away that all you can see is the promise of spring meadows stretching beneath the haze, silver ribbons of running water glinting through the fog. You’re sure once you step off this dream-shore, you’ll never hunger or ache for anything again—never guessing that you’ll have to cross miles of barren wilderness, wade through rivers as cold and black as death itself, before you ever taste a single drop of true joy. Every happiness life has to offer has to be fought for, won with scraped palms and stinging breath, and only those who’ve wrestled for the greatest prizes know how hard that struggle cuts.

At eighteen, you don’t know any of that. When Hope leans in and promises you perfect happiness tomorrow, you believe her wholly. When Love wanders to your door looking like a lost, bruised angel, you fling the door open and pull him inside, never glancing at the quiver slung over his shoulder. When his arrows sink into your skin, you think the thrum in your veins is new life, not a wound. You never fear the poison in the tips, never imagine the barb will sit under your flesh forever, no healer able to pull it free. That wild, perilous passion that is agony at least half the time, for many agony from start to finish? You think it’s nothing but unqualified, glowing good. At eighteen, you’re just about to step through the doors of the school of Experience—whose lessons are humbling, bruising, grinding, but that leave you purified, strong, finally rooted in the real world.

Alas, Experience! No other mentor has a face so lined and frozen with weariness, no other wears a robe so deep and unrelieved black, no other carries a rod so heavy, no other grabs you by the wrist and drags you to your task so sternly, forcing you to learn no matter how you squirm. It’s only her guidance that can lead you safely through life’s wild thickets—without her, you stumble, you stray, you step into forbidden ground and go tumbling down cliffs you never saw coming.

That evening, after Robert Moore had walked her home, Caroline had no wish to linger in the same room as her uncle. His study was sacred, almost unapproachable space to her, and she stayed far away until the bell rang to call the household to prayers. Their small worship followed the standard church service, Mr. Helstone reading the words in his usual loud, nasal, perfectly monotonous tone. The moment the rite was done, Caroline stepped up to his side as she always did.
“Good-night, uncle.”
“Hey! You’ve been gadding all day—visiting, dining out, what not?”
“Only over at the cottage.”
“And did you learn your lessons?”
“Yes.”
“And finish that shirt you were mending?”
“Only half of one.”
“Well, that’ll do. Stick to your needle, learn to make shirts, gowns, and proper pie crust, and you’ll turn into a clever woman some day. Off to bed now. I’m busy with this pamphlet.”

A few minutes later, Caroline was locked in her small bedroom, the door bolted, her soft white dressing gown slipping over her shoulders, her long hair falling thick and wavy down to pool at her waist. She paused in the middle of brushing it, leaned her cheek on her hand, and stared down at the carpet, and all the gilded, tender visions that only come to girls of eighteen swirled up around her.
Her thoughts were speaking to her, warm and gentle, and she smiled listening to them. She looked lovely, lost in those daydreams—but brighter than any pretty face was the glowing spirit of Young Hope standing right at her shoulder. This sweet prophet told her she’d never know cold disappointment again: she’d stepped into the dawn of a perfect summer day, no false light, but the real, soft sunrise, and her sun would climb fast and bright. She had no idea she was being fooled. Her hopes felt solid, rooted in something unshakable.
“When two people love,” she reasoned to herself, “the next step is that they marry. I love Robert, and I know deep down Robert loves me. I’ve thought it a hundred times before, but today I *felt* it. When I looked up at him after reciting that Chénier poem, his eyes—what handsome eyes he has—sent the truth straight through my heart. Sometimes I’m too scared to talk to him, worried I’ll be too frank, too forward: I’ve said too much before, blurted out feelings I shouldn’t, and feared he’d think me silly and indiscreet. But tonight, I could have told him every single thought in my head, he was so kind, so gentle. How he walked with me down that lane! He doesn’t flatter, doesn’t say the foolish silly lines the heroes in books say. His affection—his friendship, I should say; of course I don’t call him my lover yet, but I hope he will be one day—is far better than anything in novels. It’s quiet, manly, sincere. I do love him. I’d be such a good wife to him. I’d tell him gently of his small faults, tend to every little comfort, cherish him, do everything I could to make him happy. I’m sure he won’t be cold tomorrow. I’m almost certain he’ll come here tomorrow evening, or ask me to come over to the cottage.”

She went back to brushing her hair, as long and flowing as a mermaid’s. When she turned her head to arrange it, she caught sight of herself in the mirror. Plain girls find their reflection sobering enough: they look at themselves and know no one will see any magic there. But a pretty girl draws different conclusions: the face gazing back is charming, so it must charm everyone else. Caroline saw her own soft cheeks, loose hair, the pink glow of hope in her eyes, and all her sweet daydreams felt even more real, more certain. She climbed into bed that night with a heart light as dandelion fluff.

She woke up the next morning still glowing, and walked into the breakfast room to wish her uncle a soft, cheerful good morning. Even that hard, gruff little man of bronze, Mr. Helstone, paused for a second and thought his niece was growing into quite the fine young woman. Usually she was quiet, timid, docile but never talkative around him, but that morning she chattered away about small, safe things she knew he wouldn’t mind: the new spring crocuses pushing up in the garden, when the gardener would come to trim the borders, the starlings starting to build nests in the church tower right next to the rectory, how she wondered the constant tolling of the bells didn’t scare them off.
Mr. Helstone huffed that they were just like every other silly newly paired fool, too wrapped up in each other to notice inconvenience. Caroline, giddy and brave from her good mood, dared to say something she never would have before.
“Uncle, you always talk about marriage like it’s something to scorn. Do you really think no one should marry?”
“Staying single is far and away the wisest plan, especially for women.”
“Are all marriages unhappy?”
“Millions are. If everyone told the full truth, I expect almost all of them are unhappy to some degree.”
“You always grumble when you have to officiate a wedding. Why?”
“Because I don’t care to be an accomplice to pure, unadulterated folly.”
He spoke easily, almost glad for the chance to air his harsh views on the subject. Emboldened that he hadn’t snapped at her yet, Caroline pushed further.
“But why is it pure folly? If two people like each other, why shouldn’t they choose to live together?”
“They tire of each other in a month. A spouse isn’t a companion—they’re just a fellow sufferer yoked to you for life.”
Caroline’s next remark wasn’t sweet naivete: it was sharp dislike for his cold views, quiet anger that he could dismiss all love like that.
“Anyone would think you’d never been married yourself, uncle. Anyone would take you for an old bachelor.”
“Practically, I am one.”
“But you were married. Why were you so inconsistent as to tie the knot?”
“Every man’s a fool once or twice in his life.”
“So you grew tired of my aunt, and she grew tired of you, and you were miserable together?”
Mr. Helstone pushed his lip out in that familiar cynical scowl, wrinkled his brow, and grunted something unreadable.
“Didn’t she suit you? Wasn’t she good tempered? Didn’t you grow used to her? Weren’t you sorry when she died?”
“Caroline,” he said, slowly lifting his hand an inch above the table before slamming it hard on the mahogany, “understand this. It’s vulgar and childish to mix up general rules with specific exceptions. Your questions are stupid and babyish. Ring the bell if you’ve finished your breakfast.”

The meal was cleared away, and customarily uncle and niece would part ways, not seeing each other again until dinner. But today, Caroline didn’t leave. She walked over to the window seat and sat down. Mr. Helstone glanced over at her uneasily a few times, clearly wishing she’d go, but she was staring out the window, seemingly paying him no mind, so he went back to his paper—full of exciting new dispatches from Lord Wellington’s campaigns in the Peninsula, that day. He had no idea what was swirling in her head, thoughts his harsh words had stirred up, but didn’t create: old, tangled memories that buzzed in her brain like disturbed bees in a hive, thoughts she’d carried with her for years.
She was replaying his views on marriage, and on the other side of that wide, uncrossable chasm between his cold outlook and her hopeful one, she saw a dim, sinister, half-forgotten figure standing next to him: her own father, James Helstone, Matthewson Helstone’s brother.
She’d heard rumors of him from old servants, dropped hints here and there: he wasn’t a good man, he’d never been kind to her. She remembered dark, sharp fragments of a few weeks spent with him in a big, smoky town somewhere: no maid to dress her or look after her, locked day and night in a bare garret with no carpet, a bed with no curtains, no other furniture at all. He left early every morning, often forgot to come back and feed her all day, and when he stumbled home drunk at night he was raving and furious, or worse—senseless, blank, impossible to reach. She knew she’d fallen ill there, and one night, burning up with fever, he’d burst into the room screaming he’d kill her, because she was nothing but a burden. Her screams had brought the neighbors running to break down the door. She’d never seen him again, except lying dead in his coffin.
That was her father. She had a mother, too, though Mr. Helstone never spoke of her, though she couldn’t remember ever seeing her face. She knew her mother was alive, though. That woman had been the drunkard’s wife. What could their marriage have been like? Caroline turned away from the window, where she’d been staring at the starlings without actually seeing them, and spoke in a soft, sad, bitter little voice that cut through the silence.
“You think all marriage is miserable, I suppose, because you saw what my parents went through. If my mother suffered half of what I suffered locked in that garret with Papa, she must have had a dreadful life.”
Mr. Helstone wheeled around in his chair, staring at her over the top of his spectacles, completely taken aback. Her father and mother? He’d never spoken a single word about them in the twelve years she’d lived under his roof. He never imagined she had any memories, any questions of her own about them.
“Your father and mother? Who’s been talking to you about them?”
“Nobody. But I remember bits of what Papa was like. And I pity Mama. Where is she?”
That question had sat on the tip of her tongue hundreds of times before, but she’d never dared say it out loud.
“I barely know her,” Mr. Helstone said, flustered. “I haven’t heard from her in years. Wherever she is, she doesn’t think of you. She never asks after you. I have good reason to believe she never wants to see you. Now come on, it’s school time. You go over to your cousin’s at ten, don’t you? The clock just struck.”
Caroline might have said more, but Fanny walked in to tell her master the churchwardens were waiting for him in the vestry. He hurried off, and Caroline set out for the cottage.
The road from the rectory down to Hollow’s Mill sloped gently downhill, so she ran most of the way. Fresh air, the exercise, the thought that she’d soon see Robert, or at least be near him, lifted her fallen spirits fast. When the white house came into view, and she could hear the mill’s great thundering roar and the rush of the mill race, the first thing she saw was Moore standing at his garden gate. He was wearing his loose, belted Holland blouse, a soft light cap over his hair, the casual suit fitting him perfectly. He was looking down the lane, not the direction she was coming from. She stopped, stepped behind a willow tree, and just studied him for a minute.
“He has no equal,” she thought. “He’s as clever as he is handsome. What sharp, bright eyes he has! What clear, spirited features—thin and serious, but so graceful. I do love his face, I do love the way he carries himself. I love him more than any of those shuffling little curates, more than anyone. My kind, bonny Robert.”
She stepped out to greet him, and when he saw her, she was half convinced he’d vanish like a phantom if he could. But he was solid, real, so he had to stand and greet her. His greeting was short, cool. Cousinly. Brotherly. Friendly. Anything but lover-like. All the nameless soft magic of the night before was gone from his manner. He was a different man, as if the warm heart she’d seen beating under his blouse last night was nowhere to be found. It was a rude, sharp shock of disappointment. At first the eager girl refused to believe the change, even though she could see it, feel it cold in her bones. It was hard to pull her hand away from his without getting at least a kind, gentle squeeze back. It was hard to drop her eyes from his without catching one soft, fond look, something warmer than that cool welcome.
A man disappointed in love can speak up, demand an explanation. A woman? She can say nothing. To speak would bring nothing but burning shame, bitter remorse for betraying her own heart out loud. She would feel such self-contempt afterward it would break her. The wisest thing to do is take what you’re given without questions, without complaint. You expected bread, and he handed you a stone? Grin and bear the scrape of it against your teeth, don’t scream. You held out your palm for an egg, and fate set a scorpion in it? Don’t flinch. Close your fingers tight around the gift, let it sting. In time, the scorpion will die, and you will have learned the greatest lesson of all: how to endure sorrow without a single sob.
Robert had done her no wrong, told her no lie. If anyone was to blame for the pain pricking at her chest, it was her own silly, hopeful heart. She’d loved him before he’d ever asked her to. It was a natural, inevitable thing sometimes, but it always brings misery. Robert had seemed fond of her, yes—but he’d fought that feeling, hard. He didn’t want to get tangled in an attachment that would force him into a marriage he knew was imprudent, bad for both of them. So he was pulling back, deliberately.
Caroline had more sense than to chase him. She left his side quietly, no complaints, no questions, not a single tear, no change in her calm, soft expression. She went to Hortense’s lessons as usual, and walked home for dinner without lingering.
Once she’d finished eating, and her uncle was lingering over his small, temperate glass of port wine, she was alone in the rectory drawing room, and one thought tangled in her head: how on earth was she going to get through the rest of this day?
Last night she’d hoped it would be just like the day before, full of soft hours and Robert’s company. She knew now that hope had been foolish—but she couldn’t quite shake the tiny, whispering thought that he might still stop by. He’d wandered up to the rectory after tea more than once before, even when he’d been distant with her, just to sit with her uncle and chat. He’d never talk much to her in her uncle’s presence, but he’d glance over at her, soft and relenting, while she sewed at her little work table, the few words he said to her gentle and kind. False Hope whispered that he’d come tonight. She almost knew it was a lie, but she still listened.
She tried to read, but her thoughts wandered far from the page. She tried to sew, but every stitch felt like a tedious chore, unbearable. She pulled out her desk to write a French composition, but all she could scrawl down was mistakes.
Suddenly the doorbell rang sharp and loud. Her heart leaped so hard it ached. She sprang to the drawing room door, opened it softly, peeked through the crack. Fanny was letting a visitor in, a gentleman, a tall man, exactly Robert’s height. For one giddy second she thought it was him—exulted—until the visitor opened his mouth and asked for Mr

The sun had not yet pricked the frosted February horizon when Robert Moore swung himself into his saddle, galloping out towards Whinbury before his sister Hortense had even set the kettle to simmer for their café au lait, or laid out the crisp buttered tartines that were his usual breakfast. The errand that took him out so early he kept locked tight behind the quiet reserve that ran in his mercantile bones: the heavy, tangled miseries of his mill business were a secret he rarely breathed to anyone save to startle poor Joe Scott half out of his wits, or send a sharp, brisk warning to some far-flung foreign correspondent. Hortense never asked questions; it was not her way to pry, nor his to render accounts for his comings and goings.

By the time he trundled back down the mill drive, the pale golden morning was stretching over the Yorkshire moors, and the faint, warm memory of the previous evening lingered at the back of his mind – of Caroline’s soft cheek resting against the cushion beside him as he read Shakespeare aloud, of her quiet little gasp when he recited lines about untamed, enduring love – even as he hung his riding coat by the counting-house door and settled at his desk.

Joe Scott’s small son Henry trundled in with the post and the daily papers, and Moore broke the seals one by one, scanning their contents quickly. None of the news was good: every letter reeked of sour, uncompromising bad tidings, so that when he set the last one down, he huffed a sharp, defiant breath through his nose, a flicker of dark exasperation crossing his usually placid face. He did not pace or mutter oaths, but his fingers stripped the feather quill off the top of his pen in one quick, sharp spasm of barely controlled fury, before he dashed off a stack of terse replies, sealed them, and strode out to walk the length of his mill floor, his boots clapping on the wooden planks, to burn off the edge of his frustration.

When he returned, he picked up the paper, but he could not focus. More than once he laid it across his knee, folded his arms, and stared into the crackling fire in the hearth, glancing out the open door at the mild, sweet air that blew in – no spring bloom yet, of course, only the occasional faint whiff of sulphur from the mill’s tall black chimney. He checked his pocket watch for the third time, and had just begun to wonder if Sugden the constable had got lost on the road, when a figure dark as a midnight sky popped around the doorframe: it was Joe Scott, fresh from the dyeing vats, his face and arms streaked deep indigo. “He’s comed, sir,” he said, and vanished before Moore could even nod.

A moment later, in strode Sugden – broad-shouldered, thick-limbed, dressed in rough fustian coat and grey worsted stockings, his official constable’s staff clutched in one big hand. He pulled off his scuffed old hat, stuffed it under his chair, wiped his forehead on a spotted cotton handkerchief, and announced that it was “raight dahn warm for Febewerry.” Moore made a small, noncommittal sound that passed for agreement, and gestured for him to sit.

“I reckon you’ve got all the papers in order?” Moore asked.

“Ay, ay! All’s right and tight,” Sugden replied, whistling softly under his breath to look at his ease. Moore reached to the cupboard at his elbow, pulled out the same black bottle of hollands he’d shared with the curate Malone a few days prior, set a tumbler and a jug on the table, and nodded for Sugden to help himself. “Water’s in that jar in the corner.”

“Dunnut knaw there’s mich need, but a body’s niver too dry of a morning,” Sugden said, grinning as he mixed himself a generous drink, took a long, satisfied draught, and leaned back in his chair. “Yah’d as good have a sup yourself, Mr. Moore. Uncommon good stuff, that. Got it from furrin parts, I’se warrant? Them lads that’s coming are going to keep you talking for hours, you’ll need propping up.”

Moore shook his head. “Seen Mr. Sykes this morning?”

“I seed him a quarter hour back, just before I set off,” Sugden said. “He said he’d be straight over here, and I wouldn’t wonder if old Helstone comes too. I saw ‘em saddling his little nag out the back of the rectory just now.”

He was right. Five minutes later, the sharp clip-clop of a small pony’s hooves echoed across the mill yard, and a nasal, familiar voice called out, “Boy! Take my horse and lead him to the stable!” The Reverend Helstone marched in, brisk and upright, his cheeks tanned from riding, his eyes sharper and brighter than ever.

“Beautiful morning, Moore! How do, my boy? Ha! Sugden! What, you’re ready to get straight to business? I like that,” he said, nodding at the constable, then leaned in, curious. “Your message got to me safe. Are you sure you’re on the right track here? Have you got the warrant?”

“Sugden has it,” Moore said.

“Then I’m coming with you to make the arrest,” Helstone declared.

“No trouble needed, sir,” Moore smiled, dry and quiet. “They’re coming to me. I’m sat here waiting for them now.”

“Who is it, then? One of my parishioners?”

Joe Scott had slipped in unobserved, leaning against the desk, half his person dyed the deepest indigo, a sly little grin on his face. “It’s an old friend of yours, Mr. Helstone,” he said. “The gentleman you’re always joking about, the tub orator, the Reverend Moses Barraclough.”

Helstone pulled out his snuff box and took a very long pinch, blinking. “Ah! I never would have guessed it! That pious hypocrite was never your workman, Moore! He’s a tailor by trade!”

“Which is exactly why I hold it against him,” Moore said, his voice sharpening. “He’s the one who’s been stirring up my sacked workers against me, inciting all that rioting.”

“And he was there at Stilbro’ Moor, leading the lot? On his wooden leg?” Helstone asked.

“Ay, sir,” Joe cut in, happy to tell the tale. “He rode there on horseback so no one would notice his bad leg, wore a mask the whole time, while the rest just blacked their faces. And we found him out because he courted Sarah, our master’s servant lass, and she wouldn’t have a single thing to do with him. She was sweet on Fred Murgatroyd, one of our mill lads, instead. A couple of months back, Fred and Moses ran into each other lingering round the cottage after dark, both trying to persuade Sarah to go for a walk with them. They tussled, and Fred got the worst of it – for all he’s got one leg, Moses is near as strong as Sugden there. Then last night, Fred and his mate took shelter in a barn from a rain shower, and heard Moses plotting with his mates – not just leading the riot at Stilbro’, but the attack on Sykes’s mill too. They’re coming here this morning, a whole deputation, to ‘persuade’ our master to get rid of his new machinery, all under the guise of pious peace and goodwill.”

Before he could say another word, the sound of a gig rolling into the yard made them all turn. Mr. Sykes stumbled in, tall and stout, about fifty years old, his pleasant face tight with anxiety. “Have they been? Are they gone? Did you arrest him? Is it all over?” he panted.

“Not yet,” Moore said, calm as ever. “We’re waiting for them.”

“They won’t come,” Sykes fretted. “It’s near noon. We should give this up, it’ll stir up bad blood, cause who knows what harm. I’ve got a wife and three little girls at home, Moore, a wife and family – a man can’t be too cautious.”

“You don’t have to show your face at all,” Moore told him. “I’ll meet them in the yard. You can stay here by the fire.”

“But my name will be in all the legal papers!” Sykes wailed. Helstone pushed the black bottle across the table to him, and Sykes mixed himself a brimming tumbler of hollands and water, swallowed it down, and within two minutes, colour flooded back to his cheeks, and he was suddenly bold as brass. He announced he wasn’t going to be trampled on by a bunch of common rioters, he’d spend his last penny to see them punished, no one was going to push him around. He poured himself a second glass, coughing a little to excuse it, saying he had a slight chill that morning, and turned to Helstone, puffing out his chest. “You’re a man of peace, sir, your nerves couldn’t stand this sort of peril! But us manufacturers, we get belligerent! When my wife lays awake every night scared the house will get broken into, I get all fired up – I’d enjoy a bit of a scuffle, I would, that’s how spirited I am!”

Helstone let out a low, brief, amused laugh, and stopped Moore before he could press a third glass on the man, shaking his head. “Enough is as good as a feast, Mr. Sykes, isn’t it?” he said, and Sykes nodded, grinning, as Joe Scott slipped the bottle away out of sight.

For half a second, Moore’s dark face softened, unguarded, as he thought of Caroline. What would she have said if she could see him now, this sharp, unyielding side of him, the man who wasn’t the gentle companion who’d sat with her last night reading poetry? Would she still see the Coriolanus she’d looked up at with such soft love? Would she recognise the same man who’d bent over her hand last night, his touch light as a feather? The thought crossed his mind, and he smiled faintly – he knew her gentle heart well enough to guess she would not flinch. She would see the integrity under his stubbornness, the fact he never lied, never cheated, never stooped to the kind of meanness that would make true love wither. He was no slave to petty vices, no weak fool like Sykes – he was a man she could respect, no matter what rough edges he had.

A tramping of feet and murmur of voices rumbled from the yard outside. Moore walked to the window, Helstone at his shoulder, both of them peeking out from behind the frame, and shared a quick, cynical little smile at the sight that met their eyes.

A loud, performative cough echoed, followed by a sharp “Whisht!” to hush the crowd. Moore pushed the casement open an inch, to hear every word.

“Joseph Scott?” came a snuffling, pious voice from the yard. “Might we inquire if your master be within, and might we speak to him?”

“He’s within, ay,” Joe called back, nonchalant.

“Would you be so good as to tell him that twelve gentlemen wish to see him, for a purpose.”

Joe came back inside, grinning. “Twelve gentlemen wanting to see ye ‘for a purpose,’ sir.”

“Good,” Moore said, and he whistled once, soft and quiet, to signal Sugden to stand by. He strolled out into the yard, one hand in his coat pocket, the other tucked in his waistcoat, his cap brim pulled low over his eyes, a faint glint of scorn dancing in his gaze. Twelve men stood waiting, some in their shirt sleeves, some in blue work aprons, two leading the pack: one small, dapper, strutting man with a turned-up nose, and the other broad-shouldered, with a demure, untrusting cat-like look in his eyes, a wooden leg propped on the cobblestones, a thick crutch under his arm – Moses Barraclough himself, a sly leer on his lips.

“Good morning, Mr. Barraclough,” Moore said, polite and debonair.

“Peace be unto you!” Moses intoned, shutting his half-lidded eyes for full pious effect.

“I’m obliged to you, peace is a very fine thing, I wish nothing more for myself,” Moore said. “But I don’t imagine that’s all you’ve come to say to me, is it? Peace isn’t exactly your purpose today, is it?”

“Our purpose may sound strange to the ears of the children of this world, who are wiser in their generation than the children of light,” Moses whined.

“Get to the point, then,” Moore said.

Moses opened his mouth to speak, but the other leading man stepped forward, impatient to show off his fine, grand speech. “Mr. Moore,” he began, drawing out every word slow and deliberate, as if his phrases were the height of elegant rhetoric, “it may be said that reason, not peace, is our purpose. We come to request you hear reason, and if you refuse, sir, I must warn you in very decided terms that measures will be had recourse to, that will bring you to a sense of the foolishness that guides your proceedings. You are a foreigner, sir, come from a distant hemisphere, cast out upon the cliffs of Albion, and you do not understand our ways, the ways of the working classes. If you would but do the sensible thing, give up this mill, and go straight home to where you belong, no harm will come to you. What do you say, lads?”

The rest of the deputation cheered, “Hear, hear!”

Joe Scott snickered behind Moore’s shoulder. “Brayvo, Noah o’ Tim’s! Moses’ll niver top that speech! Cliffs of Albion! Did you come from the Antarctic, maister?”

Moses glowered at Noah, and pushed past him to speak again, his voice suddenly grave and earnest. “Before you came and set up your tent among us, Mr. Moore, we all lived in peace, in loving kindness. I’m a tailor, not a cloth dresser, but I am a feeling man, and I cannot bear to see my brethren oppressed. I urge you, sir, to cast aside your new infernal machinery, and hire more hands.”

“And what if I don’t take your advice, Mr. Barraclough?” Moore asked.

“The Lord pardon you! The Lord soften your heart!” Moses cried, lifting his hands to the sky.

“I saw you drunk in the gutter by Stilbro’ Market a week ago,” Moore said, sharp and clear. “You are no more a friend to the poor than I am. You’re a hypocrite, stirring up rioting for your own petty selfish ambition, you and Noah there both. The rest of the lads behind you are honest men, just led astray, but you two are scoundrels.”

He lifted two fingers to his lips and whistled shrill and loud. Sugden stepped forward, his staff in one hand, the warrant in the other.

“You were at Stilbro’ Moor, Barraclough,” Moore said, nodding at him. “You wore a mask, you knocked one of my men down. Sugden – arrest him.”

The crowd cried out and surged forward to rescue him, but Moore pulled his hand out from his coat, holding a pistol steady in his grip.

“Both barrels are loaded,” he called out, his voice unshaking. “Stand back.”

The second the deputation of disgruntled mill workers was off his property, Messrs. Helstone and Sykes swarmed around Robert Moore, giddy with congratulations, cracking jokes about how firmly he’d held his ground against the rabble. But Moore said barely a word in response. His face was as still and sunless as a overcast midwinter day, no trace of a smile, no spark of triumph, and the rector, after a long, shrewd look into his shadowed eyes, quickly buttoned up his jovial backpatting right along with his heavy coat. He turned to Sykes, who was too dull to notice he was the last person anyone wanted lingering that minute, and gestured for the door. “Come on, man, our roads run together for a stretch. Let’s be off and leave Moore to whatever quiet fancies he’s obviously set on entertaining.”

“Wait a second — where’s Sugden?” Moore called, lifting his head at last.

Helstone hooted a little, pleased with himself. “Oh, I wasn’t twiddling my thumbs while you were talking to that poor, downcast fellow Farren. I thought no time to waste. While you were parleying, I pried open the back counting-house window, shouted to Murgatroyd in the stables to bring Sykes’s gig around, and snuck Sugden and old Moses — wooden leg and all — out the sash before anyone could spot ‘em. Sugden’s got the reins, drives as fast as Jehu himself, and by now Barraclough’s halfway to the Stilbro’ jail, safe and sound.”

“Very good. Thank you,” Moore said, and rose politely to show them both to the threshold, waiting till their boots were far down the lane before he shut the door behind them.

The rest of the day he was quiet, heavy with thought, not even tossing a quick quip back and forth with his foreman Joe Scott. Joe, for his part, only spoke what was strictly necessary to keep the half-running mill turning, but kept darting sidelong glances at his master, popping into the counting-house every half hour to poke the fire bright for him, and when he locked up at dusk — trade was so slack they were on short hours already — he lingered to say the evening was fair, and he “could wish Mr. Moore’d take a bit of a walk up th’ Hollow. ‘Twould do ye a world of good, sir.”

Moore huffed a short, sharp laugh at that, teased Joe for being fussy as a old aunt, asking if he thought he was some weak child to be coddled, then snatched the keys from his hand and all but pushed him out the door. But before Joe could make it to the yard gate, he called him back.

“Joe, you know those Farrens, don’t you? They’re not making ends meet these days, are they?”

“Can’t be, sir. They’ve had no steady work near three months. Ye saw for yourself William’s half wasted away, pale as a ghost. They’ve sold nearly every stick of furniture they owned.”

“He was never a bad workman, though?”

“Never had a better man on the looms, sir, since ye first opened for trade.”

“And the whole family — decent folks?”

“Niver dacenter. Th’ wife’s a good, honest body, keeps their little cottage so clean ye could eat your porridge right off the flagstone floor. They’ve fallen so far it hurts to see. William knows gardening like the back of his own hand, too — apprenticed to a Scotsman as a lad who taught him every trick of the trade. Wish he could get a job tending some gentleman’s grounds instead of starving.”

“Right, you can go now. Stop hovering there staring at me like that.”

“No other orders, sir?”

“None but to get yourself home to your own supper.”
Joe nodded and vanished.

---

Spring evenings in Yorkshire often turn sharp and raw fast. The day had been bright, warm even in the midday sun, but the air bit cold the second the sun dipped below the horizon, the ground crusted crisp, and before full dark a thin, silvery hoar frost was creeping across the new grass and unfurling buds. It whitened the pavement out front of Briarmains, the old stone home of Mr. Yorke, nipping at the tender new blooms in his garden and dusting the mossy lawn. The great gnarled oak that guarded the road-facing gable, thick trunk and wide bare boughs, seemed to laugh off the light spring frost, same as the tall stand of leafless walnut trees rising behind the house.

It was a moonless but starry night, the windows of Briarmains glowing bright against the dark. This was no isolated, quiet spot: the house sat close to the highway, built long before that highway was even cut, back when the only path to the door was a winding lane through the fields. Briarfield village was barely a mile off, its distant hum and faint yellow glow clear to see. A hundred yards down the road, the big new raw red Wesleyan chapel, Briar Chapel, was holding a prayer meeting. Its lit windows cast a broad golden streak across the road, and a wildly exuberant hymn bounced off every nearby tree and cottage wall, so loud it felt even the staidest Quaker might feel moved to tap his foot. Snatches of the words drifted clear all the way to Briarmains:
> Oh who can explain this struggle for life,
> This travail and pain, this trembling and strife?
> Plague, earthquake, and famine, tumult and war,
> The wonderful coming of Jesus declare!

Then there was a lull of loud, heaving prayer and groans, followed by a shout echoing out the chapel doors: “I’ve found liberty! Doad o’ Bill’s has found liberty!” before the whole congregation burst back into song, faster and louder than before.
The roof never caved in, a testament to how well the slaters had done their work.

If the chapel hummed with fervent life, Briarmains hummed too, in a far quieter, softer way. The curtains were drawn across all the lower windows, hiding the interior and muffling most of the candlelight, but they didn’t muffle the faint sound of voices and quiet laughter filtering out through the panes. There were no guests, just the Yorke family all gathered in the back right parlour, their usual evening sitting room. The windows were made of rich stained glass, deep purple and amber, framing central medallions of Shakespeare’s soft, wise face and Milton’s serene one. On the walls hung prints of wild Canadian forests and blue cascading waterfalls, and blazing bright in their midst was a print of Mount Vesuvius erupting at night, the fiery reds and oranges glowing hot against the cool blue of the distant cataracts.

The fire on the hearth was the kind of roaring hot coal blaze southern folks rarely see in private homes, stacked high in the wide old chimney. Mr. Yorke insisted on fires this big even in the height of summer, and he sat beside it now with a book in his hand, not reading at all, just watching his half dozen kids play. Opposite him sat Mrs. Yorke, a large, solemn woman whose whole face and shoulders bore the weight of what she considered her duty to be grave, unsmiling, and suspicious of every last bit of lightness in the world. To her, being cheerful bordered on profanity, being merry was frivolity, no exceptions. She was a good wife and a careful, devoted mother, but she was convinced every one of her husband’s friends was a scoundrel at heart, and kept all his relatives at arm’s length.

Odd as it seemed, the two of them got along perfectly. Yorke was naturally social and warm, had always loved bright, lively women, but his own shadowy, cynical side had found a perfect match in his wife’s perpetually overcast, unforgiving view of the world. Their kids were all as unusual and sharp as you’d expect from two such strong-willed people. The youngest, a baby, sat on Mrs. Yorke’s knee — the only one she didn’t yet scrutinize and suspect, since it clung to her for every bit of its sustenance, and she was sure of its love.

The next two little girls, Rose and Jessy, huddled at their father’s knee, never going near their mother unless forced. Rose, twelve, was the spitting image of her father, but softened, her dark features gentled to ivory, round blooming cheeks, grey eyes lit with a quiet, serious soul far older than her years. Her mother was determined to force her to grow up just as grim and severe as she was, but Rose’s head was full of quiet, stubborn ideas no one had ever taught her, ideas she’d never been allowed to speak aloud. Little Jessy, younger by a year, was bright and chattering, sharp and full of mischief, fearless of her mother’s strict rules even when she got in trouble, soft and affectionate the second anyone showed her kindness. She was her father’s clear favorite, and though her face was the very copy of her mother’s, her bright, playful grin made her look nothing like the stern woman at the other end of the hearth. No one at Briarmains knew it then, but twenty years from that night, little Jessy would be gone, buried in a grave in a far foreign country, and Rose would be a lone emigrant sitting on the banks of an unknown southern river, thousands of miles from Yorkshire.

The three oldest, the boys Matthew, Mark, and Martin, were clustered in the far corner playing a boisterous board game. All three were dark-haired, dark-eyed, ruddy-cheeked, but their faces held very different natures. Matthew, the eldest, was the spitting image of the blazing Vesuvius print on the wall behind them, his whole soul made of flame and shadow, no soft sunlight anywhere in sight. His parents never dared cross him, placating him at every turn to avoid his rages, treating his younger brothers like they had to give way to him at all times, and the resentment was already simmering. Mark, the second, was calm, quiet, his smile sharp and cutting, already middle-aged in his soul at fourteen, already convinced all joy and laughter was foolish. Martin, the youngest of the three, was plain and awkward, a rough chrysalis just waiting for the day he’d shed his dry shell and bloom bright, hungry for all the pleasure and knowledge the world had to offer, no one quite sure if he’d turn out to be a great man, or something else entirely.

A soft tap at the door cut through the noise of the game. No one had heard the front door knocker over the hymn singing from the chapel and Jessy’s little Scotch song she’d been singing for her father. “Come in,” Mrs. Yorke called, her voice deliberately slow and grave, the same funereal tone she used even to give orders for the kitchen to bake a pudding.

In stepped Robert Moore. His habitual quiet gravity was just the sort of thing Mrs. Yorke approved of, and she hadn’t yet found a reason to whisper bad things about him behind his back the way she did all her husband’s other bachelor friends.

“Well, it’s you, is it?” she said, as he walked over to shake her hand. “What are you doing wandering about out at this time of night? You ought to be home.”

“Can a single man be said to have a home, madam?” he asked.

“Pooh,” she huffed, plainspoken as ever, no time for frills. “Don’t talk that nonsense to me. A single man can have a home if he wants. Doesn’t your sister Hortense make a home for you?”

“Hardly that,” Mr. Yorke cut in, laughing. “Hortense is a good honest lass, but when I was Robert’s age I had half a dozen sisters just as proper as her, and it never stopped me from going out and looking for a wife anyway.”

“And I’ve made him regret it every day since,” Mrs. Yorke joked dryly, nodding at the six kids swarming the room. “Young man, when you ever feel tempted to go and marry, look at these four rough boys and two girls, all to feed, clothe, rear, and set up in the world, and think twice before you leap.”

“I’m not tempted any time soon, at any rate,” Moore said. “These aren’t the sort of times for marrying or giving in marriage.”

That dark, grave sentiment won Mrs. Yorke over instantly. She nodded and groaned in full agreement, then waved him to a seat. The second he sat down, little Jessy jumped right off her father’s knee and ran straight into his outstretched arms, and he lifted her easily onto his lap.

“You’re talking to him like he needs to go find a wife,” she said indignantly to her mother, wiggling to get comfortable, “but he’s already got one, or as good as. He promised last summer, the first time he saw me in my new white frock and blue sash, that I’d be his wife. Didn’t he, father?”

Spring deepened, soft and lush across England. Every hedgerow burst bright green, every hillside glowed fresh after rain, cottage gardens spilled over with crocus and daffodil bloom, but the country’s heart was far from light. The poor still shivered in want, their employers haggard with sleepless worry. Trade hung on the edge of paralysis, for the war dragged on, bleeding English men and money for ends that felt smaller and smaller, further and further out of reach. Tidings of occasional Peninsula victories trickled in so slowly that weeks would stretch where the only news was Bonaparte’s crowing boasts of his own triumphs, and the men teetering on the very brink of bankruptcy felt the endless, grinding fight against his seemingly unbeatable power more than they could bear. They screamed for peace at any cost, held raucous meetings, ranted in speeches, pushed petitions that cared not a whit what terms were surrendered, so long as their shops and mills were spared ruin.

The great mass of men, when gathered in their own self-interest, are unapologetically selfish, and the British merchant class embodied that truth clearly then. Chivalry, honour, disinterested pride in their nation – all had burned thin under the threat of lost profits. Let France buffet them on the right cheek, they would turn the left, hand over their coats, their waistcoats, every last garment save the one holding their purse. They would cringe and fawn until Bonaparte’s hand closed over that beloved purse, and only then would the British bulldog spark wake in them. They claimed to hate war for its bloodshed and cruelty, but most cared nothing for the far-off fighting – they cared only for their ledgers, and deemed everyone not bent on trade to be wasting air, eating bread they had no right to earn. Long may England never become a nation of shopkeepers alone.

Robert Moore, for all his quiet grit, was one of those men thrashing hard to save his mill from ruin, fanning agitation across the north against the war, pushing his wealthier, better-connected peers to join his cause. Sometimes, in rare flashes of clarity, he saw the absurdity of begging Bonaparte for concessions: when he read dispatches of all Europe rising to throw back the invader, of the Duke of Wellington’s steady, unswerving advance across the Peninsula, his dispatches written with plain, unboastful truth, Moore knew victory would come in the end. But that end was too far off. He would be crushed before it arrived, his mills ground to dust, his family’s legacy lost. He had no time to spare for anything else.

The rift with his old Tory friend, the Rector Mr. Helstone, came swiftly after that. They clashed publicly at a town meeting, fired sharp, sniping letters back and forth across the local newspapers, and Helstone branded Moore a Jacobin radical, refused to greet him when they passed on the lane, and gave his niece Caroline a clear, unmistakeable order: her daily visits to Hollow’s Cottage were to cease, her French lessons with Hortense done for good. The French language was frivolous, he grumbled, unsuited to weak female minds, and the Moore household was full of dangerous, disloyal ideas.

Helstone had expected tears, arguments, rebellion. He had long suspected Caroline cared far more for Robert Moore than she ought. He had watched, when the oafish curate Malone dropped by of an evening, making a fool of himself pinching the old rector’s cat and firing his fowling piece at the garden tool shed for sport, how Caroline would slip silently upstairs and vanish until supper. When Robert Moore called, though, no guns were fired, no noise was made – he would only coax the old cat onto his knee to purr, and Caroline would sit quiet by the window, stitching pin-cushions and knitting socks for missionary bundles, perfectly content for hours. Helstone had once caught their eyes meet for a single second at the door, when they bade each other goodnight. No hidden scandal, no whispered secret – only Moore’s gaze softening a little at the sight of her clear, gentle eyes, and Caroline’s face flushing just faintly at the warmth of his steady, manly regard. It had annoyed him so much he could have boxed both their ears, vowing no silly romance would fester under his roof.

So he announced his order to her one evening, as she sat stitching by the drawing room window, the full light falling on her paler, quieter face. She did not throw a fit, or burst into tears. She only gave a tiny, almost unnoticeable start, and did not look up at him.

“Do you hear me?” he demanded.

“Yes, uncle.”

“Of course you will obey? No secret letters to Hortense, no sneaking visits. Their principles are Jacobinical, and I will not have you associating with them.”

“Very well,” Caroline said, soft and calm.

She was not being meek. The order was no hardship – visiting Hollow’s Cottage had become nothing but pain for weeks now. Robert was never there. Hortense would shake her head and say he was at the counting house, that he was working himself to the bone, that he never stopped long enough to eat a proper meal. The only time Caroline ever saw him was at church, and she barely dared glance his way. Looking was half sweet, half sharp agony, a surge of feeling that all went nowhere, wasted on a man who had no thought to spare for her.

One dark, rainy Sunday, the pews nearly empty, all the sharp-tongued town gossips safely home, she dared let her eyes drift to his pew. He sat alone, Hortense stayed home to protect her new spring bonnet from the rain. His arms were folded, his eyes cast down, his face shadowed and tired, the usual warm olive of his skin faded to pale, ashy grey. Caroline could see his thoughts were miles away, tangled up in the weights of business that she would never be allowed to understand, a whole world of worry she could never step into. When the service ended, he rose, and walked out of the church without turning his head once. He did not glance her way, not for a single second. That small, unthinking snub made her throat ache so tight she could barely breathe. A single kind glance would have carried her happily through the whole next week. But he had not spared it. He was gone.

That evening, when Malone came to call on the rector, Caroline slipped up to her room after tea. Fanny had lit a little, crackling fire for her, for the wind was howling sharp outside, and the rain lashed the windows. She could not read. The book of Sunday devotions lay open on her table, its words slipping right through her mind as she paced the carpet, head bowed, hands folded in front of her. Her head was full of memories: the winter evenings they had sat by the cottage fire, the long summer afternoon they had wandered through Nunnely Wood, the soft spring day in Hollow’s Copse when the cuckoo called overhead, and she had picked a basket of wild blackberries, arranging them with green leaves and clover, feeding them to him one by one, like a little bird feeding its fledgling.

The memories faded fast. The sound of his voice dissolved, the warm imaginary pressure of his hand on her shoulder turned cold as sleet, and she was left alone in her small, dim room, the rain tapping on the glass, the only companion her own shadow stretched long on the wall. She sank down into her chair, and a quiet, heavy thought settled over her. She was young, and healthy – she might live another fifty years, all the way to seventy. How on earth was she supposed to fill that whole stretch of empty time?

It dawned on her, soft and certain, that she would never marry. Robert did not care for her, not the way she cared for him. She had always assumed she would grow up to be a wife, a mother, to fill her days with the small, warm duties of a home of her own. But that path was closed to her, now. She would be an old maid. She would watch Robert marry some wealthy, accomplished lady one day, and she would be left behind. What was the point of her, then? What place was there for her in the world?

She sat musing for hours, staring into the dying embers of the fire. Everyone said old maids ought to devote their whole lives to helping others, to caring for the poor, tending sick neighbours, running errands for friends – a noble doctrine, but wasn’t it a little hollow? Was living only for other people, having no life or love of your own, enough to sustain a soul? She was not sure it was. She had rights, just the same as every other living person, a right to something of her own to love, to tend to, to call hers. And when she died, the body would turn to dust in the grave, and the soul would fly upward, to whatever unknowable hereafter waited.

By the time the study bell rang for evening prayers, the last cinder in her fire had crumbled to ash.

The next day, her uncle was gone all afternoon, dining with the Vicar of Whinbury, Dr. Boultby. Caroline sat alone, stitching listlessly, until Fanny bustled in, chiding her for sitting quiet and still all day, urging her to go out, call on the two old maids of the village, Miss Mann and Miss Ainley. Caroline protested that their houses were dismal, that old maids were an unhappy race – but then she thought of Miss Ainley, who had tended her stepmother for decades, who spent every penny she could spare on the poor, who no one ever visited, who the gentlemen of the parish made fun of behind her back.

“I’ll go see her,” she said suddenly, standing up. “How cruel it is, to neglect people just because they are not young and pretty and merry. I’ll call on Miss Mann too. I want to know what made her the way she is. I want to understand what life is like, for the women who never get to walk the path I once thought was mine.”

Fanny bustled about happily, dressing her in her soft brown silk frock, brushing her shiny curls till they fell soft over her shoulders, tucking warm shawls around her till Caroline was almost sweltering under all the layers. “You’ll never be an old maid, miss,” Fanny told her cheerfully. But Caroline glanced in the little mirror, and saw how pale her cheeks were, how faint the dark circles under her eyes, how her smile had lost some of its old brightness. She did not know what the future held. But for the first time, she was no longer afraid to face it.

Caroline Helstone would not let her heart break quietly, not without a fight. Deep under her soft, girlish ribs, a fierce unplumbed strength burned bright, stoked by the raw, sharp pain that was her only constant companion now. No soul struggles harder than the one that fights entirely alone: no friend to lean on, no confidante to whisper her grief to, no one to pat her hand and say it will pass, no one even to notice she is suffering at all.

That was exactly Caroline’s lot. Her pain was her spur, and it drove her to push herself harder than she ever had before. She would not let the memory of Robert Moore eat her alive if she could help it. She threw herself into every task she could find: pored over French grammar, hemmed stacks of household linen, and tramped out the rectory door in every kind of weather, heading down the loneliest, wildest lanes she could find, no matter if rain bit at her cheeks or wind tangled her hair. Every evening she came back pale, her boots caked in mud, her dress spattered with mist, but she would not rest the second she stepped through the door. She tossed off her bonnet and shawl and paced her small bedroom for hours, until her legs shook so badly she could barely stand, until she all but collapsed from faintness. She told herself she did it to tire herself out enough to sleep through the night, but it never worked. When the rest of the rectory slumbered deep, she tossed on her pillow, or huddled at the foot of her bed in the dark, tears soaking the linen, that sharp, unbearable despair crashing over her in waves that left her as helpless as a lost little child.

Desperate as she was, temptation nipped at her heels: more than once she sat at her desk, pen in hand, scribbling half-letters to Robert, begging him to write to her sometimes, to not forget her entirely, even if only friendship was all she dared ask for. But shame and her own quiet pride always won, and she crumpled every single one, tossing the pages into the fire before she could seal the envelope.

By and by, the weight of it all grew so heavy she thought she could not bear it another day. She ached to leave Briarfield, to go somewhere so far away the very name of Hollow’s Mill would not make her chest ache. Another secret, deeper yearning grew stronger every day: she wanted to find the mother she had never known, the woman no one ever spoke of kindly. Her uncle’s cold quiet dislike of his late sister-in-law, the old servant who only ever called her “queer” and “unreadable” — all those stories iced over that longing, leaving her to wonder if it was better never to meet her than to meet her and find she could not love her own mother.

At last, one clear plan formed in her head: she would leave and take a position as a governess. It was the only honest work a penniless girl of her station could do. A small run of luck gave her the courage to finally speak of it to her uncle.

All her long walks led her, unbidden, to the same spot every twilight: the thorn tree stile at the very edge of the Hollow, where she could look down over the mill cottages, the dewy kitchen gardens, the still black mill dam, and the little counting-house window where, every evening at the same hour, Robert’s lamp would flare to life, bright as a tiny star. She waited for it, no matter the weather: clear nights it glowed soft gold, misty nights it blurred to a hazy smudge, rainy nights it fractured into a dozen tiny dancing sparks through the slanting drops. If a shadow passed between that lamp and the glass, her heart would leap so hard she thought it might burst: that was him, that was Robert. She would walk home then, comforted, carrying the sharp clear image of his face, his smile, his low voice, tucked safe in her memory, and she would tell herself he must still care, a little, that if she could only step in front of him he would reach for her hand, pull her close, and shelter her the way he used to. Those nights her tears did not sting quite so hot, her head ached a little less.

The shortest path home from the Hollow ran past the walls of Fieldhead, that old, grand, long-empty mansion Mr. Malone had stumbled past on that dark night not long before. It had stood tenantless for ten years, but Mr. Yorke had kept it in fine repair, with an old gardener and his wife living in the gate lodge to tend the lawns and keep the halls dusted. It was the very picture of a romantic old country house: gray stone weathered soft with moss, latticed windows, a deep carved stone porch, great spreading oaks behind it and a massive ancient cedar on the front lawn. The granite urns on the garden wall, the fretted stone arch of the gate, all looked as if they had stepped straight out of an artist’s sketchbook.

One mild May evening, Caroline wandered past as the full moon was rising, too heartsick to go back to the rectory to the empty quiet that waited for her there. She sat down on the moss by the gate, gazing through the iron bars at the dark cedar boughs and the pale stone gables glowing amber in the last of the sunset. The air was still, dewy, cloudless, and the moon hung low under the cedar’s raven-dark branches, shining soft and solemn right down on her. The whole night felt mournfully, perfectly lovely, and Caroline sat there and wondered why Providence would not have pity on her, why she could not just know a little peace. She was daydreaming of the old ballad lovers who met in just such quiet moonlit places, when suddenly the big front door of Fieldhead creaked open. Two men stepped out: one white-haired, elderly, that was Mr. Yorke, and the other was tall, dark-haired, Robert Moore. They walked across the lawn, out the garden gate, and down the lane without ever seeing her hidden in the shadows by the iron bars.

He was gone as fast as a flash of lightning, but the sight of him sent a shock right through her cold bones. Where before she had been despairing, now she was wild with desperate longing.
“Oh, if only he had been alone! If only he had seen me!” she whispered to the dark. “He would have stopped. He would have spoken. He does love me, he must. But the chance is gone. He passed me by as quiet, as empty, as a drift of wind through the grass. It’s cruel, it’s all cruel.”
She dragged herself home that night, sick with unmet longing.

The next morning at breakfast, she appeared at the table pale as a ghost, her cheeks drained of all their usual rose, and asked her uncle Mr. Helstone, “Would you mind, uncle, if I looked for a position working as a governess in a family?”

Her uncle stared at her, as bewildered as the coffee pot sitting on the table in front of him. “What nonsense is this?” he blurted. “Have you lost your wits? What on earth do you mean?”
“I’m not well. I need a change,” she said, quiet.

He leaned forward to look at her, and for the first time he noticed how thin she had grown, how all her bloom had faded away, like a summer rose withered down to a fragile snowdrop. Her flesh had wasted away, her eyes were big and dark in her sharpened face, and if not for the soft curve of her mouth and the thick cascade of her hair, no one would have called her pretty anymore.
“What’s wrong with you, child?” he demanded. “Shall I send for the doctor?”
Caroline shook her head. “A doctor can’t help me. All I need is new air, new scenery.”

He laughed, gruff but not unkind. “Well, if that’s what you want, you shall have it. We’ll send you off to the seaside at Cliff Bridge, Fanny will go with you. I’ll not have my niece working as a governess, not while I’m alive. People will talk, and say I can’t provide for my own flesh and blood. I’ll buy you an annuity, see? I’m only fifty-five, perfectly healthy, there’s plenty of time to sort all that out. Stop fretting about the future, there’s no need for it.”

Caroline sighed, but she knew there was no arguing with him. A week drifted by, and she stayed in that strange half-state, no worse, no better: she did not die, as all the old gossips in the village predicted she soon would, though her appetite was gone, her sleep was thin, and she was so weak she tired after walking half a mile. She avoided all the village calls, the tea parties where the other young ladies would stare at her, whispering behind their hands that she had been “disappointed in love,” a phrase that made her shrink so far into herself she hardly left the rectory walls at all.

One morning, her uncle burst into the parlour where she was daubing watercolours of wildflowers she had picked down by the Hollow, and ordered her to drop the paint, put her bonnet on, and come with him to pay a call.
“Where are we going?” she asked, bewildered — he never took her with him on calls.
“Fieldhead,” he said. “Miss Shirley Keeldar’s come home at last, turned twenty-one, moved into the house a week ago. Met her at the party last night you refused to come to. She’s a fine, spirited girl, no moping nonsense about her. You two will get on a treat. It’ll do you a world of good.”

Caroline’s heart fluttered with nerves as they walked up the broad paved drive to the old porch. She followed her uncle into the dark, vast vestibule, lit only by one small latticed window, its high walls hung with carved stags’ heads, its wide empty hearth stacked full of willow boughs for the summer. The servant showed them into a panelled parlour, its walls lined with dark glowing old oak, full of heavy antique furniture that had sat in that room for a hundred years at least.
Sitting by the fireplace was a soft, round, quiet-looking lady with auburn hair, dressed in an old-fashioned gown, who fumbled and stammered through greetings, clearly far more nervous than Caroline was. Caroline, used to shyness herself, sat down beside her to put her at ease, but Mr. Helstone’s sharp, sarcastic stare made the poor woman flustered more and more, until she stammered that she must go fetch Miss Keeldar, and had half risen from her chair when the glass door leading out to the garden swung open, and a girl stepped in.

Erect, bright-eyed, slight, her left hand full of daisies and wild roses tucked into her silk apron, Shirley Keeldar walked right up to Mr. Helstone, held out her right hand, and grinned. “I knew you’d come call on me, even if you are convinced Mr. Yorke has turned me into a Jacobin. Good morning!”
Mr. Helstone laughed, taking her hand. “Oh we’ll soon knock that Jacobin nonsense out of you, Miss Shirley. Now you’re here in my parish, you’re my pupil — I’ll teach you all the sound doctrine you need for politics and religion, mark my words.”

Shirley Keeldar proved her words no empty courtesy by haunting the lanes between Fieldhead and the rectory, turning up unannounced at Caroline Helstone’s door so often that the maids began to smile and set an extra teacup out on the sill of every fine afternoon. Not that Caroline would ever have sought her out first: the quiet, diffident girl could never shake the ingrained belief that no one could truly want her company, that she was too slow, too uninteresting, too little acquainted with the bright, sparkling world the young heiress moved in. She could not fathom how Shirley, surrounded by half a dozen eager neighbouring families all vying for her notice, could ever prefer the stillness of her small parlour to their noisy dinner parties and dances.

Shirley was bright, and for the most part happy, but no soul alive can thrive without the kind of society that seeps into your bones like warm sunlight. She had, in the space of a month, made polite acquaintance with every family for three parishes round: she laughed with the Misses Sykes, gossiped with the Misses Pearson, and traded witticisms with the two much-celebrated Miss Wynnes of Walden Hall, but not one of them, as she put it, felt like kin. “If I were indeed Shirley Keeldar, Esquire, lord of Briarfield manor,” she told her old governess Mrs. Pryor one soft May morning, leaning against the wide window frame overlooking Fieldhead’s lawn, “there is not a single lady within twenty miles I would ask to step up and be mistress of the manor beside me.”

Mrs. Pryor, darning a fine linen handkerchief in her lap, did not look up at once, her fingers moving steady over the stitch. “My dear, do not let that odd habit of referring to yourself as a gentleman take root. Strangers hearing you speak so will think you affect masculine manners, and that will do you no credit.”

Shirley never laughed at Mrs. Pryor. She had too much respect for quiet, unassuming worth to ever turn her governess’s little harmless peculiarities into a jest. So she said nothing, only stood still, her eyes fixed on the great ancient cedar at the lawn’s edge, watching a plump little sparrow hop along its lowest bough. Before long her lips parted, and she began to chirp softly at it, clear and trilling, till the chirp swelled into a whistle, sweet and light, winding through the old Scottish air she had heard only the night before, drifting over the hedge from the lane where Robert Moore had been walking after closing his mill.

“My dear!” Mrs. Pryor expostulated, lifting her head.

“Did I whistle?” Shirley said, flushing a little. “I quite forgot. I beg your pardon, ma’am. I promised myself I would never do that in your hearing.”

“And where did you pick up that habit?” Mrs. Pryor asked, mild but curious. “You never once whistled when you were in my charge as a girl.”

“I learned it long ago, by ear,” Shirley said, leaning her shoulder against the window frame. “I’d forgotten I knew how, till last night. I was walking up our lane, and heard a gentleman whistling that very tune on the other side of the hedge, and it all came back.”

“What gentleman?”

“There’s only one young gentleman in this part of the world who is not gray-haired, isn’t there? Mr. Moore. Though I will say my two old favourites, your rector Mr. Helstone and Mr. Yorke, are far finer company than any of the silly lads our age round here.”

Mrs. Pryor fell quiet for a long moment, then said, “You walk out this morning, my dear?”

“I am going straight to the rectory,” Shirley said, already reaching for her bonnet. “I am going to drag Caroline Helstone out for a walk over Nunnely Common. The wind is fine and bracing, and she mopes indoors far too much.”

“Then do remind her to wrap her shawl tight,” Mrs. Pryor said. “The air has a sharp bite to it, and she looks as though she catches cold easily. Will you not ask me to come with you?” Shirley added, smiling. “We would make a very merry party.”

“No, my love,” Mrs. Pryor said, shaking her head. “I am too stout, and I cannot keep pace with your long legs. I should only be a restraint on you.”

Shirley had no trouble at all persuading Caroline to come out with her. The two of them stepped out onto the quiet road, the wide, empty sweep of Nunnely Common stretching out before them covered in new green turf and dotted with the first purple buds of heather, and before long all Caroline’s shyness melted away. The first few casual words they exchanged were enough for both of them to know they had stumbled on something rare, something no other friend had ever given them. Shirley talked of the Scottish border moors she had travelled the summer before, that long sultry afternoon where they had driven from noon till sunset across endless heath, seeing nothing but wild sheep, hearing nothing but the cries of curlews, the sky heavy and livid with coming storm.

Caroline nodded, her eyes softening. “I know that exact hue. The heath turns purple-black, deeper even than the storm cloud hanging low over it. You wait for lightning to split it any second.”
