They talked of the distant thunder muttering over the hills, of the rain pouring down in sheets so thick the mountain peaks vanished entirely from sight, as if they had been washed clean off the face of the earth, and then the quiet that comes after, the soft sliver of sun breaking through the clouds like a promise the world has not ended.

“Stop here a minute,” Caroline said, tugging gently at Shirley’s sleeve, and they paused on the high green brow of the common. Below them Nunnely Dale lay draped in all its May finery: meadows pearled over with daisies, blazing golden with kingcups, the old remnant of ancient British forest Nunnwood draped in a soft drifting cloud shadow, the distant hills dappled blue and purple, the horizon tinted like mother-of-pearl, faint rose and pale green melting into fleeces of white cloud so pure they looked like fresh snow fallen on the sky. The wind blew cool and sweet on their faces, scented with gorse and wild violet.

“England is a bonny isle,” Shirley said softly. “And Yorkshire is its fairest corner. I was born here, you know. Five generations of my family are buried under the aisles of Briarfield church. I drew my first breath in that old black stone hall back behind us.”

Caroline held out her hand, and Shirley took it, shaking it firmly, a smile tugging at her lips. “Then we are countrywomen,” Caroline said.

“And that dark stretch of trees down there is Nunnwood?” Shirley asked, pointing.

Caroline nodded, and told her how the trees were old giants, trunks as thick as castle pillars, their tops so high they seemed to touch another sky, the ruins of an ancient nunnery tucked away in a green dell at its heart. Shirley’s eyes lit up. “Then we will go there, just the two of us, one long bright summer day. We’ll bring sketchbooks, and a book of old ballads, and two little baskets Mrs. Gill can pack full of provisions, and we’ll wander all day, no one to bother us.”

Caroline’s face lit up, and she began to list all her favourite spots in the wood: the glades carpeted with moss bright as gemstones, the wild strawberry patches, the gnarled old oaks draped in ivy, the little dell where the nunnery ruins half-buried in ferns lay. “There is no third person alive whose company would not spoil that,” Caroline said. “Not even the nicest gentleman. The moment a man joins the party, you forget all about the trees, don’t you? Nature herself seems to draw a veil over her face, and all that quiet joy vanishes, replaced by excitement and restlessness that makes the day fly by too fast, leaving nothing behind when it’s gone.”

They fell to talking then, quiet and earnest, of marriage. Shirley spoke of the horror she felt at the thought of being trapped, of being tied to someone who might one day grow tired of her, till her presence became a burden she could not escape no matter how badly she wanted to slip away into solitude. Caroline laughed a little, and said her uncle thought every man who married was a fool, and that Shirley was brave to hope for better, and then slowly, shyly, the two of them found themselves talking of Robert Moore. Shirley’s eyes sparkled, her cheeks flushing warm, when Caroline told her he was the one the stray dogs and the rectory’s black cat adored, the one who never pushed an animal roughly away, who was gentle even to the poorest beggar at his door.

“He is a noble being,” Shirley said, her voice soft with enthusiasm. “When a man is good, and kind, and handsome, there is nothing finer in all creation. I would never dream of competing with such a soul for precedence. It is not demeaning to look up to someone, not at all. It is glorious. There is no joy greater than finding a soul far higher and better than your own to admire.”

They had reached the rectory gates by then, the ivy climbing high over the stone pillars. “Don’t come in today,” Caroline said, smiling. “My uncle will be in his study, and he will badger you for half an hour about politics.”

“Then I will come fetch you tomorrow, to spend the evening at Fieldhead,” Shirley said, bending to press a soft, warm kiss to Caroline’s cheek. “I have never talked to any girl the way I have talked to you today, Caroline Helstone. I think you and I are going to suit very well. Goodbye, for now.”

* * * * *

Mrs. Pryor had no less eager a desire to be Caroline’s friend than Shirley did. A few days later, she paid a rare call at the rectory, stepping in through the door on a close, warm afternoon when the rector was safely out visiting his parishioners. She was flushed from the walk, and strangely fluttered, trembling a little as she sank down on the parlour sofa, fanning herself with her handkerchief, as if the strange house had stripped away all her usual composure. Caroline, surprised, went to her at once, gently lifting off her bonnet, loosening the fastenings of her heavy wool shawl, her light soft hands soothing the nervous tremor out of Mrs. Pryor’s limbs as no one else’s could. She recovered in a few minutes, and soon the two of them were talking easily, Mrs. Pryor’s conversation thoughtful and well-spoken, her sentiments fair and gentle, far more pleasant than Caroline had ever expected.

Their eyes fell on the portraits hanging on the opposite wall then: two gentlemen in dark old formal coats on either side of a lovely lady’s face over the mantelpiece. “That is a remarkably beautiful face,” Mrs. Pryor said, pausing in their talk. “Perfect features, no sculptor could improve on them. That is Mrs. Helstone, your uncle’s wife, is it not?”

Caroline nodded, and told her the story: how the quiet, silent woman had been the beauty of the district, had married the rector, and died only five years later. “He always says he could never have borne a talkative wife,” Caroline said. “He claims you go out to gossip, and you come home to read in quiet.”

Mrs. Pryor stood up to go, smoothing her skirts, and let Caroline wrap her shawl around her shoulders. “I hope you will come to Fieldhead very often, my dear,” she said softly. “You must be so lonely here, with no other woman in the house. And if you ever wish for help with your studies, languages, drawing, anything at all, you have only to ask me.”

There were hours when Shirley Keeldar slipped into such soft, unhurried indolence that moving even a single finger felt like a waste of perfect, sun-drenched joy. On bright afternoons, following a morning spent galloping her favourite mare or poring over her estate ledgers, she would stretch motionless on thick clover turf beneath the gnarled old oak at Fieldhead’s edge, perfectly content to do nothing at all. She needed no grand company save Caroline Helstone somewhere within earshot, no spectacle but the endless blue arch of sky streaked with wispy cloudlets, no sound but the drowsy hum of foraging bees and the soft rustle of leaves overhead. In those quiet moments she never reached for a book: her reading was the gentle drift of old happy memories, and the sweet, unspoken premonitions of what might lie ahead—some secret dream that pulled a soft private smile to her lips, never sad, never shadowed. Fate had always been kind to her, and she trusted it would not fail her now.

One golden afternoon, Caroline came trotting over to rouse her, thinking she had lain napping long enough, only to stop short when she looked down. Shirley’s cheeks were damp with tears, her dark eyes glistening and brimming over like dewdrops on a wild rose petal.
“Shirley, why are *you* crying?” Caroline asked, the word slipping out soft and stunned, for Shirley was the last person she would ever have imagined weeping unbidden in the sun.

Shirley laughed, turning her lovely wild-haired face up to her, tears still clinging to her lashes. “Because it feels so good to cry,” she said. “My heart is so full it aches, half with joy, half with tender sadness. But you, good patient thing—why don’t you join me? My tears are the sweet, fleeting kind that dry in the sun. Yours would be the bitter sort, I think, if you let them fall.”

“Why would I ever weep bitter tears?”
“Because you are a little mateless, solitary bird,” Shirley said softly, the only answer she would give.

“And aren’t you mateless too, Shirley?”
“At heart? No,” Shirley said, that giddy secret smile tugging at her mouth again.
“Oh—who has nested there, then?” Caroline teased, but Shirley only laughed louder, springing nimbly to her feet, curls tumbling loose over her shoulders.
“It was just a daydream,” she said, cheeks pink. “Bright as sunlight, and probably nothing but air.”

By this point, Caroline had few illusions left about her own quiet unrequited love for Robert Moore. She saw clearly how her future was likely to unfold, and yet old habits and old tender ties still drew her to the familiar field stile and the gnarled thorn tree overlooking the Hollow every evening, where she used to wait for the faint lamp glow that used to shine from Robert’s cottage window.

The night after he had sent her the short sharp business note, she waited there for hours, watching in vain: no lamp lit that night. She stayed until the constellations climbed high enough to warn her the hour was far too late, and turned for home. Passing Fieldhead, the full moon’s silver glow over its walls stopped her in her tracks. The great old hall rose peaceful under the sky, its stone gilded pale as pearl, wrapped in soft mellow brown shadow, the oak boughs over its roof draped in deep dark green. The wide pavement before the door gleamed too, as if some fairy spell had turned dark granite to glimmering white marble. Two sharp dark shadows stretched across that silver space, cast by two figures walking slowly side by side. They were still and silent at first, then began to move in perfect easy step, their voices low and soft. Caroline leaned forward, squinting as they stepped out from behind the thick cedar trunk. Was that Mrs. Pryor with Shirley?

Of course it was Shirley. No one else had that lithe, proud, graceful stride. Her face was turned up to the moon, soft and unguarded, half pensive, half mirthful, half teasing, half tender. She hadn’t bothered to cover her head against the night dew, her dark curls tumbling free, brushing her neck and caressing her shoulders. A gold chain glinted through the soft folds of the silk scarf wrapped across her chest, and a large bright sapphire glittered on the white hand that held the scarf closed. It was unquestionably Shirley.

Her companion then couldn’t possibly be Mrs. Pryor—unless Mrs. Pryor had suddenly grown to six feet tall, and traded her neat widow’s weeds for a tall young man’s coat. It was Robert Moore himself, her tenant, the master of the Hollow mill.

They spoke too softly for their words to carry at first, and Caroline could not bring herself to slip away. There had been a time not so long ago when Robert had walked these same paths with her, after the sun went down, when they would wander up the Hollow together to smell the sweet wild herbs that grew on the narrow terrace at the edge of the deep ravine, listening to the little watercourse gurgle over wet stones under the dark boughs of the alders.

“But I used to be closer to him,” Caroline thought, a sharp quiet ache winding through her chest. “He never felt like he had to treat me with distant homage. He only had to be kind. He used to hold my hand, and now he doesn’t even brush Shirley’s fingers. But Shirley isn’t proud with the person she loves—her stance has that natural noble grace she was born with, but there’s no haughtiness in her face right now. Robert must be looking at her and seeing exactly what I see: that bright soft fire in her eyes, that smile sweeter than any I’ve ever seen on any other woman. I can tell he feels its beauty, deep in a man’s heart, the kind of feeling I never dared let myself hope he would have for me. They look like two happy souls, walking that silver pavement like it’s the white shore we all dream of, beyond the river of death. And here I am, hidden in the dark, my own heart heavier than the shadows I’m shrinking into. I’m just a poor mortal, standing alone, wondering why I was ever born at all.”

She took a shaky breath, steadying herself. “I saw this coming, the very first day Shirley arrived. Rich, young, lovely—of course Robert would fall for her. She’s his now. He’s her lover. They’ll be married, and he’ll grow to love her more and more every day. I don’t begrudge them their happiness, truly I don’t. But my own pain burns so sharp sometimes, I can hardly breathe. I almost wish I’d never been born at all.”

Just then, Shirley stepped aside to pluck a dewy wild rose, and the pair turned toward the gate, their voices suddenly loud enough to hear. Caroline would not stay to eavesdrop. She slipped away silently, the moonlight falling soft on the stone wall where her shadow had just stood.

“You have all the tenacity of a bulldog,” Shirley was saying, half teasing. “I’ve never met anyone so stubborn, so quiet in his schemes, never giving warning, just coming up noiselessly behind a problem and holding on until it’s solved.”
“That’s hardly a compliment,” Robert laughed. “Am I really so unrefined?”
“Far-seeing, calculating, unshakable,” she went on. “That’s you.”
“I’ve gathered word that the men who rioted at the mill are plotting vengeance now that Barraclough has been sentenced to transportation,” Robert said, turning serious. “I’ve drawn up plans to defend the mill, to make sure no one gets hurt, and no one destroys the livelihood of the workers who depend on me. I wanted your approval before I act. Will you stand with me, as long as I stay on the defensive?”
“Of course I will,” Shirley said at once.
“Then I am satisfied,” he said, his voice warm, softer than she had ever heard it. “I would have gone through with it anyway, but now it doesn’t feel like a burden at all. It feels like a pleasure.”
“I can tell you’re enjoying yourself far more than you ever would have taking a government order for army cloth,” Shirley teased.
“Frankly, I do. Would you talk to old Mr. Helstone for me? I know he’s still cross with me over our old disagreements, but I’d like the rector on my side. He’s made of true, solid metal.”
“Leave it to me,” Shirley said, grinning. “He’ll be on your side before the week is out. He can’t resist a good noble cause, no matter how cross he is with you.”
“Whom couldn’t you win over to your side?” Robert said, and there was a low unspoken adoration in his tone that hung thick in the silver moonlight.
“Watch me win him, then,” Shirley laughed. “It’ll cost me a whole afternoon of tea, toast, cake, and a lot of gentle persuading. Oh, it’s growing chill,” she added, shivering a little.
“I’m so sorry to keep you out here,” he said, at once concerned. “It’s just that evenings like this, and your company, are so rare for me. I never want it to end.”
“I should go in,” Shirley said gently. “Mrs. Pryor will be cross if I stay out too late. You ought not to wander the Hollow alone tonight, either—there’s a mad weaver named Michael Hartley roaming the paths with a gun, the sort of man who’s sworn he’ll do you harm. Promise me you’ll go straight home, and stay out of the glen tonight.”
“If you wish it,” he said, pausing, his voice so low it was almost a murmur. “Life used to feel aimless to me, three months ago. I was drowning, and I didn’t much care if I sank. Then a hand reached out to me. A delicate, bright hand, strong enough to pull me all the way back from ruin. My life’s invaluable now. I have something worth living for.”
“Then live for it,” Shirley said, her cheeks pink. “Don’t go throwing yourself in harm’s way. Good night, Robert.”

The next evening, Caroline kept her promise to spend the night at Fieldhead. She had spent the whole prior shut up in her little bedroom, sewing and sewing at a summer muslin dress, her brain working faster than her fingers. She ached for some hard absorbing work, something to occupy her mind so thoroughly she would not have to think of the two people she loved most in the world, happy without her. She decided she would beg her uncle to let her take a post as a governess, and first she would ask Mrs. Pryor to speak for her. Tears fell on her busy hands every now and then, but she wiped them away quickly, re-threaded her needle, and kept sewing.

When she arrived at Fieldhead’s oak parlour, tea was just being set on the table. Shirley looked her over, frowning a little at her pale cheeks. “You look lovely in that new dress,” she said. “But you’ve gone too thin, Caroline. What’s weighing on you?”

When Caroline told them she wanted to leave the rectory and work as a governess, Shirley was immediately horrified. “Nonsense!” she said. “You will not go be a servant amongst strangers, while I am here. What sort of friend do you think I am, to let you waste away all alone, unhappy somewhere far away? My greatest joy these days is looking out my window to see you walking up the lane, coming to spend the day with me. I won’t let you leave me. Instead, I have a far better plan. Every summer I take a long holiday. This year, I’m going to the English Lake District for two months. And you are coming with me. If you say no, I won’t go a single step without you.”

Caroline’s throat tightened, and she smiled through the ache in her chest. “You are so very good to me, Shirley.”

The first thought that drifted through Caroline Helstone’s sleepy head when the rectory rooster crowed her awake was soft, unarguable, and sharp as a shard of thin winter ice. Of course he will marry Shirley.

She sat up in her chintz-curtained bed, fingers twisting in the frayed linen of her nightgown, and breathed in the familiar scent of lavender sachets tucked under her pillow. It was true, and it was right, she told herself firmly. Shirley was wealthy enough to shore up all of Robert Moore’s struggling mill ambitions, clever enough to stand at his side against every hardship the hungry, restive workers of Briarfield threw his way. But the next thought cut deeper, unbidden: I shall be forgotten, the second they are wed.

Oh, she would be nothing to him then. Not his cousin, not the quiet girl who sat up half the night mending his torn cuffs when he came in soaked from a storm, not the one who knew every quirk of his grim, kind smile. She was no match for Shirley, after all: she had nothing but a small dowry, a quiet tongue, no grand fortune to offer. What was the point of lingering, playing the dear platonic friend, pretending she did not burn with a love hot enough to consume her when he was in the room? She would not stoop to that hypocrisy. She could not be half of anything to Robert Moore—she had to be all, or nothing at all. Sunder us then, Providence, she thought, pressing a hand to her aching chest. Part us speedily.

That sharp, heavy ache was still coiled tight under her ribs late that afternoon, when a familiar figure drifted past the parlour window. Shirley Keeldar wandered by slow, her indigo wool skirts swishing over the gravel path, that usual half-wistful, half-careless look on her face that Caroline had long since come to know better than her own reflection. When Shirley was animated, that distant wistfulness melted into a bright, warm gaiety no one else could muster, her laugh never harsh or sharp, always sweet with some quiet undercurrent of sentiment that made every moment in her company feel like a gift.

The parlour door opened, and Shirley stepped in, bonnet askew.
“What do you mean by skipping our afternoon walk, just as you promised?” she demanded, not angry, just amused, her eyes already sharp on Caroline’s drawn face.

“I wasn’t in the humour,” Caroline said, and it was no lie.

Shirley’s gaze held hers for a long moment. “No. I can see you’re not in the humour for loving me right now. You’re in one of those sunless, cold moods, where you’d rather no one at all bothered you. Do you even realize how quiet you get when you’re hurt, little wounded deer?” She did not wait for an answer, just untied her bonnet strings and tossed it onto the side table, making herself at home without being invited. “Do you think I won’t notice you’re bleeding, if you hide it from me?”

“I never feared you would hurt me, Shirley,” Caroline said softly.

“Then why do you avoid me half the time? I can feel when I’m being shunned, you know. You walked home last night with Moore, the two of you lingering so long over a single mile you didn’t get back to the rectory till ten. Which of you was dragging your feet, hmm?”

Caroline’s face flushed. “You talk nonsense, Shirley.”

“Of course he talked nonsense, or worse—looked nonsense, that soft, half-stern look he gets when he’s not guarding his face. I can see the reflection of his dark eyes on your forehead right now. I was half tempted to march out last night and challenge him to a duel, if I could find a trustworthy second. I’ve got pistols, you know, and I can use them.”

“Stuff and nonsense! Who would you have shot, me or Robert?” Caroline laughed, despite herself.

“Neither. Probably a bat, or a tree branch. Your cousin is a puppy, you know—quiet, serious, ambitious, sensible, but a puppy all the same. I cannot stand the way he keeps intruding between us. If it weren’t for him, we’d be the dearest friends in the world. But that six feet of stubborn man keeps eclipsing the friendship I want to keep bright and clear forever. You didn’t want my company this afternoon, and it stings. I’m tired of being nothing more than a nuisance to you, whenever he’s nearby.”

Caroline’s throat felt tight. She reached out, and caught Shirley’s cold hand in hers. “No. That’s not true. I like you, Shirley. More than like you. I was never the sort to rush into a loud, fiery friendship all at once, the way other girls do, but you’re nothing like any ordinary young lady. I esteem you. I value you. There’s no one else in the world I’d rather sit in silence with, no one else who makes me feel safe when my heart feels like it’s tearing in two. Love hurts so, Shirley. It burns you up from the inside out, racks you with pain you can never speak of. But this—this affection between us, soft, no fire, no torment—this is balm. This is sustenance. I never had a sister, but I think this is what it would feel like. Nothing could break it. Not even him.”

Shirley’s face softened, all that sharp edge melting away. She squeezed Caroline’s hand back. “All right, then. We really are friends, no matter how much that troublesome man tries to get between us?”

“Chance what may,” Caroline promised, tugging her down to sit on the sofa beside her.

They were just about to turn their talk to lighter things, when the rector, Mr. Helstone, stepped into the room, and the moment passed. Shirley delayed in the passage to say goodbye later, her voice quiet and heavy.
“Caroline, I’ve got a great weight on my mind. Not my private conscience, the stupid, over-serious conscience that comes with being a lady of the manor. There’s a stern, hard influence settling over this valley, something I don’t approve of but can’t quite fight. There’s going to be trouble soon, I fear—trouble that will hurt him, too. I’m going to try to head it off by good works. Don’t be surprised if you see me turn outrageously charitable all of a sudden. I don’t know the first thing about running relief schemes, so you’ll have to help me. We’ll ask that sweet, sensible Miss Ainley over to Fieldhead tomorrow. I’m putting myself under her tutelage. Drop her a hint that I’m a very ignorant pupil when it comes to clothing societies and soup kitchens, won’t you? She won’t be half so shocked at my stupidity then.”

The next morning, Caroline found Shirley hunched over her desk at Fieldhead, a pile of banknotes, a fat purse, and a household account book spread out in front of her, her expression so gravely serious it was almost comical.
“I tried to do the accounts,” she confessed, looking up, a little sheepish. “I lectured Mrs. Gill the cook on economy till I was practically eloquent—then I looked at the books, and I couldn’t bring myself to cut a single pound of butter, or ask where all the dripping went, or question the stacks of soap and candles that could keep half the parish going for a month. I couldn’t even hint to her that she’s been cheating me a little. I’m a coward, Caroline. Physical danger doesn’t faze me—last week I faced down Wynne’s great red bull in the cowslip lea without flinching—but I couldn’t bear to see Mrs. Gill humiliated. But the books add up. I’ve got three hundred pounds spare, and the poor of Briarfield are starving. I have to help them.”

“Don’t go throwing shillings out the door willy-nilly,” Caroline protested. “You’ll get taken advantage of. Miss Ainley is exactly the right person to run the scheme for you. The scruples of all those rich people who say charity degrades the poor are just nonsense to anyone who’s ever gone hungry. Life’s too short, and too bitter, to let people suffer when we have the means to help them.”

Shirley’s eyes flashed, suddenly sharp, fire under the soft surface. “I know. The poor are suffering now, and their anger is building. If that anger turns into a mob, if they try to burn down Robert’s mill, hurt him, I’ll turn on them like a tigress. I’ll defend what’s mine—what’s his—with every bit of strength I have. But I’m going to do everything I can to make sure it never comes to that. I know their hatred comes from pain. I’m going to ease that pain, before it turns to fire.”

Caroline’s own eyes glowed back, her hand tightening on Shirley’s arm. “If I had half your power to help him, I’d use every last bit of it to stand by his side, till the day I died.”

Off they went to fetch Miss Ainley, who was near to tears with joy when she arrived at Fieldhead, to be seated at the head of the table with paper, pen, ink, and the full three hundred pounds laid out before her. She knew every poor family in three parishes by name, knew exactly what each of them needed to get back on their feet, and she drew up a simple, brilliant relief plan in no time at all, already seeing the hungry fed, the naked clothed, the sick warmed by the money. She insisted they consult the three rectors of Briarfield, Whinbury, and Nunnely before they took a single step, for the clergy were sacred in her eyes, their station making them holy even when the curates were silly, arrogant boys barely worthy to carry her cotton umbrella.

Shirley laughed, and made one strict stipulation: the snot-nosed young curates were to have no hand in the money whatsoever, their meddling fingers were to stay far away from the charity pie. The rectors were sensible enough, could be trusted, but the boys were to be kept far from the till. It was only Caroline’s soft, gentle defence of poor sweet Mr. Sweeting—Miss Ainley’s favourite curate, who she always fed sponge cake and cowslip wine when he visited her cottage—that calmed the good lady’s horror at such a rule.

By that evening, Shirley had managed to gather all three senior clergymen at Fieldhead for a small, private council. She walked stubborn old Welsh Dr. Boultby and gruff Mr. Helstone around the rose gardens, tucking bunches of sweetbriar into their buttonholes, as warm and affectionate as a favourite daughter. The gentle Mr. Hall she left to Caroline, who slipped her arm through his, and led him slowly towards the parlour where Miss Ainley waited, all her papers spread out, the warm light of the fire gilding the banknotes laid out on the table. No one spoke of the dark clouds gathering on the horizon, of the restive workers, of the quiet, shared love the two young women bore the same man. For that one soft evening, they only spoke of good things, of bread for the hungry, of coats for the cold, of peace that might stretch across the valley, unbroken.

The morning after their small dinner party, Shirley leaned against the oak windowsill of the breakfast parlour, gushing to Caroline over how perfectly the evening had unfolded. "I do adore entertaining a room of gentlemen, don't you?" she laughed, sunlight gilding the ends of her dark curls. "It's so delightful to watch them light up over a properly roasted joint or a well-aged bottle of wine. For us, all those fancy dishes and rare vintages are little more than nice treats—but men carry that same sweet, childlike naivete about good food they never quite outgrow, don't they? And it's such a joy to please them, when they carry themselves with that gentle, decent decorum our good rectors do. I've been watching Robert Moore, though, trying to work out what small, trivial little thing might make him smile. He doesn't have that easy softness, that child's openness, at all. Have you ever found his weak spot, Caroline? You've spent far more time in his company than I have."

Caroline flushed, a shy, warm little thrill curling in her chest at the sound of his name—the subject she never dared bring up unprompted, but that she could talk about for hours if given leave. "It's certainly not the same as what charms my uncle or Dr. Boultby," she smiled back, her fingers plucking at the edge of her linen apron. "But I truly don't know what it is, either. Every time I try to watch him, to puzzle him out, I realize all at once he's already watching me, his eyes fixed right on my face."

"There you go!" Shirley exclaimed, clapping her hands. "He never lets his guard down, does he? He won't give you an inch of advantage. Even when he's not looking at you, you can feel his thoughts tracing yours, following every word you say, every move you make, picking apart your motives like he's sorting through spools of thread at his mill. Oh, that sort of character piques my curiosity to no end. Does it do the same for you?"

The question came so sharp and sudden Caroline almost floundered, but she'd long since learned to parry Shirley's quick, unexpected prods. "Pique you? What about him piques you, exactly?"

Shirley never got the chance to answer. Her head snapped toward the drive, and she jumped to her feet so fast her chair scraped across the floor. "Diversion! Right on cue. I never told you about the ridiculous conquest I've made at those parties you always refuse to come to with me, did I? I didn't even try for it, it just happened! There's the bell—wait, there are two of them. Do these curates never hunt for heiresses except in pairs? You get first pick of the pair, Lina. I'm feeling generous today. Listen to Tartar!"

The big tawny bulldog they both loved so much boomed out a bark from the hall, deep and resonant enough to shake the plaster dust off the rafters, followed by a growl so low and menacing it rumbled like distant thunder.

"Listen to him!" Shirley cackled, doubling over with laughter. "They'll think he's about to tear them to shreds. They don't know our old Tartar—all sound and fury, no bite at all, unless someone strikes him first."

Sure enough, the next sounds they heard were a high, imperious voice barking "Down, sir, down!" followed by the sharp crack of a cane hitting fur, then a yelp from the dog, a scuffle, a full-blown tumult.

Shirley bolted for the hall at once. One of the curates was already sprinting up the polished oak stairs, scrambling for the safety of the gallery bedrooms, the other backing away from Tartar, wildly waving a gnarled walking stick and yelling "Down! Down! Down!" at the top of his lungs, while a crowd of bewildered servants poured out of the kitchen. The dog lunged, and the second man turned tail and ran after his friend. The first curate slammed the bedroom door shut behind him, latching it tight, and the second threw his weight against the door screaming to be let in—terror was far less merciful than any dog, it seemed.

"Gentlemen," Shirley called out, her silver voice ringing clear over the chaos, sharp and amused, "pray do not break my locks. Calm yourselves, come down at once. Look at Tartar—he wouldn't hurt a stray cat, I promise."

She knelt to wrap her arms around the dog's thick neck, and he settled at her feet immediately, paws stretched out, tail still thumping a little in irritation, his small bulldog eyes glowering in the direction of the staircase. He was a good, honest, stubborn old thing, loved only Shirley and the man who fed him, didn't care for anyone else at all—until someone hit him, that was, and then a demon came right out.

"Mr. Malone, how do you do?" Shirley called up to the gallery, laughing up at the red-faced man leaning over the balustrade. "That corridor doesn't lead to our oak parlour, that's Mrs. Pryor's apartment. Do ask your friend Mr. Donne to vacate that bedroom, won't you? I should be delighted to receive you both downstairs."

Malone laughed, a hollow, embarrassed rattle, and started making his stately way down the stairs—only to slip on the polished oak, and catch himself on the banisters so hard the whole structure creaked. Tartar rumbled again, low in his throat, but Malone didn't flinch. He stalked past the dog, his face dark with suppressed rage, and shoved into the parlour ahead of Shirley, barely even nodding at Caroline, glowering like he wanted to clutch both ladies by the shoulders and shake them. But Shirley took pity on him, stopped laughing, and Caroline, too, was far too much of a lady to snicker at a man who was that mortified. She sent the servants away, had Tartar taken off to his kennel to calm down, and spoke to him with that soft, warm tone she had that could soothe even a raging bull. Malone was sharp enough to realize he couldn't very well challenge a woman over her own dog, so he forced himself to be polite, and before long he was back to his usual self, determined to be as charming as possible—come in like a lion, go out like a lamb, as the saying went.

He refused the soft armchair near the fire Shirley offered him, refused the spot on the sofa beside Caroline, and dragged a hard chair right up next to the door, where he could make a quick escape if another dog emergency cropped up. He sighed dramatically after every sentence he spoke, sighed in the pauses, sighed even before he opened his mouth to talk, until finally he pulled out a big red-and-yellow silk handkerchief to keep his hands busy. He folded it corner to corner, flapped it open, folded it again into a narrow band—and then, to both ladies' utter astonishment, he tied it tight around his own crossed ankles, winding it back and forth until his legs were bound together. He even did it twice, clearly very proud of his little trick. Shirley had to turn all the way to the window to muffle her silent, shaking laughter, and Caroline turned her head aside, letting her long curls hide the grin spreading across her cheeks. It was so obvious what he was doing, too—he'd stopped even pretending to court Caroline, who he thought only had a tiny five thousand pound inheritance, and had set all his sights straight on Shirley and her great estate at Fieldhead, no subtlety at all.

It took their manservant John nearly twenty minutes to talk Mr. Donne out of the locked bedroom, and when he finally wandered into the parlour, he wasn't even the slightest bit ashamed. Cold, phlegmatic, infinitely pleased with himself, he didn't have a scrap of modesty in his whole body, no fire in his blood at all. He fully believed all he had to do was visit Shirley a few more times, write her a formal marriage proposal, and she'd leap at the chance to marry a curate, be mistress of Fieldhead, wait on him hand and foot. He spoke to her in an injured, haughty tone the second he walked in. "That's a very dangerous dog you keep, Miss Keeldar. I can't imagine why you'd own such a brute. Ugly thing, just a common carter's hound. You ought to hang him, and get yourself a nice little poodle instead—ladies all love lapdogs, everyone knows that."

Caroline almost gasped, but Shirley only smiled, sharp and sweet. "Everyone but me, it seems. I'm very fond of Tartar, and I wouldn't part with him for anything."

Donne was just about to argue when Tartar trotted past the glass French doors to the garden, growling again. Then they heard a cheery, familiar voice call out to him, and old Mr. Hall and little Mr. Sweeting came through the gate, patting the big dog's head like he was a harmless old puppy. Tartar didn't even bark, just sniffed them both over, then wandered off to sun himself on the front steps. The two men had come by to tell Shirley and Caroline their Sunday school subscription drive had gone far better than expected that morning. Caroline ran straight to Mr. Hall, taking both his hands in hers, and the soft, gentle look he gave her was so warm it felt like wrapping up in a wool shawl.

No one wanted to stay inside on such a bright, breezy day, so they all wandered out into the garden, the air sharp with the scent of roses and clover, the wind tangling the girls' curls, their cheeks pinked with cold. Shirley gathered armfuls of the blooming flowers, gave Caroline a handful to make a nosegay for Mr. Hall, and the two of them remembered the upcoming Whitsuntide Sunday school procession and tea, the big combined feast for all three parishes. Mr. Hall teased Caroline about the first time she'd run the tea stand as a tiny twelve year old, trembling so bad her teacups rattled, and he'd sat beside her and helped her pour for an hour. "Someday I'll get to officiate at your wedding," he smiled, "but I'm going to vet the groom first, make sure he's good enough for the little girl who used to follow me all around the churchyard after Sunday school." Caroline laughed and said she'd never marry, she'd stay a spinster just like his sister Margaret, and he promised her if she ever needed a home, Nunnely Vicarage would always have a room waiting for her.

She handed him the nosegay of wild and garden flowers, and made him tuck a tiny blue forget-me-not into the pages of his pocket Testament, while she put the matching one in her little enamelled souvenir book, writing the date in pencil next to it: For the sake of my dearest friend, the Rev. Cyril Hall. Mr. Hall smiled, tucked the flower safe between his pages, and the sun dipped low over the cedar trees, the impromptu picnic of cold chicken and tarts laid out on the lawn long forgotten, all of them wrapped up in the soft, gentle quiet of the afternoon, the silly chaos of the curates' arrival already fading into a joke they'd laugh about for months.

---

The relief fund thrived. Fuelled by Shirley Keeldar’s fiery, generous example, the tireless efforts of the three parish rectors, and the quiet, unshakable diligence of their spectacled, spinster lieutenants Mary Ann Ainley and Margaret Hall, a substantial sum was soon raised. Judiciously distributed, it softened the sharp edge of want for the unemployed mill workers, and the whole neighbourhood seemed to breathe easier. For a full fortnight, no cloth had been slashed in the looms, no mill walls or manor gates had been marked by outrage across the three parishes. Shirley walked her lands with a light step, convinced the threat of storm had nearly passed. Summer was drawing close, and trade always picked up with the sun, did it not? That weary, dragging war could not rage forever. Peace would come, and when it did, it would send commerce surging forward like a river freed from ice.

This was the line she always pressed on her tenant, Gérard Moore, whenever they crossed paths somewhere private enough to talk. He listened so quiet, so still, that it never failed to fray her patience. She would dart him an impatient glance, demanding more than that measured silence — a word of agreement, an explanation, something. He would smile then, that soft, rare smile that lent his mouth a surprising sweetness even as his brow stayed set and grave, and answer that he too staked all his hopes on the war ending soon, that every one of his risky speculations was anchored to that belief.

“You know full well,” he told her, the low rumble of his voice holding a quiet edge of gratitude, “that Hollow’s Mill runs on nothing but speculation right now. I can sell nothing — there is no market for it. I weave, I card, I spin all to be ready for the first day the ports open again, the first breath of peace. Three months ago that would have been impossible. I’d run my credit and my capital dry. You know who came to my rescue, whose loan kept the mill doors from closing. It’s that money that lets me play this bold game now, the one I thought I’d never get to play again. I know if I lose I’m ruined, I know the win is far from certain — but I can’t be downhearted. Not while my hands are free to work, to strive. Give me six months of peace, and I am saved. You’re right about that, Shirley. But the quiet you’re counting on? The permanent good your fund will do? I doubt it. Charity never soothed working men’s resentment. It never makes them grateful — it makes them feel humiliated, and who can blame them? They don’t thank us mill owners for it, either. They hate us worse than ever. The disaffected here are in constant correspondence with groups in Nottingham, Manchester, Birmingham. They take orders, they wait, they plan, and no strike is struck without weeks of deliberation. You’ve seen the sultry summer days where thunder threatens for a week straight, and each night the clouds clear and the sun sets soft. But the storm is never gone. It’s just delayed. And it will break.”

“Mr. Moore,” she would say, every one of those conversations ending the same way, “take care of yourself. If you ever owe me any kindness, pay me back by promising to watch over your own safety.”

“I promise. I will guard myself close. I want to live, not die. The future unfurls ahead of me like Eden — and when I look deep into the green shade of that paradise, I see a vision more dear to me than any angel, gliding far off among the trees.”

“Do you? What vision, then?”

“I see —”

The parlour maid bustled in then, arms heaped with tea things, and the soft, charged moment broke.

Early May had been fine, the middle of the month damp with constant rain, but the last week, as the moon turned, the skies cleared all at once. A sharp, sweet wind swept the heavy silver rain clouds east, piling them along the horizon until they vanished behind its rim, leaving nothing but pure, unbroken blue for the summer sun to rule over. That sun rose broad and golden on Whitsuntide morning, just in time for the grand annual gathering of the three parishes’ Sunday schools.

Whit-Tuesday was the big day. The two large Briarfield schoolrooms, built almost entirely at the rector’s own expense, had been scrubbed, whitewashed, repainted, strung with flowers and evergreens — armfuls from the rectory garden, two full cartloads from Fieldhead, even a grudging wheelbarrow’s worth from Mr. Wynne’s stingy De Walden estate. Twenty tables, each set to seat twenty guests, were draped in crisp white linen, and twenty canary cages hung from the rafters, their bright little occupants trilling loud over the hum of voices, a fancy of Mr. Helstone’s clerk who swore the birds sang loudest in the middle of a crowd. These tables were not for the twelve hundred children coming to the feast — the children’s meal would be spread out on the grass under the sun. The seats indoors were reserved for patrons and teachers. By one o’clock the children would march in, by two they would be formed into order, they would parade the parish roads until four, then feast, then gather in the church for speeches and music.

Briarfield had been chosen for the fête for no grander reason than that Mr. Helstone willed it so, and no one dared argue with him. Whinbury was far more populous, Nunnely far older, but neither of the other two rectors had half his stubborn, imperious will. They let him lead.

For years, this holiday had been a torture for Caroline Helstone. Shy, nervous of crowds, she was forced to march at the head of the Briarfield school contingent as the rector’s niece, run the tea service for all the neighbourhood’s most wealthy and respected families, all without a mother, aunt, or chaperone by her side to steady her. The very thought of Whitsuntide used to make her tremble.

But this year, Shirley was to be at her side, and that changed everything. The trial turned into a treat. Shirley Keeldar, bright, self-possessed, never awkward, never presumptuous, was worth a dozen ordinary friends. The only worry Caroline had was that the heiress would be late — Shirley had a habit of losing track of time when a book caught her eye, and Caroline’s uncle would not wait a single second for anyone. The second the church clock struck two, the procession would begin.

She rose almost with the sun that Tuesday. She, Fanny, and Eliza bustled all morning, arranging the rectory parlours to perfection, laying out a spread of cold cakes, ripe fruit, and spiced wine on the dining room sideboard. Then she dressed in her freshest white muslin, tied the new blue sash Margaret Hall had given her for her birthday — the sash she was quietly sure Mr. Cyril Hall had bought himself, the one she had repaid with a fine set of cambric bands in a handsome leather case. Her simple bonnet was trimmed to match, her soft white crape scarf falling light over her shoulders. She looked not dazzling, but tender, sweet, with the quiet grace of a wild rose, all soft pink and white and gentle light, her brown eyes clear and her forehead smooth, modest, pensive, and perfectly at peace.

Off she went then, slipping through hidden green lanes and quiet hedgerow paths across to Fieldhead. The rain had left the ground soft and clean, no dust to stain the hem of her white gown, no mud to dampen her thin sandals. She walked fast across the daisy-sprinkled turf, reached the house, and went straight up to Shirley’s dressing room.

It was a good thing she came. Shirley was sprawled across a couch, completely absorbed in a book, paying no mind at all to Mrs. Pryor’s useless urging that she get dressed. Caroline wasted no words. She took the book right out of Shirley’s hands, and set to dressing her herself, lacing stays, fastening hooks, adjusting frills as fast as her fingers could move. Shirley, warm from the May heat, giddy and playful, kept trying to stop to laugh and chatter, but Caroline chided her gently for being so careless, and finished tying the last ribbon just in time.

Shirley made a striking contrast to Caroline. Every fold of her rich silk gown, every line of her straight, tall figure, had a style all its own. Her deep embroidered scarf hung negligently over one shoulder, the wreath of wild roses on her bonnet sitting perfectly atop her curls. She looked like a bright bird of paradise, next to Caroline’s soft white dove, and the two of them ran hand in hand down the Fieldhead drive, laughing as they hurried back towards Briarfield.

They arrived just in time. Over the treetops they heard the church bell tolling, calling everyone to gather, and the distant sound of hundreds of footsteps, and the low murmur of voices. From a rise in the road they saw the five hundred strong Whinbury school procession coming towards them, led by Dr. Boultby in his full canonicals, his shovel hat perched firmly on his head, his gold-headed cane swinging in his hand, and his curate Mr. Donne trailing after him, prim, self-satisfied, every bit the pedantic young clergyman from his turned-up nose down to his square-toed clerical shoes.

The two girls ran down the hill to join the crowd. The churchyard was packed with children all dressed in their very best, and even in the hard times, every little frock was neat, every bonnet tidy — the English love of decency, and Shirley’s quiet, timely bounty, had seen to that. She had given out new frocks and new boots to dozens of families in the weeks leading up to the holiday, and she glowed with quiet pleasure to see the crowd happy and well-dressed. Caroline was happy too: she had given away more than one of her own favourite ribbons and collars to the girls in her class, sewn late by candlelight to finish new frocks for the poorest children.

The rectory garden was full too, groups of patrons walking among the lilacs and laburnums that hung heavy with yellow blossom. Behind the house, the three parish bands were tuning their instruments, and the maids Fanny and Eliza were handing out quart tankards of the strong ale Mr. Helstone had brewed especially for the occasion — he would tolerate no shabby, half-hearted doings, and Shirley heartily approved of his generous, uncompromising taste.

Caroline, who usually hid in corners at public gatherings, moved easily through the parlours, talking, smiling, even speaking first to people before they spoke to her. Shirley’s easy, unafraid company had transformed her. Shirley herself was the centre of a laughing circle on the sofa, and when Mr. Donne came in, she bowed and smiled at him good-naturedly, ready to forget their old quarrel. But he slunk past her with a sullen, sheepish scowl, too cowardly to make peace and too petty to let the grudge go.

“He’s not even worth a proper quarrel,” Shirley whispered to Caroline. “Crushing him would be like squashing a gnat for stinging a rhinoceros. Malone is far better sport.”

As if on cue, Peter Augustus Malone sauntered over, gloved, scented, his hair oiled and brushed to a shine, holding out a huge bunch of full-blown cabbage roses to her. He presented them with a grand, dramatic flourish, then backed away, so proud of his own gallantry he was chuckling to himself — until he caught sight of the wide, laughing grins on both Shirley and Caroline’s faces. His face turned dark as a thundercloud, angry that his grand romantic gesture had turned into a joke.

“Peter is absolutely worth a quarrel,” Shirley murmured to her friend. “I’ll give him one someday, if he keeps glowering at me like that.”

Just then the three rectors appeared at the dining room door, their black clerical coats solemn against the bright flowers. The big morocco easy chair had been saved for Dr. Boultby, who sank into it happily, surrounded by his adoring congregation, his wife dabbing imaginary sweat from his forehead, Caroline handing him a plate of macaroons and a glass of spiced wine. Mr. Hall stood by the open window, breathing in the scent of lilacs, talking gently to Mary Ann Ainley, and Caroline and Shirley and Margaret Hall clustered around him, all four of them regarding him with the soft, reverent affection he so deserved. The third rector, old Helstone, had a whole crowd of admirers pressing around him, and the three curates huddled together in a little group of their own, watched from afar by a cluster of giggling young ladies.

Mr. Helstone pulled his watch out of his pocket, and held it high for everyone to see.

“Ten minutes to two,” he announced loud and clear. “Line up, everyone. Let’s go.”

He seized his shovel hat and marched out, and the whole crowd followed on his heels. The twelve hundred children were drawn up in three regiments of four hundred each, a band at the back of every group, teachers posted in pairs every twenty paces. Mr. Helstone called out the order for the vanguard:

“Grace Boultby and Mary Sykes lead Whinbury.
Margaret Hall and Mary Ann Ainley lead Nunnely.
Caroline Helstone and Shirley Keeldar head Briarfield.”

This procession, led by the rector and shepherded by sharp-eyed village women, had not set out spoiling for a fight or hunting down enemies, yet the brass band at its head blared bold, martial tunes. For some of their number, most notably Shirley Keeldar, those rousing notes lit a wild, bright spark under their skin, kindling a longing that had no place in their quiet, unthreatened countryside. Old Helstone chanced to turn, caught the glint in her dark eyes, and laughed outright. Shirley laughed back, unashamed.

“There’s no battle waiting for us today, lass,” he told her, shaking his head. “Our land doesn’t need us to take up arms. No tyrant threatens our liberty. We’re nothing more than a group out for a walk. Rein in that fiery spirit of yours, Captain—there’s no call for it, more’s the pity.”

“Might I suggest you take your own advice, Doctor?” Shirley shot back, grinning, before leaning in to murmur to Caroline at her side, “If reality won’t give me a grand cause, I’ll steal one from imagination, shall I? We’re not soldiers, heaven knows I’ve no taste for bloodshed—if we carry a banner, it’s the banner of the Cross. Let’s say it’s hundreds of years back, and we’re Scots Covenanters trekking up the hills, sneaking past persecuting troopers to hold our prayer meeting. We know the fight might come right after the hymns, but we’d stain the peat bogs with our blood before we’d give up our faith. That music wakes every sleeping part of me, Caroline—makes my heart beat so fast it’s like it learned a whole new rhythm. I almost wish for danger, something worth fighting for: a cause, a home, or… or even a man to defend.”

“Shirley, look!” Caroline cut in, pointing to the distant rise of Stilbro’ Brow. “There’s a little red speck up there. You’ve sharper eyes than me—what is it?”

Shirley squinted, then her brows lifted. “More than one. It’s a whole line of red. Cavalry, six of them, riding hard. Wait—they just swerved right. They spotted our procession, and they’re circling round to avoid us. Off on some ride of theirs, I suppose.”

Mr. Helstone called out that they’d be taking Royd Lane as a shortcut to Nunnely Common, and the whole party filed into the narrow, sun-dappled lane, barely wide enough for two to walk abreast without tumbling straight into the muddy ditches on either side. Halfway down the path, the rector and Mr. Boultby both jolted to attention, the young curates nudged each other snickering, and gentle Mr. Hall turned to the ladies with an amused little smile.

“What on earth is it?” someone asked.

He pointed with his walking stick to the far end of the lane. Another procession, just as long, led by a line of black-coated Dissenting ministers, with their own band tramping behind them, had stepped into the lane at the exact same moment.

“Our doppelgänger?” Shirley laughed. “Looks like we’ve drawn a very unexpected card.”

“Looks like you’re about to get your wish for a battle, at least of the sharp-staring variety,” Caroline whispered back, giggling.

“We won’t give way an inch!” the curates all yelped at once.

“Give way?” Helstone rounded on them, sharp as a tack. “Who said anything about giving way? Boys, hold your tongues and stand straight. The ladies I know will stand firm for the honour of the Established Church. Miss Keeldar, what say you?”

“I say they’ve got abominable manners, cutting us off like this,” Shirley said, her eyes glinting. “And I hate bad manners. Of course they deserve a lesson.”

“A lesson in politeness, not rudeness,” Mr. Hall hurried to interject, ever the peacemaker.

But Helstone was already striding ahead, marching yards in front of his group, before the leader of the opposing party — a large, plump spirit merchant who was the most prominent Nonconformist in the district — called his own procession to a halt. He pulled out a hymn book, boomed out a verse, and his whole crowd started singing the slowest, dreariest canticle ever heard.

Helstone snapped his fingers at the band. The brass blared so loud the leaves on the hedgerows shook, and he called for *Rule Britannia*, waving the children to join in at the top of their lungs. The Dissenters’ hymn was drowned out completely, their voices swallowed up in the roar. Noise-wise, the church party had won handily.

“Now follow me!” Helstone called. “No running, just a steady, firm pace. Stay close, hold onto each other’s skirts if you have to.”

He strode forward with such unshakable purpose, the whole crowd behind him marching cool and solid as a wall, that the Dissenters first gaped, then panicked, then got pushed steadily backwards until they had no choice but to turn tail and scramble out of the far end of the lane. The plump hymn-singing merchant ended up sat right in the ditch, so covered in mud it was said he drank more water that afternoon than he had in the entire prior year. By the time the whole procession had cleared the lane, Shirley and Helstone were shaking hands, grinning like co-conspirators, and even meek Mr. Hall was smiling at the absurdity of it all.

By four o’clock, the whole party was back at the school. Long lines of benches were set out in the freshly mown surrounding fields, stacked with plump, jam-oozing currant buns and great steaming tins of sweet, hot tea. No one was stinting that day: every child got twice as much as they could possibly eat, the extra wrapped up to carry home for siblings or grandparents too sick or too old to make it to the feast. The musicians and singers got their own share of buns and beer, and once the benches were cleared, the kids ran wild playing games across the grass.

A bell called the patrons and ladies back inside the schoolroom. The white walls were draped in evergreens and wild roses, the tables laid out with polished silver teapots and bright painted porcelain, and the room hummed with soft, merry chatter, canaries trilling in their high-hung cages by the windows. Caroline took her place serving at the main front table, her long curls falling loose down her back, her modest muslin dress cool against her skin in the warmth.

Shirley sat just beside her, uncharacteristically quiet, her gaze darting to the door every few seconds. She kept spreading her satin skirt out across the empty spot next to her, tucking her gloves and embroidered handkerchief down on the bench to mark the space. When Caroline asked who she was waiting for, Shirley leaned in, her rosy lips almost brushing Caroline’s ear, her voice soft and honeyed with that private, tender thrill only Caroline had ever heard from her.

“I’m waiting for Mr. Moore. I saw him last night, made him promise to come with his sister Hortense and sit right here with us. He won’t break his word, I know he won’t — but if he’s late, all the seats will be taken before he gets here.”

The words had barely left her mouth when Squire Wynne the magistrate, his wife, and his two children filed into the room in all their finery, and his oafish son Sam plopped himself down right on top of Shirley’s gloves, her handkerchief, and the very spot she’d been saving. Sam had been making no secret of his intentions to court Shirley, and his father had even made a public fool of himself saying it would be such a grand match to combine the Fieldhead and De Walden estates. Shirley’s jaw tightened, and she shot Caroline a look that said she was plotting murder.

When Caroline finally got a chance to glance around the room once she’d finished serving the first round of tea, she spotted Hortense Moore in her familiar yellow bonnet and black silk dress, standing by the wall with quiet old Miss Mann. She half rose to run over and hug her old teacher, when a hand rested light on her shoulder, and a low, familiar voice whispered behind her ear: “Wait till after tea, Lina. I’ll bring her to you.”

Caroline looked up, and her breath caught. Robert Moore was standing right behind her, smiling at her eagerness, looking handsomer than she had ever seen him, his face lit with a warm, soft light she’d never noticed before — so bright it was like lightning had etched his image straight into her memory, sharp and vivid and impossible to look away from for long.

He moved to Shirley’s side, and she snapped at him immediately for being so tardy, cross that Sam Wynne had planted himself in her reserved spot. Moore said nothing, just stood there quiet and patient, until Shirley’s temper melted three minutes later, and she held out her hand to him. He took it, a tiny, amused shake of his head teasing her for her pettishness, his palm pressing gentle and warm against hers.

“There’s no spot left for you here,” Shirley told him, still half grumpy. “You’ll have to go sit over at Mrs. Boultby’s table, all the way across the room.”

Moore didn’t go. He lingered, wandering the room to greet other gentlemen who’d also been left standing without seats, but he kept drifting back to Shirley’s side, murmuring little observations that made her lips twitch despite herself.

Poor Sam Wynne, stuck next to her, soon found he could not be more miserable. Shirley fanned herself wildly, complained the air was stifling, announced she was about to faint from lack of space. When he offered to walk her outside, she snapped that he’d give her her death of cold. He swallowed his last sip of tea, and practically ran for the door.

The stout, wealthy corn factor who stepped forward to take the empty seat next was even less lucky. Shirley made a great sweep with her scarf, knocked her full teacup straight over, spilling tea all over her satin dress and the bench, and raised such a fuss calling for napkins and waiters that the bewildered man backed away, beat a hasty retreat, and did not dare come near the spot again.

Moore wandered back just in time, surveyed the chaos with that quiet half-smile of his, and sat down in the newly empty spot like it had been made exactly for him. Shirley’s scowl faded at once, all her sharp, peevish lines softening, and she turned to grumble that he’d put her to all that trouble, that now she’d have to go apologize to Sam Wynne and the corn factor tomorrow to make up for it. He only laughed low, quiet and calm, the easy equality of his bearing making him look far nobler than any wealthy landowner in the room, no trace of the poor struggling mill master to be seen in his steady, relaxed posture. Their heads bent close, murmuring to each other, and Shirley’s face softened, glowing, as if every word he said was a secret only the two of them got to hear.

Caroline grew faint from the stuffy warm air, and slipped outside soon after, finding Hortense Moore waiting for her. Her old teacher was stiff and formal at first, still cross with Mr. Helstone for separating Caroline from the Moores, but the second she saw how thin and pale Caroline had grown, her face softened, and she kissed her on both cheeks, fretting over her health. Soon after, Hortense left to walk the tired Miss Mann home, and Caroline was left standing alone, as the group of young village girls she’d been talking to were chased off to play games by the over-eager Sam Wynne.

She was just about to slip back into the quiet of the schoolhouse when Shirley spotted her from across the lawn, and came hurrying over to her side, linking their arms together.

“Let’s walk up to the top of the highest field,” Shirley said softly. “I know you hate crowds, Caroline.”

“Oh, but you don’t have to leave all those people fawning over you just to keep me company,” Caroline protested.

Shirley only smiled, and squeezed her arm tighter, the sound of the feast’s music and laughter fading soft behind them as they started up the sunlit path.

## (Genteel readers are hereby politely encouraged to skip this one, for folks of humbler station step front and center here.)

The evening hung soft and warm, thick with the sultry hum of coming summer heat. Purple glows girdled the sinking sun, those rich, hazy tints of midsummer that feel far more tropical than English, spilling rose-gold light over the sloping hills, gabled cottage fronts, gnarled oak trunks, winding lanes, and rolling pastureland stretching as far as the eye could see. Caroline and Shirley drifted slowly down from the fields, and by the time they reached the lychgate of the churchyard, the last of the bells had fallen silent, the last straggler had slipped through the heavy oak doors, and the whole quiet scene was theirs alone.

“Isn’t it perfectly peaceful?” Caroline breathed.

“Far pleasanter than sweltering inside that packed church,” Shirley laughed. “You know Dr. Boultby will drag on his sermon for an hour and a half, full of fire brimstone about schism, and the curates will hammer their pre-written speeches like they’re nailing planks to a new shed. I for one haven’t the stomach for it.”

“But your uncle will be cross if he notices we’re missing,” Caroline fretted.

“Let me bear the brunt of his temper—he won’t eat me alive. I’ll even miss his sharp little jabs about the Royd Lane battle, and I’ll be sorry you miss Mr. Hall’s warm, sincere homily full of his funny, hearty Yorkshire turns of phrase, but I can’t go in. Look at it: the gray stone church, the grayer old headstones, bathed in this crimson glow—they look like something out of a fairy tale. Nature’s saying her evening prayers right now, kneeling before those red-hued hills. I can almost see her bowed there on the wide stone steps of her altar, praying for calm seas for the sailors out on the ocean, for safe passage for travelers crossing the desert, for the lambs huddled on the moors and the baby birds still unfledged in their nests deep in the woods. She looks exactly like Eve must have looked when she and Adam were the only two people alive on the whole earth.”

“That’s not Milton’s Eve you’re describing, Shirley.”

“Milton’s Eve? Pfft! No, by all that’s holy, she isn’t! Cary, we’re alone here—we can say exactly what we think. Milton was a genius, yes, but was he good? His brain was sharp enough, but what of his heart? He saw heaven, he peered down into hell, he watched Satan and Sin and Death march past him, he saw the ranks of angels with their adamantine shields glinting so bright they seared his blind eyes, he saw the armies of fallen devils trudge by. But when he tried to picture the first woman? He didn’t see her at all.”

“You’re bold to say that,” Caroline chided.

“Bold only to be honest. What he saw was his own cook, or Mrs. Gill fussing over custards on a hot summer day in the cool dairy, rose and nasturtium vines climbing the latticed window, laying out cold treats for the rectors—jams and sweet creams, fussing over what tastes to mix so none of them clash. That’s not Eve.”

“All very charming, that vision, Shirley.”

“Let me remind you: the first people on earth were Titans, and Eve was their mother. From her came Saturn, Hyperion, Oceanus—she was the one who bore Prometheus, who dared steal fire from the heavens. The first woman’s breast that ever heaved with life held the daring to stand against Omnipotence, the strength to bear a thousand years of bondage, the life force that fed that vulture Death for uncounted ages. She was heaven-born. Vast was the heart that spilled the blood of all nations, grand was the unspoiled head that wore the consort’s crown of all creation.”

“But she ate an apple, tricked by a snake!” Caroline protested. “You’ve mixed Scripture and pagan myth all up in your head so no one can make sense of it. You haven’t even told me what that kneeling woman on the hills actually looks like.”

“I see her right now, clear as day: a woman Titan. Her robe of blue air stretches all the way to the edge of the heath, where that flock of sheep is grazing. A veil white as an avalanche falls from her head to her feet, with little flashes of lightning dancing along its borders. Her girdle, wrapped tight under her breast, is purple like that sunset horizon, and the evening star glows right through its rose blush. I can’t even draw her eyes: they’re clear as deep lakes, lifted up in worship, trembling with soft love and the bright light of prayer. Her forehead stretches wide as a summer cloud, paler than the early moon that rose hours before dark. She rests her elbow on the ridge of Stilbro’ Moor, her great hands clasped tight beneath her, kneeling face to face, talking straight to God. That Eve is Jehovah’s daughter, just as Adam was His son.”

“She’s all too vague and dreamy. Come on, Shirley, we really ought to go into church now.”

“I won’t. I’m staying out here with my mother Eve, the one everyone now calls Nature. I love her, that great undying being. Heaven may have faded from her brow when she fell in Paradise, but every glorious thing on earth still shines there. She’s pulling me close to her bosom now, letting me see her heart. Hush, Cary—be quiet a little, and you’ll see her, feel what I feel too.”

“Fine, I’ll humor your whim,” Caroline laughed. “But you’ll be chattering away again before ten minutes are up.”

Shirley leaned against an old upright headstone, caught in that soft warm thrall of the summer evening, eyes fixed on the burning west, drifting off into a pleasant trance. Caroline wandered a little way off, pacing beneath the high rectory garden wall, lost in her own daydreams. Shirley had said the word mother, and the word didn’t summon any grand mystical Titan figure for her—only a soft, gentle human face: the face of the mother she’d never known, the mother she’d ached for her whole life.
*Oh, if only the day would come when she remembers her little girl! If only I could meet her, and love her, and she would love me back.*
The old childish longing that had kept her awake night after night as a small girl, that she’d almost buried for fear it would never come true, flared up warm and bright in her chest: her mother would come one happy day, send for her, look at her with soft loving eyes, and say, *Caroline, my child, I have a home for you. All the love you never got as a little girl, I’ve saved it all for you. Come, and I’ll cherish you.*

The sound of horses’ hooves jolted Caroline out of her daydream, and Shirley out of her vision of the Titan Eve. They listened, and glimpsed a flash of bright scarlet through the oak leaves: six red-coated soldiers rode past quiet as shadows, helmets glinting, plumes bobbing, keeping as low a profile as possible when the whole village was packed inside church.
“It’s the same patrol we saw this afternoon,” Shirley whispered. “They’ve been hiding somewhere, waiting for everyone to go inside to sneak to their meeting spot. Didn’t I tell you we’d start seeing strange things soon?”

Before the last clop of hooves faded down the road, a new noise broke the hush: a small child’s squall, loud and impatient. A man stepped out of the church porch, holding a sturdy, red-faced two-year-old boy in his arms, who was roaring at the top of his lungs, fresh from waking up mid-sermon curled on the pew bench behind his father. Two little girls of nine and ten trundled after him. The fresh cool air and a handful of wild flowers he’d plucked off a grave soon quieted the baby, and the man sat down on the edge of a flat headstone, dandling him on his knee as tenderly as any doting mother, the two little girls perching one on each side.

“Good evening, William,” Shirley said, when she’d recognized him through the dusk. He tipped his hat to them, grinning a pleased smile: it was their old friend William Farren, the gardener, rough-headed, weather-beaten, not old but lined hard by years of work, his clothes clean and decent, his children dressed far neater than most of the village youngsters.

“You two aren’t coming back into church, then?” he asked, gazing at them with a bashful warmth, not out of awe for their higher station, but just because he thought they were kind, young, and lovely. With stuck-up gentry or rude bosses he was stubborn as a stone, but Shirley’s frank, friendly hospitality at Fieldhead had won him over completely, and Caroline he’d known since she was a small child trotting around the village, her soft gentle ways something he loved to look at the same way he loved tending rare roses in his garden. The two girls liked William just as well: they lent him books, gave him cuttings of rare plants, and his conversation was far more interesting to them than half the tedious, pretentious rich folks around who thought they were far above him.

“Who was preaching when you came out, William?” Shirley asked.
“Somebody you don’t care for much, Miss Shirley—Mr. Donne, the curate.”
“You look like you know a secret, William. How’d you guess I don’t like him?”
“There’s a sharp little light in your eyes sometimes that gives you away, miss—you look fair scornful every time he walks in the room.”
“Do you like him yourself, then?”
“Me? I’m sick to death of all the curates, me and my wife both. They’ve no manners at all, talk to poor folks like we’re dirt under their boots, strutting about acting like their little job makes them better than everyone else. I can’t stand that kind of nasty pride.”
“But you’re proud in your own way, aren’t you?” Caroline put in. “You’re proud of your house, keeping everything neat and nice. When you were out of work a few years back, you were too proud to buy anything on credit, I reckon you’d have starved before you went to the parish for help. And do you remember how hard I had to beg you to take that five shillings I tried to give you last winter?”
“Aye, that’s true enough, Miss Caroline. I’d far rather give than take, especially from the likes of you two. You’re a tiny little lass, not even half my age, and I’m a great strong man in the prime of my life. It’s not right that I should be taking favors from you, right? That day you turned up at my door with that five shillings, which I know you could barely afford to spare, I was fair furious. It felt so shameful that I was strong and willing to work, but I was so poor a girl no older than my own daughter thought she had to give me charity. But I forgave you right quick, you meant no harm. That’s clean pride, the Yorkshire sort, the kind those curates know nothing about. Their pride is mucky, ours is honest. I’m raising my girls to be as proud as Miss Shirley, my lads to be as proud as me, and if I ever caught any of them acting as stuck-up as those curates, I’d tan their hide good.”
“What’s the difference between our clean pride and theirs, William?” Shirley asked.
“You know full well, you just want me to run my mouth. Those curates are too proud to ever do a single little thing for themselves, but we’re too proud to ever take a rude word from anyone who thinks they’re better than us.”
“Now tell me the truth, William—are you managing alright these days?”
“Couldn’t be better, Miss Shirley. Ever since Mr. Yorke helped me get that gardening job, and Mr. Hall helped my wife set up that little shop selling bread and sweets, me and mine have plenty to eat, plenty to wear, I’ve got a little money put aside for rainy days, I’d never go crawling to the parish. But the rest of the village? They’re starving. There’s terrible distress out there.”
“Which means there’s discontent, too, I suppose?” Shirley said.
“Exactly. You can’t expect a man who’s gone three days without bread to be calm and content. This whole county’s in a dangerous state, mark my words.”
“What can be done? What more can I do, for example?”
“You can’t do much, poor lass—you’ve given all the money you can spare, that’s more than most rich folks do. Though if you could ship your tenant Mr. Moore off to Botany Bay, that’d make folks a whole lot happier. Everyone hates him.”
“William, that’s a terrible thing to say!” Caroline burst out, warm and indignant. “If people hate him, it’s their fault, not his. He doesn’t hate anyone! He’s just trying to do his duty, keep his mill running, that’s all. You’re wrong to talk like that.”
“I say what I see. That Moore’s got a cold, stone heart.”
“But if you drove him out of the country, burned his mill to the ground,” Shirley cut in, “would that give everyone more work?”
“Less, that’s the truth, I know it, everyone knows it. But there’s hundreds of honest lads so desperate they don’t care what happens, and there’s scoundrels everywhere calling themselves the People’s Friends, that don’t care a jot about the poor, just want to use them as tools to cause trouble. I’ve lived over forty years in this world, and I know real friends of the working man are few and far between. Any rich man who’s nice to you just for political reasons? He’s not to be trusted. I’ve had a few of those offers lately, and I told them where to stick it straight to their faces. No one’s going to make a fool out of me.”

“Aye, and we’ll look out for ourselves too,” a new voice chimed in. Joe Scott, the hard-headed mill overlooker, had sauntered out of the church to get some fresh air, and was standing there dusting off his coat.
“See that you do, Joe,” William laughed.
“And I’ll look out for my master too,” Joe huffed, puffing out his chest, and turned to the two girls. “Young ladies, you’d best go back inside the church right now.”
“Why ever for?” Shirley asked, who was long used to his grumpy, overbearing manners. Joe had very firm opinions that women ought to have nothing to do with mill business, and secretly resented that the master’s property was half under Shirley’s control, that the heiress of Fieldhead could march into the counting house whenever she pleased.
“Because there’s talk going on that’s no fit business for women to be mixed up in. Politics, namely.”
“Politics are our usual pastime, Joe,” Shirley grinned. “I read a newspaper every single day, two on Sundays.”
“I reckon you only read the bits about weddings, murders, and traffic accidents, don’t you?” Joe sniffed.
“I read the leading articles, the foreign news, I even glance over the market prices—all the same stuff the gentlemen read.”
Joe looked at her like she was a chattering magpie, and went silent with disdain.
“Come on, Joe,” Shirley teased. “I can never work out if you’re a Whig or a Tory. Which side are you on?”
“Hard to explain to someone who won’t understand,” he said haughtily. “But I’d as soon be a silly old woman as a Tory. Those Tories are the ones running this stupid war and ruining all our trade. If I’m any party at all, I’m for the side that wants peace, that’s good for the mills and the workers.”
“Well, I’m for peace too, mostly,” Shirley said, enjoying riling him up. “Though I’ve got a soft spot for the farmers too, seeing as most of my income comes from the land around the mill, not the mill itself. We can’t pass laws that hurt the farmers, right?”
“The night dews are bad for women’s health,” Joe said, dodging the point.
“If that’s your excuse to get rid of me, don’t bother. I’m as tough as old boots. I wouldn’t mind taking a turn patrolling the mill some summer night, armed with your musket.”
Joe’s already prominent chin jutted out a good two inches further.
“Back to what I was saying,” Shirley went on, “even as a mill owner, I can’t help thinking that us business folks are a little bit selfish, a little short-sighted, too busy chasing profit to care about the suffering of the people who work for us. Don’t you agree?”
“I won’t argue with someone who can’t possibly understand,” he snapped.
“Your master argues with me about it all the time, Joe. He’s not as stubborn as you are.”
“We’ve all got our ways,” Joe shrugged.
“Do you seriously think all the wisdom in the whole world is locked up in men’s skulls?”
“I think women are a flighty, stubborn generation. And I follow the good doctrine in the second chapter of Paul’s first letter to Timothy.”
“What doctrine, Joe?”
“‘Let the woman learn in silence, with all subjection. I suffer not a woman to teach, nor to usurp authority over the man, but to be in silence. For Adam was first formed, then Eve.’”
“That’s just primogeniture nonsense!” Shirley cried. “I’m going to quote that to Mr. Yorke next time he rants against inherited privileges.”
“And Adam wasn’t the one tricked by the snake,” Joe continued. “The woman was the one who sinned first.”
“More shame to Adam for sinning with his eyes wide open!” Shirley laughed. “I’ve never been able to make sense of that chapter, for the life of me.”
“It’s plain as day, miss. Anyone can read it.”
“People can read whatever they want into it, though,” Caroline put in. “You believe in the right of private judgment, don’t you, Joe?”
“Course I do—for every line of the Bible.”
“Then why can’t women exercise it the same as men?”
