“Nah. Women ought to just take their husband’s opinion for politics and religion. It’s healthier for them.”
“Oh, rubbish!” Shirley and Caroline both exclaimed at the same time.

Dusk draped the Briarfield lanes soft as gossamer, and the crisp, clean midsummer air seemed to coax the first faint points of starlight out to glimmer across the wide, cloudless sky.
“There’s just enough glow left to light my path all the way home,” Shirley Keeldar said, pausing at the rectory garden gate where Caroline Helstone lingered to see her off.
“You cannot go wandering alone after dark, Shirley. I’ll send Fanny to walk you back to Fieldhead,” Caroline protested, her fingers brushing the sleeve of Shirley’s light muslin gown.
“That you will not. What on earth have I to fear in my own parish?” Shirley laughed, her bright voice carrying on the warm breeze. “I’d stroll the three miles from Fieldhead to the church at three hours past midnight on a fine midsummer evening, just to watch the stars wheel and catch a glimpse of a fairy dancing in the fern fronds at the lane edge.”
“At least wait until all the crowds from the service have scattered,” Caroline urged.
“Fair enough. Look, the five Misses Armitage are drifting by now, gossiping arm in arm. There’s Mrs. Sykes’s phaeton trotting past, Mr. Wynne’s closed carriage, Mrs. Birtwhistle’s little cart. I’ve no desire to go through the tedious formality of waving goodbye to every last one of them, so let’s duck back into the garden for a minute and hide under the laburnum boughs.”
The rector, his two curates, and the churchwardens soon stepped out of the church porch in a babble of chatter, shaking hands, clapping each other on the back, congratulating one another on well-delivered speeches, and warning every passerby to wrap up warm against the night dew. Bit by bit the throng dispersed, the last carriage wheels rumbled away down the road, and Shirley was just stepping out from behind the golden dripping laburnum blooms when Mr. Helstone rounded the garden path and almost ran right into her.
“Ah, there you are! I was afraid you’d already set off for home,” he said, grinning. “Caroline, come here, will you?”
Caroline hurried over, both young women half expecting a scolding for vanishing mid-church service earlier that evening, but the rector’s mind was fixed on far more urgent matters.
“I won’t be sleeping at the rectory tonight,” he explained. “I ran into an old army friend of mine only ten minutes ago, and promised to ride back to his inn with him. I’ll be back around noon tomorrow. Thomas the parish clerk is otherwise engaged, so I can’t get him to stay here to watch the house like I usually do when I’m gone for a night. Now—”
“Now you need me, the self-appointed first gentleman of Briarfield, to step into your post,” Shirley cut in, eyes sparkling. “Be master of the rectory, and stand guard over your niece and your two maids while you’re off gallivanting, isn’t that it?”
“Exactly, Captain. Knew the post would suit you,” the rector chuckled. “Will you do Caroline the favour of staying here as her guest for the night, instead of trekking all the way back to Fieldhead after dark?”
“But Mrs. Pryor will be waiting up for me, expecting me home,” Shirley protested gently.
“I’ll send a boy over at once to leave her a note explaining the whole situation. Come, make up your mind. The dew’s falling heavy, and it’s getting far too late to traipse across the fields. You and Caroline will have far more fun catching up with each other than you would alone at Fieldhead, I’d wager.”
“Then I promise to stay,” Shirley said at once, turning to squeeze Caroline’s hand. “You’re right, we won’t waste a single minute of the night apart. Go on now, meet your old friend, and don’t worry about a single thing here.”
“If there happens to be any trouble in the dark hours, Captain—if you hear a lock being jimmied, or a pane of glass being cut, or soft stealthy steps creeping around the house—and I don’t mind telling you, what with that sharp, brave heart of yours under all that lace and ribbon, those little incidents are all too common these days—what would you do?”
“Can’t say for sure. Might faint dead away and make Caroline carry me to the sofa,” Shirley joked, “but if you’re posting me on sentry duty, you’ve got to give me weapons. What’s stowed in your little stronghold, doctor?”
“You don’t know how to wield a sword, I suppose?”
“Not a chance. I’d do far better with a carving knife.”
“There’s a lovely sharp one in the dining room sideboard,” he told her. “It’s a lady’s knife, light as a feather in your hand, and pointed as a tiny poniard.”
“That one will suit Caroline. But you’ve got pistols, I know you do. Give me a brace of those.”
“I’ve two pairs total. I’ll lend you one pair. They’re hanging over my study mantelpiece in their cloth cases.”
“Loaded?”
“Aye, but not cocked. Remember to cock them both before you turn in for the night. This is a great compliment, you know. If you were one of those clumsy lads who can barely hold a gun without shooting their own foot off, I’d never hand them over.”
“I’ll be careful. You don’t need to hang around here any longer, Mr. Helstone. Off you go,” Shirley laughed, waving him through the garden gate. “He’s awfully gracious to lend me his pistols, isn’t he?” she murmured to Caroline as the rector vanished down the lane. “Come on, Lina, let’s go in and get some supper. I was so thoroughly annoyed sitting next to Mr. Sam Wynne at tea that I couldn’t eat a single bite, and now I’m absolutely ravenous.”
They stepped inside, making their way to the dim dining room, where the wide open windows let the soft night air drift in, heavy with rose and jasmine scent from the garden, the faint far echo of last stragglers’ footsteps on the road, and a low, thrumming murmur Caroline paused to listen to, leaning her elbows on the windowsill.
“Shirley, I can hear the beck rushing over the stones down in the Hollow,” she whispered.
She rang the bell, asking Fanny to bring a candle and two plates of bread and milk, the simple supper both of them preferred. When Fanny arrived with the tray, she moved to close the windows and shutter them, but both girls asked her to leave them propped open. The twilight was too calm, its breath too sweet, to lock out just yet. They ate their quiet meal in soft, easy silence, until Caroline rose to move the overscented vase of carnations from the sideboard to the windowsill, where their perfume wouldn’t cling thick in the sultry room. On her way back, she tugged a side drawer half open, and drew out the sharp, glinting carving knife the rector had mentioned.
“So you assigned this one to me, Shirley?” she said, turning the blade over in her fingers. “It’s bright, sharp-edged, tapering to a fine point. Dangerous-looking. I can’t even imagine what it would feel like to point this at another human being. I don’t think I could ever summon the strength to drive it home.”
“I’d hate to do it too,” Shirley replied, sipping her glass of cold fresh milk, her face thoughtful and pale as ever, “but I know I would, if there was no other way to protect the people I care for.”
When they’d finished their supper, they called Fanny and Eliza back in, urging the two tired maids to head straight to bed after a long day of baking currant buns, filling teapots, and running up and down stairs with trays. Within minutes, they heard the maids’ bedroom door click shut down the hall. Caroline took a candle and wandered through every room in the house, making sure every window was latched tight and every door barred, even the spooky back kitchen and the vault-like cellars. When she returned to the dining room, it was nearly eleven o’clock.
“Every door and window’s locked, no strangers hiding anywhere inside,” she said. “It’s almost bedtime, but I don’t want to turn in just yet, if you don’t mind, Shirley. I brought the two pistols out of uncle’s study for you.” She set them on the table in front of Shirley.
“Why don’t you want to sleep?” Shirley asked, picking up the firearms one by one, checking the mechanisms gently before setting them back down.
“I’ve got this strange, jittery, wide-awake feeling in my chest.”
“So do I.”
“Is it something electrical in the air, do you think?”
“No, look, the sky’s perfectly clear, there are more stars than I’ve ever seen. It’s a perfect night.”
“But it’s so still. I can hear the water in the Hollow chattering over its stony bed as clearly as if it was running right under the churchyard wall.”
“I’m glad it’s quiet,” Shirley said. “If there was wind howling or rain lashing down, I think it would drive me half feverish right now.”
“Why is that, Shirley?”
“Because I wouldn’t be able to hear properly.”
“You’re listening towards the Hollow, aren’t you?”
“Yes. It’s the only direction any sound could possibly come from right now.”
They settled side by side by the window, leaning their arms on the sill, their faces lit soft by starlight and that faint lingering June twilight that never fully fades from the western sky until the first hint of dawn bleeds pink in the east.
“Mr. Helstone thinks we have no idea where he’s gone, or what he’s planning to do,” Shirley murmured, “but I think I’ve guessed most of it. Haven’t you?”
“I’ve guessed some of it,” Caroline whispered back.
“All those men out there—your cousin Robert Moore included—think we’re fast asleep in our beds, none the wiser.”
“That we don’t care a thing about them, that we’ve no hopes or fears for them at all,” Caroline added.
They fell quiet for half an hour, only the church clock chiming the quarters marking the slow passage of the night. When the air grew chill, they wrapped their silk scarves tighter around their shoulders, tucked their bonnets back on, and kept watching.
Just before midnight, the rectory’s guard dog started barking, sharp and nonstop. Caroline slipped noiselessly through the dark kitchen passages to give him a piece of bread to quiet him, and when she came back to the dining room, the candle was out. Shirley was leaning far out the open window, her dark hair catching the faint starlight. Caroline didn’t say a word, just slipped to her side. The dog started barking again, furious, then fell abruptly silent.
They both strained their ears. It wasn’t just the rush of the mill stream they could hear now. There was a nearer, muffled, rhythmic tramp of feet on the road below the churchyard—hundreds of feet, marching slow and steady, drawing closer.
They couldn’t see a thing, the thick leafy hedge in the garden blocking their view of the road, but the tramp grew louder, until a sharp, rough voice cut the silence.
“Halt!”
The march stopped dead. There was a low huddle of whispering voices, no single word clear from where they stood.
“We have to hear what they’re saying,” Shirley breathed.
She grabbed the two pistols off the table, slipped silently through the glass dining room door that opened right onto the garden path, and stole down to the high garden wall, pressing herself against it under the bloom-heavy lilac bushes. Caroline would never have dared leave the house alone, but she would follow Shirley anywhere. She glanced at the sharp carving knife on the sideboard, but left it there, and joined her friend at the wall. They didn’t dare peek over the top for fear of being spotted, so they huddled down behind it, and the words drifted clear to them.
“Looks like a rambling old dump. Who lives here besides that damn parson?”
“Just three women—his niece, and two maids.”
“Where do they sleep?”
“The maids are in the back rooms. The niece has a bedroom at the front.”
“What about Helstone?”
“That room up yonder’s his. He had a light burning a little while ago, but it’s out now.”
“Best way to get in?”
“If they told me to do for him—and he’s more than earned it—I’d try that long window down there, the one leading to the dining room. I could creep my way up the stairs easy, I know exactly where his bed is.”
“What about the women? They’ll scream.”
“Leave ‘em be unless they make a noise, then I’ll shut ‘em up quick. Wish I could find the old sod asleep. If he wakes up, he’s dangerous.”
“Does he have guns?”
“Firearms, aye, and they’re always loaded.”
“Then you’re a fool to hang around here. One shot and the whole neighbourhood’s alerted. Moore’s men would be on us before we could turn around. We’d blow our whole plan.”
“Go on ahead then, I tell you. I can take care of Helstone all on my own.”
There was a pause. One of the men dropped a heavy tool, and it clanged loud on the stone causeway. The guard dog burst into wild, furious barking again.
“Damnation! That’s ruined it,” the lead man snarled. “He’d be woken up by that racket even if he was dead. You never said there was a dog. Forward, move out!”
The tramp of hundreds of feet picked up again, rumbling down the road, until the last echo of it faded away.
Shirley stood up straight, and peeked over the top of the wall. “Not a single one left,” she said, quiet. Then she breathed out, soft and shaky, “Thank God.”
Caroline echoed the words, her voice far less steady. She was trembling all over, her heart hammering so hard she could feel it in her ears, her face cold, her forehead damp with sweat.
“Thank God they left us alone,” she whispered. “But where are they going? They passed right by us to go somewhere far more important.”
“They did the smart thing,” Shirley said, calm as ever. “The men they’re heading for can defend themselves. They’re prepared. If those rioters had broken in here, I had my finger on the trigger of my pistol, ready to greet the first one through the window with a bullet he’d never expect—but three hundred of them behind him? I haven’t got three hundred hands, or three hundred guns. I couldn’t have kept you, or the two poor maids asleep upstairs, safe. Thank the Lord we didn’t have to find out.”
She paused a second, then went on. “We can’t just stand here doing nothing. We’ve got to go to the Hollow.”
“The Hollow, Shirley?”
“Where the rioters are heading. They took the high road, so we won’t run into them. The path across the fields is all quiet, no one on it at all. Will you come with me?”
“Yes,” Caroline said at once, even though her knees felt wobbly, even though every instinct was screaming to hide behind a locked door, because she couldn’t possibly leave Shirley to go alone.
“Then let’s lock all the windows up tight, make sure the house is as secure as we can leave it,” Shirley said. “You know why we’re going, don’t you, Cary?”
“I… because you want to go.”
“You’d follow me straight into the middle of a fight just because I wanted you to? What a gentle, loyal wife you’d make for a stern husband!” Shirley laughed soft, but her voice was warm. “Your face is whiter than the moon right now, and your fingers are trembling like the aspen tree by the gate. Let me give you a better reason. We’re going for Robert Moore’s sake. We’re going to try to warn him the rioters are on their way.”
“Of course!” Caroline said, lighting up at once. “I was being such a silly blind fool. I’ll go with you, Shirley, I’ll go gladly.”
“I know you will. You’d follow me to my death meekly, but you’d run straight to Robert’s side without a second thought. But there’s no dying tonight, I promise. We’re not in any real danger.”
Caroline ran around the house, latching every shutter and every window fast. “Don’t worry, I can run as fast as you need me to,” she said, grabbing Shirley’s hand tight. “Let’s cut straight across the fields.”
“You think you can climb the stone walls?”
“Tonight I can.”
“Aren’t you afraid of the hedges, and the beck we’ll have to cross?”
“I can cross it.”
They set off, running, every now and then skidding to a halt to climb over a stone wall. Shirley was light on her feet, quick as a deer, leaping over gaps without even breaking stride. Caroline, less sure-footed, stumbled twice, scraped her palms raw, but jumped straight back up, laughing that she wasn’t hurt. They reached a thick hawthorn hedge, fumbled for ten minutes finding a narrow gap to squeeze through, and their hair got tangled, their muslin gowns tore, but they didn’t care about anything but speed. On the other side, they reached the beck, rushing deep in its stony bed, with only a single narrow plank strung across it for a bridge. Shirley had run across it a hundred times before, but Caroline had never dared step foot on it.
“I’ll carry you over,” Shirley said. “You’re light, I’m strong. Let me.”
“If I fall in, you can fish me out,” Caroline said, squeezing her hand tight, and she stepped onto the wobbly plank like it was solid grass, not a thin strip of wood over rushing dark water. Shirley stepped right behind her, and neither of them so much as wobbled. They were so far past caring about small dangers that even a raging flood wouldn’t have stopped them. They’d barely set foot on the opposite bank when a gunshot cracked the air from the north. A second later, another shot boomed far off to the south. Within three minutes, two more reports echoed from the east and west.
“I thought we’d both been hit when that first one went off,” Shirley said, breathing out a long shaky breath. “I swear I felt a sting on my temple

The two girls slipped through the greying dawn on their walk back to the rectory, not a single soul crossing their path. They turned the key in the latch as soft as breath, stole up the staircase without a single creak to betray them, the faint, honeyed light of breaking June guiding their steps. Shirley fell straight onto her bed the moment she stepped through the door; she had never spent a night away from Fieldhead before, and the wild, terror-charged drama of the riot they’d just witnessed was unlike any excitement she’d ever known, yet scarce had her head touched the pillow before deep, restoring sleep swept over her, smoothing every line of tension from her face.

Perfect, glowing health was Shirley’s gift: warm-hearted, fierce in her sympathies, she was never nervous. The strongest emotions could rouse her, sway her, shake her like a gale shakes an oak, but they never exhausted her. Her elasticity never bent, her bright freshness never faded. Where every day brought her some new, stirring joy, every night brought her the rest to meet it. Caroline sat beside her now, watching the soft serenity of her sleeping face, and felt the gulf between their two temperaments yawn wide.

She could not sleep. Even the mild, pleasant bustle of the previous day’s school tea would have kept her restive through the dark hours, and the raw, screaming terror of the mill attack replayed behind her eyelids on a loop, sharp and vivid. It was useless even to try to lie still; she sat upright beside Shirley, counting the dragging minutes, watching the pink edge of the sun climb higher over the thatched rooftops.

Caroline had kept too many of these lonely vigils of late: hours where the mind, starved of sweet hope, of warm, happy memories, tries to feed itself on nothing but thin, half-formed wishes, and when that fails, turns to philosophy, to resolution, to resignation, begging for aid that never seems to come. A devout Christian, she had poured out prayer after prayer through the dark, begging for patience, for strength, for a little relief from the quiet ache that had gnawed at her for months, but it felt as if her words had drifted unheard up to the empty sky. There were moments she sank into that cold dark of religious despair, convinced she was forsaken entirely, though she could not name the sin that had earned it.

Most people have known that hour: when hope after hope has been deferred, till the heart sickens in the chest. It is the darkest point before the dawn, the sharp January wind that carries both the dirge of dying winter and the secret promise of spring just over the horizon. The suffering soul cannot see that promise in the throes of its pain, but it is always there. Hold fast to love, hold fast to faith: He never deserts the souls He loves.

At last the rectory roused. The servants stumbled down to light the kitchen fire, the shutters were thrown open below. Caroline rose from the hard, thorny couch that had given her no rest, feeling that small, gentle lift of spirits that the return of day and the chance of small, kind actions gives to anyone not quite broken. She dressed with her usual careful neatness, taming her hair and smoothing the fabric of her gown till no trace of the forlorn, heavy languor in her heart showed on her face. When they both came down to breakfast, she looked as fresh as Shirley, save that Miss Keeldar’s eyes sparkled bright with anticipation, and Caroline’s held a soft, tired dimness.

“Today I am going to cross-examine Moore thoroughly,” Shirley declared as her first words, her face glowing with all the bright, busy interest that made every day feel like an adventure for her. “He thinks he outwitted us so cleverly, hiding all that danger away last night. That is always the way with men, is it not? They keep all sharp, hard truths from women, thinking they are sparing us pain. They had no idea where we were last night, just as we had no idea where they meant to hide. I do believe most men fancy women’s minds are no sharper than children’s. What a mistake that is.”

She spoke as she stood before the glass, twisting her naturally wavy hair into soft curls around her fingers, and picked up the theme again five minutes later while Caroline fastened the hooks of her gown and clasped her girdle snug.

“If men could see us exactly as we are, they would be quite shocked. The cleverest, sharpest of them all misread women, every time. They make our ‘good women’ into half doll, half angel, and our bad women into nothing but fiends, no shades in between. And then they gush over the heroines of poems and novels and plays, calling them divine! They may be pretty enough, but half the time they are as artificial as the silk rose pinned to the brim of my best bonnet. If I spoke all I thought on that subject, I would be stoned to death under a cairn of avenging stones before the hour was out.”

“Shirley, do hush, I cannot fasten these hooks if you fidget so,” Caroline laughed. “And for what it is worth, authors’ heroines are hardly more foolish than authoresses’ heroes.”

“Not a bit of it! Women read men far more truly than men read women. I shall write a magazine paper proving it one day, when I have time. Though I know perfectly well it will be ‘declined with thanks’ and left gathering dust at the publisher’s office.”

“Of course it will. You are not nearly learned enough to pull that off, Shirley.”

“God knows I cannot argue with you, Cary. I am as ignorant as a stone. The only comfort is, you are not much better.”

They settled down to their breakfast tea and toast.

“I do wonder how Mrs. Pryor and Hortense Moore fared through the night,” Caroline said as she poured out the coffee. “Selfish thing that I am, I did not think of either of them till this moment. They must have heard all the noise—Fieldhead and the cottage are so close to the mill, and Hortense is so timid of disturbances. I am sure Mrs. Pryor was frightened too.”

“Never fear, Moore will have made sure his sister was sent off to stay with Miss Mann last night, far away from the tumult,” Shirley assured her. “He will have stashed her there safely. Mrs. Pryor I am a little uneasy about, but we shall be back with her in half an hour.”

By that time, wild rumors of the night’s attack on Hollow’s Mill were spreading like wildfire across the whole parish. Fanny, who had run to Fieldhead to fetch the morning milk, came panting back with news that a full battle had broken out at the mill, and some folk were saying twenty men lay dead. Eliza, left at home, had been told by the butcher’s boy that the whole mill had burned to the ground. Both servants burst into the parlour breathless, spilling out their wild tales, swearing the rector must have run off last night to join Mr. Moore and the soldiers, that Mr. Malone had not been seen at his lodgings since the afternoon before, that Joe Scott’s wife and children were distraught wondering if their husband and father was hurt or dead.

Hardly had they finished the story when a knock came at the kitchen door: Fieldhead’s errand boy, red-faced and breathless, bearing a hurried scrawled note from Mrs. Pryor. She begged Shirley to come home at once, for the whole parish was in an uproar, and there were orders to be given that only the mistress of the hall could see to. A postscript added that Miss Helstone must on no account be left alone at the rectory, and that she ought to come with her.

“There is no question of that,” Shirley said, tying the ribbons of her own bonnet in a swift, neat bow before darting to fetch Caroline’s.

“But what of Fanny and Eliza? What if my uncle comes back?” Caroline protested.

“Your uncle will not be back for hours,” Shirley waved her off. “He will be galloping back and forth between Briarfield and Stilbro’ all day, rousing magistrates in the courthouse and officers at the barracks. Fanny and Eliza can ask Joe Scott’s wife and the clerk’s wife over to keep them company. Besides, there is no real danger now. The rioters will not dare rally for weeks, and I would wager Moore and your uncle will use last night’s outbreak to get the authorities to put a stop to the unrest entirely. I only hope they are not too cruel, not too relentless in hunting down the men who ran away defeated.”

“Robert will not be cruel,” Caroline said softly. “We saw that, last night.”

“But he will be hard,” Shirley corrected her. “And your uncle will be harder still.”

As they hurried along the path through the meadows and plantation to Fieldhead, they saw the distant highway already thronged with riders and pedestrians all streaming towards the usually quiet Hollow, every soul curious to gawk at the scene of the riot. When they reached the hall, the backyard gates stood wide open, the court and kitchen crowded with chattering neighbours who had come for their daily milk, Mrs. Gill the housekeeper vainly trying to shoo them off home.

Shirley stepped straight into the throng, her manner frank and easy, the quiet natural charm that always won over working folk far faster than any fine lady’s airs. She greeted them cheerfully, asked if everyone had got their milk measured out. When the men and women nodded, she raised an eyebrow.

“Then what on earth are you all lingering here for, gossiping?” she laughed, good-humoured. “The whole world loves to stand about talking over trouble, don’t they? You talk when someone dies sudden, you talk when there is a fire, you talk when a mill owner fails or gets hurt. What good does all that talk do?”

The Yorkshire crowd loved that plain, honest good-humoured chiding far better than any flattery, and they laughed back, unoffended. Shirley teased them gently for wasting their hard-earned free hours gossiping over tea and beer at their neighbours’ expense, then softened, asking them to do her the favour of heading home so the drive to the house would not be blocked for all the callers she expected that day. Stubborn against any force, Yorkshire folk yielded easily to fair, kind persuasion: the yard was empty and quiet in five minutes.

They stepped inside, and Mrs. Pryor came running down the broad oak staircase to meet them, her usual soft, placid face pale, her timid blue eyes wide and unsettled. She had not slept a wink all night, had heard every shot and shout, and had been too nervous to give any orders touching Shirley’s stores without her mistress there to approve.

“The soldiers sent up word asking for refreshments, and for linen and wine for the wounded,” she said tremulously, “but I did not dare take the liberty of giving away your property, my dear. I was afraid they would eat us out of house and home, I could not think how many men there were. I do fear there has been some delay, now.”

Shirley’s face flushed dark, her temper flaring hot. “Those men risked their lives to defend my mill, my property,” she said, sharp and clear as a bell, calling for Mrs. Gill so loud her voice rang through the whole house. She remembered the day Shirley had fought two huge carrier dogs single-handed to save her old hound Tartar, choking one of the brutes off his bleeding eye till help arrived, and everyone in the house knew that flash in her eye meant she would not be crossed.

“Send a man down to the mill at once, tell them everything in this house is at Mr. Moore’s service, the rector’s, the soldiers’ and the wounded’s,” she ordered. “Load all the bread, all the meat, every cask of wine in the cellar onto the hay cart, and send it all down to the Hollow. If we have not enough, send the butcher and the baker every scrap they have.”

Caroline slipped an arm around the flustered Mrs. Pryor, soothed her, walked her up to her room to rest, then came back down to help the overstretched servants. She was just hefting a heavy basket of linen up the cellar stairs when Shirley spotted her, darting over to take it from her hands at once. “This is nonsense, you will strain your soft arms,” she said, and the last of her sharp anger melted away, leaving only her usual warm, cheerful self.

She was still supervising the loading of the cart when a gentleman stepped into the yard, approaching her quiet and unannounced. It was Robert Moore, his face still smudged with ash, a faint band of court-plaster stuck to his temple.

“I hope Miss Keeldar is well this morning?” he said, a faint, teasing glint in his sharp eyes as he took in her still-flushed cheeks.

“Well enough, if I have not been too foolish,” Shirley laughed, not bothering to hide her smile. “And you are well too, I see. You deserve a little scolding for hiding all that danger from us last night, that is for certain. What brings you here?”

“Your messenger came down saying every single thing in Fieldhead was at our disposal,” he said, his quiet laugh rumbling in his chest. “We guessed you were overdoing it. There are only half a dozen soldiers and as many civilians down at the mill, not a full regiment. Let me help you cut this load down to size.”

He ordered half the baskets and casks unloaded, sent back to the cellar, teasing her all the while about what a wonderful over-generous army contractor she would have made. As the cart rumbled off towards the Hollow, Shirley leaned forward. “What of the wounded?” she asked.

“None of our side were hurt badly,” he answered.

“You were hurt yourself, on the temple,” came a soft, quiet voice from the shadow of the doorway, where Caroline had been lingering half-hidden behind Mrs. Gill. Moore’s eyes snapped to her at once.

“Lift your hair, let me see,” she said, her voice gentle. He took off his hat, pushed his dark curls back to show the small plaster, and Caroline nodded, satisfied, before stepping back into the dimness.

“How did she know I was hurt?” Moore asked, quiet.

“Rumour, no doubt,” Shirley said, then her tone hardened a little. “I was asking after your victims, the rioters, not your men. What harm came to them?”

“One of the rioters was killed,” Moore answered, his face growing serious. “Six others were wounded.”

“What have you done with them?”

The smouldering tension of last night’s mill riot hung soft as woodsmoke over Fieldhead Hall that morning, even as pale sun broke through the haze gilding the distant moors. Downstairs, Shirley was still locked in heated, eager conversation with Robert Moore, the mill owner who had stood his ground against two hundred rioters hours before, while Caroline Helstone slipped quietly back up the staircase to find Mrs. Pryor.

Her governess sat huddled by the bedroom window, shoulders hunched tight, and the faint, almost invisible line of pain around her mouth told Caroline all she needed to know: Shirley’s sharp, hasty temper earlier that morning had cut deeper than anyone could have guessed. To a stranger, Mrs. Pryor would have seemed entirely unmoved by the small, tender attentions Caroline offered—steeping her favourite chamomile tea, tucking a soft wool shawl around her shoulders, murmuring quiet little anecdotes to make her smile—but Caroline knew better. She saw the way the older woman’s shoulders softened, the way her fingers curled gently around Caroline’s wrist when she passed the cup, and knew the comfort was sinking in, mending the quiet hurt.

“I have never had enough confidence, enough firmness of purpose,” Mrs. Pryor said at last, her voice thin and wobbly. “Surely by now Miss Keeldar knows I would never do anything but strive to do what is right, no matter how much it troubles me. The riot, the alarms of the night, the sudden demand to make a choice for another person… it puzzled me. I dared not act rashly. I can only pray my hesitation did no real harm.”

A soft, tentative knock rapped at the door. Caroline opened it a crack, and there was Shirley, standing in the gallery like a repentant child who’d smashed a beloved ornament, her face flushed with shame, her usual boldness nowhere to be found.

“How is she?” Shirley whispered, almost too quiet to hear.

“Her spirits are low,” Caroline told her gently.

“I was horrid to her,” Shirley burst out, all contrition. “Ungenerous, ungrateful, insolent. I snapped at her for nothing but her own overabundance of conscientiousness. Tell her I am sorry, tell her I beg her forgiveness—please, Caroline.”

Caroline delivered the message with a heart full of gladness, and Mrs. Pryor rose to her feet, trembling a little. She hated public shows of affection, dreaded reconciliations as all timid souls do, but she faltered to the door and said, “Come in, my dear.”

Shirley swept inside on a burst of impulsive warmth, wrapping her arms around her governess and kissing her cheek so hard it left a pink mark. “You have to forgive me,” she insisted. “I cannot bear even a single misunderstanding between us. I would be utterly lost without you.”

“There is nothing to forgive,” Mrs. Pryor replied, patting her back gently. “Let us forget the whole incident. It only proves what I have long known: I am not fit to face moments of crisis.”

That quiet, bitter little conviction clung to her long after, no matter how much Shirley and Caroline teased and laughed with her to chase it away. She could forgive Shirley for her outburst, but she could not forgive herself for being weak.

Shirley was barely settled before a servant summoned her downstairs again. The first visitor was her rector, Mr. Helstone, bright-eyed and giddy with the thrill of the night before, ready to shower her with both a lively welcome and a playful reprimand for the reckless way she’d thrown herself into the fray. He expected both, and took both in perfect good humour.

In all his excitement, he quite forgot to ask after his niece. The rioters, the mill, the magistrates, the heiress herself filled all his thoughts, and he rambled on about the part he and his curate had played defending the Hollow.

“Everyone who disapproves of us will be spitting mad over this,” he said, laughing. “But I defy every last gossiper. I was there only to uphold the law, to stand my ground as a man and a Briton—those roles sit perfectly well alongside the duties of a clergyman, if you ask me. And your tenant Moore? He has won my full approval. Never have I seen a cooler, more determined man. He was fully prepared for the riot before it even happened, and once his plans won the day, he had the good sense not to abuse his victory. Half the magistrates are so frightened now they’re baying for cruel, excessive punishment, but Moore restrains them all with incredible prudence. He’s been unpopular in these parts for years, but mark my words: the tide is about to turn. The whole parish will soon realize how much they’ve underestimated him, and he’ll drop that gruff, reserved air he’s worn around us all to show the kind, gracious man he really is.”

He was just about to make a half-joking, half-serious remark about the obvious, unspoken partiality everyone had noticed between Shirley and her talented tenant when the door bell rang. He caught sight of the new visitor: white-haired, truculent old Hiram Yorke, his old bitter political enemy, and Helstone snatched up his hat, bade Shirley a hasty goodbye, and fled before they could exchange a single hostile word.

Mr. Yorke was in no gentle mood, and he let loose a torrent of blunt Yorkshire invective about the previous night’s events, lashing out at the rioters, the magistrates, the soldiers, and most of all the two “demon parsons” who’d dared to fight alongside the mill workers.

“The Church is in a fine mess now,” he grumbled. “What’s the world coming to when parsons go swaggering around with guns, killing men far honester than themselves?”

“What would you have had Moore do?” Shirley challenged, immediately leaping to her tenant’s defence. “Let them burn his mill down? He’s got enough courage for ten men, but no man can fight two hundred angry rioters alone.”

“He had the soldiers, those poor fools who sell their blood for a shilling a day.”

“You abuse soldiers as much as you abuse clergymen!” Shirley laughed, heated now, pacing the oak parlour floor. “Everyone in a red coat is trash to you, everyone in a black coat is a swindler. According to you, Moore should have just stood by and let his livelihood be destroyed, let himself be murdered, and every gentleman in the parish should have stood by and watched and done nothing.”

“If Moore had treated his workers right from the start, they never would have turned on him,” Yorke argued.

“That’s easy for you to say!” Shirley cried, cheeks flushing bright pink. “Your family’s lived at Briarmains for six generations! Everyone here has known you for fifty years, you know all their little quirks and prejudices. Moore came here a stranger, poor, friendless, with nothing but his own wits and his hard work to build a life. It’s no crime that he couldn’t turn his naturally quiet, grave nature into the jolly, familiar demeanour everyone expected overnight. Why should he be left defenceless against a mob for being a little too reserved? Why should anyone who stood by to help him be called a villain?”

“Calm down, lass,” Yorke said, grinning, amused by her fiery defence. He’d never seen her so eager, so bright, and the fact she was defending Robert Moore so fiercely pleased him more than he’d ever admit.

“Calm down?” Shirley tossed her head. “I will not sit here and listen to dangerous nonsense! I like you well enough, Mr. Yorke, but half your principles are utter rubbish. All that cant about hating every parson, hating every soldier, pitting class against class, all those tyrannies you dress up as ‘liberty’? I want no part of it. You think you’re a great philanthropist, but Mr. Hall, the quiet gentle parson of Nunnely, is a far better friend to the people than you will ever be.”

Yorke would never have tolerated that speech from a man, or from most women, but Shirley was honest, sharp, and pretty, and her temper was a delight to him. “What else have you got to say for yourself?” he asked, when she paused to catch her breath.

“I could talk all day!” she laughed, still striding back and forth across the parlour rug. “All your extreme political views are nothing but opposition talk—you’d drop every last one of them the second you had any real power. You’d do exactly what Moore did, if you were in his shoes. I’m sick of everyone judging each other so harshly, no one giving anyone else an inch of grace. I think you’re a fine, honest old Yorkshireman, but you’re harsh, narrow, and far too unforgiving.”

Yorke stood up, brushing dust off his coat, and fixed her with a sharp, knowing grin. “When is the wedding, then?”

Shirley stared, colour rising in her cheeks, but her eyes didn’t flinch. “Whose wedding?”

“Yours and Robert Gérard Moore’s. Everyone’s talking about it, aren’t they? The wealthy heiress of Fieldhead marrying the poor hardworking mill tenant.”

Shirley drew herself up, her face glowing. “Is that your revenge for me arguing with you? Would the match be so bad, then? Is he not good enough for me?”

“Moore’s a gentleman,” Yorke said immediately. “His blood is as old and as fine as yours or mine.”

Shirley smiled, bright and proud. “That he is. There’s no inequality there, then, is there? No one could say we don’t suit. Thank you for your approval. Will you give me away when I stop being Miss Keeldar to become Mrs. Moore?”

Yorke stared at her, completely baffled. He couldn’t tell if she was teasing or dead serious, if her bright eyes were laughing or burning with earnest feeling. “I don’t understand thee at all, lass,” he said, turning for the door.

Shirley laughed, the sound light and warm. “Don’t worry. You’re not the only one who’s confused. As long as Moore understands me, that’s all that matters, isn’t it?”

“Moore can sort his own business out from now on,” Yorke said, shaking his head. “I’ll not meddle in it anymore. Good day, Miss Shirley.”

The day was soft and fair, thin clouds dimming the sun just enough to take the edge off the heat, and Caroline had convinced Mrs. Pryor to slip on her bonnet and light summer shawl for a walk up the far end of the Hollow, leaving Shirley to her callers. The glen narrowed here, the sides rising steep and thick with scrub oak and bramble, the mill stream gurgling and foaming over stones, rushing past gnarled old roots as if it was in a hurry to get somewhere no one else could follow. Half a mile from the mill, they stepped off the path into quiet: no footprints marred the wild rose blooms, no sound of men or machinery broke the hum of bees and the song of dozens of small birds. Bluebells spangled the shade like scattered bits of sky, tiny white star flowers dotted the grass, and the whole place felt like a secret no one but the trees had ever witnessed.

Mrs. Pryor loved quiet walks. She avoided high roads, hated crowded lanes, but with Caroline beside her, she had no fear at all. The tension melted out of her shoulders, her face softened, and for the first time Caroline could remember, she talked freely, pointing out different birds in the branches, naming every tiny wild flower that grew at their feet, talking about the botany she had studied years before, long before she came to Fieldhead. They stopped to sit on a mossy grey rock half way up the green hill that rose above the ravine, and Mrs. Pryor looked around at the familiar view, talking softly of how the valley had changed over the decades, her descriptions gentle and vivid, full of a quiet love for the land that no one else would have guessed she held.

She turned to Caroline then, brushing a stray curl back from the girl’s pale forehead, her voice softer than Caroline had ever heard it. “I do hope this sweet hill air does you good, my dear. I wish I could see a little more colour in your cheeks—were you ever rosy and bright, as little girls are supposed to be?”

“I was, once,” Caroline smiled. “Two years ago, I looked in the glass and saw a round, rosy face, not the pale thing I see now. Back when I had no cares at all.”

“Do you fret over the future?” Mrs. Pryor asked, the words coming out slow, as if she was fighting her natural shyness to say them. “Don’t. Let the morrow look after itself.”

“It is not the future that troubles me,” Caroline said, her voice dropping almost to a whisper. “The present is heavy, sometimes. Too heavy. I long to run away from it all.”

She could see Mrs. Pryor struggling to ask if her uncle was unkind to her, and Caroline hurried to reassure her. “Uncle is kind enough. We never quarrel. No one is cruel to me. But sometimes… sometimes I feel as if I am not living at all, just enduring the days, marking time. Since you and Shirley came to Fieldhead, I have been happier, but… there is a reason I cannot name, a secret I cannot tell anyone, that makes me ache to leave this place, and forget every last person in it.”

No one who watched Robert Moore defend his mill that fateful night had seen even half of the steel that ran through his bones. The rest of that sharp, unyielding resolve—raw, unsparing, almost ferocious—blazed to life in the weeks that followed, as he hunted the riot’s ringleaders with the tireless focus of a hound on a hot trail.

The ragged, hungry foot-soldiers of the mob he never touched. Some quiet, unacknowledged thread of justice in him knew that men goaded by empty bellies and twisted lies were no fit targets for vengeance: to lash out at suffering bent low was the mark of a tyrant, not a man. Even though dawn had broken clear enough in the final minutes of the attack for him to recognize half their faces, he passed them on the roads and market streets day after day without a glare, a threat, or a single word of rebuke.

But the leaders were strangers, outside agitators bussed in from the big manufacturing cities, most of them not working men at all: failed bankrupts, perpetual debtors, drunkards who had nothing left to lose and nothing good to offer. These Moore chased as though the hunt itself was sweeter than turning a profit on his finest cloth. His horse grew lean and tired under the weight of his constant rides, he all but lived in the saddle, galloping over the moors where the fresh wind filled his lungs far better than the steamy, dye-stinking air of his mill workrooms. The local magistrates, slow and timid men who had long let fear of assassination rule every choice they made, quailed at the sight of him. He delighted in rousing them, in forcing them to confront the cowardice they had hidden behind for years: the very same cowardice that had paralyzed every mill owner and public official in the district, save old Helstone, the firebrand rector who would have faced a musket ball without a flinch.

Moore knew the danger he courted every time he rode home after dark across the empty heather, moonlit or pitch-black as the skies allowed. Far from terrifying him, that risk stoked his pride, spurred his high-spirited temper on. He returned from those late rides elated, sharper and more alive than he ever was cooped up in his stuffy counting house. Two of the four ringleaders were cornered near Stilbro’ inside a fortnight; the remaining two were tracked as far as Birmingham, and he planned to leave to hunt them down before the week was out. Even as he did, he never neglected his mill: the rioters had never broken through the mill doors, his beloved iron machines were untouched, and the small repairs needed to fix the broken windows and scuffed frames were well underway.

No one could tell, in the thick of that grinding, dangerous work, if he ever spared a single soft thought for anything other than justice and business. He rarely stopped at Fieldhead; when he did, his visits were so brief they barely registered. The few times he called at the rectory, he only locked himself in the rector’s study for hours of talk, and slipped away without pausing to greet Caroline. He held himself to that rigid, unyielding line, no cracks in his composure to show the gentler feelings he kept locked deep under all that stern purpose.

Outside, the war raged on, unbroken, across the Continent. No hint of peace came, no break in the smoke of battle, no soft rain to nurture the olive trees of harmony. Ruin crept closer to Moore too, eating away at the foundations of his fortune, so that every step he took, across his counting house floor or galloping over the dark moor at Rushedge, he could swear the ground hollowed under his boots, trembling.

While that sweltering summer dragged on for Robert Moore, what of Shirley and Caroline?

First we turn to Shirley Keeldar, the heiress of Fieldhead. Anyone expecting a pale, pining love-lorn maiden, wasting away for the neglect of a cold-hearted suitor, would have been shocked at the sight of her. The faint wistfulness that sometimes touched her face had not vanished, but neither had her bright, careless, unselfconscious smile. She lit up the dark, old stone manor house with the sheer force of her good cheer. Her laugh echoed down the long gallery, her silk dress rustled softly across the dim entrance hall as she drifted from room to room, tucking wild roses into the ugly peach-bloom salon, throwing open the dining room casements to let the scent of mignonette and sweetbriar drift in, carrying potted geraniums out to sun themselves on the front porch.

She never could sit still at her sewing, no matter how hard she tried. Her thimble was barely on her finger, her needle scarcely threaded, before some random thought sent her running upstairs: to hunt for a long-forgotten ivory needlebook she did not need, to straighten a messy drawer, to lean against that one upstairs window where she could catch a glimpse of Briarfield church and the rectory, half hidden in oak trees. No sooner would she sit back down to her work than her great shaggy dog Tartar would scratch at the porch door, and she would dart to let him in, lead him to the kitchen to make sure his water bowl was full. Out in the sunlit farmyard, the turkeys and their poults, the peahens, the spotted guineafowl, the flock of bright pigeons scattered across the grass, and she could not resist grabbing a bread roll from the pantry to stand on the step scattering crumbs, laughing as the birds swarmed around her feet. Then she had to stop to talk to the stable hand John, to pet her mare, to watch the cows file in for milking, to feed the tiny rejected lambs and new-born calves with her own hands. John would bring up questions about the crops in the far fields, and she would grab her gipsy straw garden hat and trudge after him over stiles and along hedgerows, to settle the agricultural dispute right there on the spot. Bright summer afternoons slipped away like that, melting into soft golden evenings, and by the time she came back to the house for late tea, she never bothered to pick up her sewing again.

After tea, she curled up on the rug at Mrs. Pryor’s feet, a book in her lap, Tartar’s great lion-sized body stretched out beside her, his head resting on his paws. One hand rested always on his fur, because if she pulled it away, he would huff a soft, discontented groan. The old habits of childhood clung to her, even now. Sometimes Mrs. Pryor would chide her, telling her the great dog would ruin her dress, that she should sit at a table to read, that the firelight was too dim for her eyes. Shirley only laughed softly, and promised to change later, too wrapped up in her book to move.

Then she would look up, and the moon would be rising, spilling pale silver light over the page. The book had filled her head with stories, her heart with warm, bright feeling, and the quiet parlour, the clean hearth, the moon hanging low over the dark treetops outside, made the whole world feel like a poem, like Eden. A deep, unshakable native joy burned in her veins, a gift no one could take away, no man could earn, a pure free dower of Nature to her favourite child. She was almost buoyant, lifted up on that quiet rapture, walking slow through the dim gallery in the dark, too soft and indolent to ever pick up a pen to write down the strange, glowing visions that filled her mind. She had no idea how rare those feelings were, how precious that bright spring of joy in her heart was, that kept her young and green and alive.

By the end of July, she and Caroline would have left for the long northern tour they had planned for months, only a party of unwelcome visitors descended on Fieldhead out of nowhere: her uncle, aunt, and two prim cousins from Sympson Grove in the south. Hospitality demanded she let them in, and Caroline stared, surprised, that the usually stubborn, wilful Shirley surrendered so easily. She explained that she had spent two years of her childhood there, old ties pulled at her, even though she had nothing in common with any of them save little Harry, their son who was not coming north for months. The next Sunday, in Briarfield church, the Fieldhead pew was filled with the fidgety old gentleman who shifted his spectacles every three minutes, the placid old lady in brown satin, and two perfectly pattern-behaved young ladies in perfectly pattern clothes. Shirley looked like a bright black swan dropped in the middle of a flock of dull barnyard geese, small and forlorn, surrounded by their rigid respectability.

We leave her there for a while, to turn to Caroline Helstone. She could not intrude on Fieldhead in the middle of all the visiting fuss the new relatives had stirred up across the neighbourhood, so she was confined once more to the grey stone rectory, her long morning walks on empty country lanes, her afternoons sat in the parlour where the sun vanished by noon, or tucked in the garden alcove where dappled light fell through the rose vines and red currant bushes, over her white summer dress, still as a garden statue. She read the old tattered books from her uncle’s library: water-stained old Lady’s Magazines, wild Methodist tracts full of ghost stories and dreams, old English classics that had once tasted sweet in her childhood, but were now completely tasteless. She sewed garments for the poor, as Miss Ainley had taught her, and more than once she felt hot tears drop slow onto the linen in her lap, as she wondered how Miss Ainley could bear the quiet solitude of her little cottage, so serene and uncomplaining.

Her thoughts turned sharp, fierce, and tender, as she sat there stitching. She did not want to fade into a quiet old pious woman who looked only to heaven for comfort. She believed God had not made them to spend all their days waiting to die. She ached for something more: for single women to have the right to profitable, interesting work, a place in the world that did not revolve entirely around catching a husband. She thought of all the girls in the neighbourhood, the Armitages, the Birtwhistles, the Sykeses: their brothers all had professions, all had purpose, while their sisters were left with nothing but endless sewing and unprofitable calls, their whole lives narrowed down to the single hopeless goal of marriage, which most of them would never reach. The men mocked them, called the matrimonial market overstocked, and then demanded their daughters stay home and sew and cook and be content forever, as if they had no faculties, no dreams, no hunger for more than that. She thought of the virtuous woman in the Bible, the one who bought estates and ran businesses, who was a manager and a leader, and wondered why no one ever asked for their daughters to reach that royal, capable standard, instead of shutting them up in small, useless lives. Fathers could not alter everything at once, she thought, but they could stop dismissing their daughters’ longing with a jest and an insult. Cultivate them, give them scope and work, and they would be the dearest companions in health, the tenderest nurses in sickness, the most faithful prop in their old age.

The summer day hung heavy and soft, bright as polished gold, not a single breeze stirring the rose trellises or a wisp of cloud dimming the wide blue sky. Caroline had spent every hour of it alone, her uncle off on business in Whinbury, and the long, silent stretches of time felt as vast and empty as the Sahara wastes, even with the scent of jasmine drifting through the rectory garden. Curled in her favourite window alcove, her half-finished sewing for the parish charity basket heavy on her lap, her fingers moving mechanically over the needle, her mind wandered restlessly, chasing the same soft, unspoken thought that had haunted her every day since her uncle’s falling out with Robert Moore.

When Fanny popped around the corner of the house, craning over the lawn to spot her, and called her name, Caroline answered with a quiet murmur, and the maid hurried over, pressing a crumpled sealed note into her hands. Caroline’s fingers felt so numb she nearly let it slip between the folds of her work. “Harry Scott brought it over from the Hollow,” Fanny told her.

The name jolted her wide awake, as if someone had tapped her shoulder and breathed hope back into her chest. She lifted her head fast, eyes bright with sudden, sharp energy. “From the Hollow?” She snatched the dropped note before it could slide off her lap, tore through the seal in a single motion, and devoured the words in two seconds flat. It was a warm, affectionate note from Hortense – she was home from Wormwood Wells, alone for the day because Robert had left for Whinbury market, and there was nothing she wanted more than for Caroline to come over for tea. She added, with the practical affection only Hortense could muster, that Caroline must be bored out of her mind stuck at the rectory with no decent company since the rift between their families, and she should put her bonnet on and run over immediately.

Caroline didn’t need to be told twice. She set aside the little child’s slip she’d been trimming with braid, flew up the stairs, tucked her loose curls under her plain straw bonnet, and wrapped her soft black silk scarf around her shoulders – its dark fabric set off her white summer dress and the pink flush rising in her cheeks perfectly, and she didn’t waste a second checking her reflection before she was out the door. This was the escape she’d ached for for weeks, away from the quiet sadness of the empty rectory, away from the constant weight of missing Robert. The green lane sloping down to the Hollow smelled like wild honeysuckle and clover, sweeter than any hothouse rose, and every step felt like a little victory. She knew Robert was not home, of course, but just to step through the doors of the cottage he lived in, to sit in the chair he’d occupied that morning at breakfast, to breathe the same air he breathed, felt almost like a reunion. A tiny, giddy hope nuzzled at the back of her mind, soft as a peri’s wings brushing her cheek: what if he came home early? What if he walked through the door while she was there, and she could look right into his face, shake his hand, sit beside him for even just a minute? She chided the silly thought away, but she couldn’t stop smiling.

Hortense must have spotted the flash of Caroline’s white dress through the cottage garden shrubs, because she stepped out onto the porch to meet her, straight and dignified as ever, no giddy rush to her steps, but a soft smile tugging at her lips. She kissed Caroline’s cheek, hugged her gently, and led her inside, half charmed by the girl’s obvious delight – she would have sent Caroline right back to the gate if she’d guessed that all that joy was for her brother, not for her. No sister likes to imagine a sweet young girl has fallen head over heels for her brother, after all; it feels like a silly, foolish delusion, a crude waste of romance, until you get used to the idea.

“I told you in my note you’d find me alone,” Hortense said, as she guided Caroline toward the parlour door, “but I wrote it this morning. We had a visitor drop by after dinner.”

She pushed the door open, and Caroline’s eyes fell on a massive heap of crimson fabric overflowing the fireside armchair, topped by a cap so vast, frilled and ribboned with puffy “love-ribbon”, it looked like it could outrank a crown. Mrs. Yorke was sat there, the most formidable lady in the parish, who never bothered calling on anyone, who dismissed every local gossip as a silly idle woman – her stopping by for tea was as much a honour as if the queen herself had dropped in unannounced for a casual meal. She’d taken a strange liking to Hortense, after all, admired her steady, no-nonsense foreign ways, her plain, precise dress and unflappable temper – everything Mrs. Yorke despised in the flighty pretty young ladies of the parish.

Caroline stepped forward, shy, a little nervous – as the parson’s niece, she’d never gotten much warmth from Mrs. Yorke, and sure enough, the older woman greeted her with a cool, brief nod. She turned away to take off her bonnet, glad for the distraction, when a tiny figure in a blue frock with a bright sash popped up from the footstool hidden under the folds of that enormous red gown, ran at her, and threw her arms around her neck. “My mother’s not being nice to you,” Jessie Yorke said, planting a loud kiss on her cheek, “and Rose over there won’t even look up. They’re like that. If an angel with a star crown walked in here, mom would just nod stiffly, and Rose would never lift her head. But I like you, I’m gonna be your friend.”

“Jessie, stop that forward nonsense at once,” Mrs. Yorke called.

“But mom, you’re frozen!” Jessie protested. “Miss Helstone never did anything to you. Why are you always so dry and stiff to every nice young lady? Even Shirley Keeldar gets the same treatment. You’re gonna scare everyone away from Briarmains, Martin says so all the time.”

Rose, sat cross-legged on the carpet by the bookcase, half buried in a tattered copy of Mrs. Radcliffe’s *The Italian*, looked up just long enough to give a brief, quiet greeting, then dropped her gray eyes back to her book. Caroline, who always had a knack for reading quiet, unique souls, knelt down beside her, peeked over her shoulder at the page, and read along in silence. Before long, Rose glanced at her, and asked, “Are you ready for me to turn the leaf?”

They talked for an hour after that, about the book, about how the story made them feel like they were really under the bright blue Italian sky thousands of miles away. Rose, who was barely twelve, announced she was never going to be stuck in Briarfield forever – she was going to travel the whole world, see every corner of the planet, no matter what anyone said. “Your rectory’s like a windowed grave,” she told Caroline bluntly. “No noise, no movement, nothing ever changes. Monotony is almost the same as death.” She argued that hiding your talents away, doing nothing but the same small domestic chores for your whole life, was the same sin as burying your talent in a napkin – she’d trade four years of apprenticing at sewing and mending stockings first, but after that, she was making her own path.

Mrs. Yorke watched them, then turned to Caroline, starting in on her usual sharp, prodding remarks. She teased Caroline for being too romantic, too guided by her feelings, for looking “lackadaisical” like a novel heroine instead of a hardworking sensible woman who could survive the real world. Caroline stood her ground, gentle but firm, until Mrs. Yorke dropped the first subtle, cruel insinuation: she joked that Hortense was hiding Robert behind the curtains somewhere, that silly young girls would do anything to wheedle their way into a house where there was an eligible single brother.

The comment landed like a stab straight through Caroline’s chest. Her cheeks burned bright, her eyes dropped to her lap, and she felt cornered, humiliated, until the sharpness in her chest flared into quiet, steady anger. She lifted her head, her blue eyes clear and steady, the flush of shame fading from her face until it was pale and determined. “Mrs. Yorke,” she said, her voice clear as silver, no tremble in it at all, “you have no right to say those things. You don’t know a single thing about my relationship with Hortense and Robert, and those cruel, unprovoked insinuations are far craftier and more false than anything you’ve ever accused me of. My being quiet, liking books, not gossiping around the parish is none of your business. If you dislike me so much, you can keep that opinion to yourself, and if you ever insult me unprovoked like this again, I will answer far less gently.”

Mrs. Yorke didn’t get angry. She laughed that short, dry laugh of hers, and nodded approvingly. “You’ve got spirit in you, after all. Always speak that honestly, and you’ll do fine.”

“I don’t take advice poisoned by insinuation,” Caroline replied, still calm, still steady. “I will never speak that harshly to anyone unless they’ve insulted me first, unprovoked.”

Jessie clapped her hands, delighted. “Mom, you finally met your match!”

Rose never looked up from her book. “No she isn’t,” she said, quiet and matter-of-fact. “Miss Helstone let herself get vexed. Mom would wear her out in a week. Shirley Keeldar is the one who manages it – she wears armour under her silk dress that not even mom’s sharpest words can pierce.”

The future breathes sometimes a soft, shuddering sob of warning long before the calamity it carries crashes into our lives. It lingers like a distant, gathering storm: the wind’s low, keening note in treetops, the strange bruised flush of sunset bleeding across the sky, tattered clouds that warn of a blast strong enough to strew the whole sea with wreckage, or to drift a fog thick with the yellow taint of Eastern pestilence over white Western cliffs, dimming the lattice panes of English homes with the poisoned exhalation of far-off plagues. Other times it bursts upon us all unannounced, as if a rock has split open to reveal a long-sealed grave, and the shrouded form of one who slept through the ages steps forth to stand face to face with you before you have time to gasp: a new Lazarus, risen unlooked for, bearing no gift but unforeseen calamity.

Caroline Helstone had walked home from Hollow’s Cottage that evening perfectly well, or so she had thought. When she woke the next morning, though, a strange heavy languor clung to her bones, a weight she could not shake off. At breakfast, and every meal the day after that, she could summon no appetite at all. What had once been her favourite spiced tarts, currant jams, and thick fresh cream all tasted like dry ash and coarse sawdust on her tongue.
“Am I unwell?” she murmured, pausing to stare at her reflection in the glass. Her eyes were bright, pupils blown wide, her cheeks rosier and fuller than they had been in months. “I look well. Why can I not eat?”

A pulse thudded fast and hard at her temples, and her brain hummed with strange, bright scattered energy: hundreds of half-formed, glittering thoughts darted through her head, gilded with the same warm flush that stained her cheeks. Then came a night of parched burning thirst, no rest, no ease. Towards dawn, a terrible nightmare seized her, claws sinking deep like a tiger’s hold, and when she jolted awake she knew, with sharp cold certainty, that she was ill.

She could not fathom how she had caught the fever. Most likely, she thought, some sweet poisoned breeze, thick with honeydew and hidden miasma, had wrapped around her on that late walk home, slipping into her lungs and veins to feed the slow spark of fever she had carried for months: the smouldering excitement of unspoken love, the heavy languor of long quiet conflict and habitual gentle sadness. It had fanned that spark to a bright unquenchable flame.

For days no one feared for her. After two hot restless days and sleepless nights, her symptoms seemed mild, no wild delirium, no violent spikes. Her uncle, the maids, the local doctor, even Shirley Keeldar when she came to call, all agreed a few days of rest would set her right. She’d be up and about picking roses in the rectory garden before the week was out, they all said.

But the few days stretched on, and still she did not recover, though everyone kept insisting health could not be far off. Mrs. Pryor, who had visited her every single day, sat at her bedside for a long quiet watch one morning, two weeks into her illness, holding her thin wrist between her fingers to count the thready racing pulse. Then she slipped quietly out of the room, shut herself in Mr. Helstone’s study, and stayed closeted with him for half the morning. When she came back, she set down her bonnet and shawl, stood by the bedside rocking gently back and forth, one hand folded in the other, that quiet old habit of hers, and spoke soft.
“I’ve sent Fanny over to Fieldhead to fetch my things. I’m to stay here and look after you till you’re well. Your uncle has kindly given his permission. Would that be alright with you, Caroline?”

“I’m sorry you should take such needless trouble,” Caroline had smiled back, faint and soft. “I do not feel very ill, but I cannot refuse resolutely. It will be such comfort to know you are in the house, to see you sometimes in the room. But don’t confine yourself on my account, dear Mrs. Pryor. Fanny nurses me very well.”

Bending over the pale little sufferer, Mrs. Pryor smoothed the soft hair under her cap, and gently fluffed her pillows. Caroline lifted her face to kiss her, smiling still.
“Are you free from pain? Are you tolerably at ease?” the older woman asked in a low earnest voice, returning the caress.
“I think I am almost happy.”
“You wish to drink? Your lips are parched.” She held a glass of cool barley water to her mouth. “Have you eaten anything today, Caroline?”
“I cannot eat.”
“But soon your appetite will return. It must return. That is, I pray God it may.”
When she laid her back on the couch, her arms wrapped around Caroline almost involuntarily, drawing her tight to her chest for a long still moment.
“I shall hardly wish to get well, that I may keep you always,” Caroline laughed.
Mrs. Pryor did not smile. A tremor ran over her features, and it took her minutes to steady it back into calm.
“You are more used to Fanny than to me,” she said at last. “I should think my attendance must seem strange, officious?”
“No. Quite natural, and very soothing. You must have been accustomed to wait on sick people, ma’am. You move about the room so softly, and you speak so quietly, and touch me so gently.”
“I am dexterous in nothing, my dear. You will often find me awkward, but never negligent.”

Negligent she never was. From that hour Fanny and Eliza faded into nonentities in the sick room: Mrs. Pryor made it her whole world, tending to every duty, never leaving it day or night. Caroline protested faintly at first, then not at all. Loneliness and gloom were banished from her bedside; protection and soft solace sat there in their place. The two settled into a wondrous gentle union. Caroline had always hated needing or receiving much care, and Mrs. Pryor had never before had the habit or knack of performing small tender services for anyone, but all of it unfolded so easily, so naturally, that the patient was as willing to be cherished as the nurse was bent on cherishing her. No flicker of weariness ever crossed Mrs. Pryor’s face to remind Caroline she ought to feel guilty for the trouble.

Even with all this tender care, it was strange the girl did not get well. She wasted like a snow-wreath in thaw, faded like a rose left parched in drought. Shirley Keeldar, whose thoughts never lingered on danger or death for long, had feared nothing at all for her friend at first, but as she visited week after week and saw Caroline sink a little further each time, cold alarm clutched at her heart. She confronted Mr. Helstone with such fierce energy that the gentleman was forced, very unwillingly, to admit his niece was suffering from something far worse than a mild migraine. When Mrs. Pryor quietly demanded a second specialist, he told her she might send for two if she liked. One came, delivered a dark vague pronouncement only time would unpack, wrote his prescriptions, issued his directions with crushing authority, pocketed his fee and left, no doubt knowing full well he could do no good but unwilling to say so.

Still no rumour of serious illness spread through the neighbourhood. At Hollow’s Cottage, everyone thought Caroline only had a severe cold, for she had written Hortense a little note to that effect, and Mademoiselle Moore contented herself with sending two pots of homemade currant jam, a recipe for a healing tisane, and a page of gentle advice. Mrs. Yorke, hearing a physician had been summoned, sneered at the hypochondriac fancies of the rich and idle, who, she said, having nothing but themselves to think of, would send for a doctor if their little finger so much as ached.

The “rich and idle” Caroline, meanwhile, was falling fast into a state of prostration whose rapid, wasting weakness puzzled every single person watching her—except one, who alone knew how liable a long undermined structure is to collapse all at once.

Sick people often have fancies no ordinary attendant can unravel, and Caroline had one even her tender nurse could not explain at first. Every single week, on the same day, the same hour, no matter if she was better or worse, she would beg to be helped up, dressed, and settled in the armchair by the window. She would sit there, quiet and patient, until noon had long passed. No matter how pale and exhausted she looked, she would gently put off every plea to lie back down until the church clock had tolled all twelve strokes of midday. The second the last chime faded, she would turn docile, meekly letting herself be helped back to bed. Once settled under the covers, she would bury her face deep in the pillow and pull the blankets tight around her, as if shutting out the sun and the world she was growing tired of. More than once, a faint tremor shook the small sick bed, and a quiet sob broke the stillness, and Mrs. Pryor saw all of it.

One Tuesday morning, as usual, Caroline had asked to rise. She sat wrapped in her soft white dressing gown, leaning forward in the easy chair, gazing steadily, patiently, out the lattice. Mrs. Pryor sat a little behind her, ostensibly knitting, but in truth watching her every breath. A soft change crossed Caroline’s pale mournful brow, animating its languor; a spark shot back into her faded eyes, reviving their old warm lustre. She half rose, craning forward to stare earnestly out. Mrs. Pryor glided softly to her side, glancing over her shoulder. From that window they could see the churchyard, and beyond it the open road. And there, riding sharply past, was a horseman. He was not far enough to be unrecognised, and Mrs. Pryor had long sight. She knew Mr. Robert Moore. Just as a little rise of ground between the road and the house blocked him from view, the church clock struck twelve.

“May I lie down again?” Caroline asked, quiet and small.
Her nurse helped her back to bed, drew the curtain around her, and stood listening near. The little couch trembled, and a suppressed sob stirred the still air. A sharp contraction of anguish twisted Mrs. Pryor’s features; she wrung her hands, half a groan escaping her lips. She remembered then: Tuesday was Whinbury market day. Mr. Moore always rode past the rectory on his way there, just before noon, every single week.

Caroline wore a slender braid of silk around her neck at all times, strung with some small trinket. Mrs. Pryor had seen a glint of gold peep out once or twice, but never got a clear look at it. Caroline never parted with it: when dressed, it was hidden deep in her bodice; when lying in bed, she always held it tight in her hand. That Tuesday afternoon, the thin doze—more like lethargy than true sleep—that sometimes shortened her long days had stolen over her. The weather was hot, and in her restless febrile tossing, she had pushed the blankets a little aside. Mrs. Pryor bent to tuck them back in. The small wasted hand, lying nerveless on her breast, still clasped that jealously guarded treasure. Those thin, attenuated fingers, painful to look at, were lax in sleep. Mrs. Pryor gently disengaged the silk braid, drawing out a tiny locket: a simple little thing, cheap enough for her small girlhood purse to afford. Under its crystal face rested a tight, crisp curl of black hair, far too short and coarse to have been cut from a woman’s head.

A small agitated movement made the silk chain twitch. The sleeper started, jolted awake. Her thoughts were scattered now when she woke, her gaze wandering. Half rising in fear, she cried out:
“Don’t take it from me, Robert! Don’t! It is my last comfort. Let me keep it. I never tell anyone whose hair it is; I never show it.”

Mrs. Pryor had already slipped behind the curtain, withdrawing out of sight. Caroline looked around the empty room, thinking herself alone. Her stray thoughts drifted back slow, like tired birds folding their wings on the sad shore of her mind, and the words she had never spoken aloud in health poured unconsciously from her lips.
“Oh, I should see him once more before all is over! Heaven might favour me thus far!” she cried. “God grant me a little comfort before I die! But he will not know I am ill till I am gone, and he will come when they have laid me out, and I am senseless, cold, and stiff. What can my departed soul feel then? Can it see or know what happens to the clay? Can spirits, through any medium, communicate with living flesh? Can the dead revisit those they leave? Can they come in the elements? Will wind, water, fire, lend me a path to Moore? Is it for nothing the wind sounds almost articulately sometimes—sings as I have lately heard it sing at night—or passes the casement sobbing, as if for sorrow to come? Does nothing, then, haunt it, nothing inspire it? Why, it suggested words to me one night; it poured a strain I could have written down, only I was appalled, and dared not rise to seek pencil and paper by the dim watch-light. What is that electricity they speak of, whose changes make us well or ill, whose lack or excess blasts, whose even balance revives? What are all those influences that are about us in the atmosphere, that keep playing over our nerves like fingers on stringed instruments, and call forth now a sweet note, and now a wail—now an exultant swell, and anon the saddest cadence? Where is the other world? In what will another life consist? Why do I ask? Have I not cause to think that the hour is hasting but too fast when the veil must be rent for me? Do I not know the Grand Mystery is likely to burst prematurely on me? Great Spirit, in whose goodness I confide, whom, as my Father, I have petitioned night and morning from early infancy, help the weak creation of Thy hands! Sustain me through the ordeal I dread and must undergo! Give me strength! Give me patience! Give me—oh, give me FAITH!”

She fell back on her pillow. Mrs. Pryor stole quietly out of the room, then slipped back in again soon after, as composed as if she had not heard a single word of that strange, raw soliloquy.

The next day callers began to arrive. Word had spread that Miss Helstone was worse. Mr. Hall and his sister Margaret came, and both left the sick room in tears, shocked at how much she had altered. Hortense Moore came, too, and Caroline seemed almost revived by her presence. She smiled and assured her she was not dangerously ill, talking low but cheerfully, the flush of excitement bringing colour back to her cheeks so she almost looked well.
“How is Mr. Robert?” Mrs. Pryor asked as Hortense gathered her things to leave.
“He was very well when he left.”
“Left! Is he gone from home?”
It was then explained that urgent police news about the rioters he had been chasing had called him away to Birmingham that very morning, and he would not likely be back for a full fortnight.
“He does not know Miss Helstone is very ill?”
“Oh no! He thought, like me, that she only had a bad cold.”

After Hortense left, Mrs. Pryor did not approach Caroline’s bedside for over an hour, for she heard her quiet weeping and dared not look on her tears. As evening closed in, she brought her a cup of tea. Caroline opened her eyes from a short doze, and looked at her nurse with a blank unrecognising gaze.
“I smelt the honeysuckles in the glen this summer morning,” she said, “as I stood at the counting-house window.”
Strange words like these from pale fevered lips pierce a loving listener’s heart sharper than steel. They sound romantic on a page, but in real life they are harrowing.
“My darling, do you know me?” Mrs. Pryor whispered.
“I went in to call Robert to breakfast. I have been with him in the garden. He asked me to go. A heavy dew has refreshed the flowers. The peaches are ripening.”
