“My darling! my darling!” the nurse repeated over and over.
“I thought it was daylight—long after sunrise. It looks dark. Is the moon now set?”
That moon, lately risen, gazed full and mild down upon her, floating unclouded in the deep blue summer sky.
“Then it is not morning? I am not at the cottage? Who is this? I see a shape at my bedside.”
“It is myself—it is your friend—your nurse—your—— Lean your head on my shoulder. Collect yourself.” Her voice dropped to a trembling murmur. “O God, take pity! Give her life, and me strength! Send me courage! Teach me words!”

Several minutes passed in silence. The patient lay mute and passive in the trembling arms, against the throbbing bosom of her nurse.
“I am better now,” Caroline whispered at last. “Much better. I know where I am. This is Mrs. Pryor near me. I was dreaming. I talk when I wake up from dreams; people often do in illness. How fast your heart beats, ma’am! Do not be afraid.”
“It is not fear, child—only a little anxiety, which will pass. I have brought you some tea, Cary. Your uncle made it himself. You know he says he can make a better cup of tea than any housewife can. Taste it. He is concerned to hear that you eat so little; he would be glad if you had a better appetite.”
“I am thirsty. Let me drink.”
She drank eagerly.
“What o’clock is it, ma’am?”
“Past nine.”
“Not later? Oh! I have yet a long night before me. But the tea has made me strong. I will sit up.”
Mrs. Pryor raised her, and fluffed her pillows around her.
“Thank Heaven! I am not always equally miserable, and ill, and hopeless. The afternoon has been bad since Hortense went; perhaps the evening may be better. It is a fine night, I think? The moon shines clear.”
“Very fine—a perfect summer night. The old church-tower gleams white almost as silver.”
“And does the churchyard look peaceful?”
“Yes, and the garden also. Dew glistens

Not all who wage that divine, desperate battle to hold fast to the life of the one they love come out victorious. Night after night, dark beads of agony may burst on the supplicant’s forehead, their soul crying out to the Unseen in that soundless, burning plea no human ear can catch: Spare my beloved. Heal the life of my life. Rend not from me what long, deep affection has woven into every thread of my being. God of heaven, bend, hear, be clement! And after all that strife, that bitter wrestling in the dark, the sun may rise to find them worsted. That dawn which once greeted them with zephyrs soft as a whisper and skylarks caroling high overhead may carry on its very first breath the thin, weak voice of the one who is slipping away, all colour and heat fled from their lips: “Oh, I have suffered so through the night. This morning I am worse. I tried to rise, and I could not. Strange, heavy dreams have troubled me.” Then the watcher leans over the pillow, sees that new, unrecognizable moulding on the familiar beloved features, knows at once the unendurable moment draws nigh, that God’s will is to break the idol they have worshipped, and bows their head, forcing their soul to submit to a sentence they cannot avert, and can scarce bear to endure.

Happy, thrice happy Mrs. Pryor! She was still bowed in prayer, unaware the full summer sun hung bright above the distant hills, when her child stirred softly and woke in her arms. No piteous, half-unconscious moaning — that sound which drains the strength so thoroughly that even if you have sworn to be firm, a flood of unconquerable tears sweeps the oath clean away — preceded her waking. No long stretch of deaf apathy followed, the kind that tells you the sick one’s mind is already wandering halfway to realms the living cannot reach. The first words Caroline spoke were clear, bright, unclouded by any distance from the world around her. She remembered perfectly every moment of her illness.
“Mamma, I have slept so well. I only dreamed and woke twice.”

Mrs. Pryor started to her feet at once, that the girl might not see the flood of joyful tears that rushed to her eyes at that soft, affectionate word “mamma”, and the blessed, welcome assurance that followed it. For many days after, she dared rejoice only with trembling. That first flicker of Caroline’s revival looked no more steady than the flare of a dying lamp: one moment the flame would leap up bright, and the next sink dim and low in the socket. Exhaustion followed close on the heels of even the smallest excitement. Caroline always made a touching effort to *appear* better, but too often her strength refused to obey her will; too often the gentle attempt to sit up, to smile, to chat a little, ended in her sinking back pale and breathless. Many an hour Mrs. Pryor feared the chords of her daughter’s life would never strengthen again, that only the final snap was being slowly delayed.

All that while, the mother and daughter were almost entirely alone in their little corner of Briarfield. It was the end of August, the weather “fine” in name only — parched, thick with dust, for an arid east wind had blown steadily for a full month, sucking every drop of freshness from the air. The sky was cloudless, but a pale, dull haze hung stationary in the atmosphere, robbing the blue of all its depth, the green grass of all its dewy glow, the daylight of all its warmth. Nearly every family in the parish was away on an excursion: Miss Keeldar and her party were at the seaside, so were Mrs. Yorke and all her household. Mr. Hall and Louis Moore, who had struck up a quiet, spontaneous friendship built on shared views and similar temperaments, were off on a walking tour north to the Lake District. Even Hortense, who had longed to stay home and help Mrs. Pryor nurse Caroline, had been entreated so earnestly by Miss Mann to join her at Wormwood Wells, where the insalubrious east wind had aggravated her old ailments terribly, that she could not refuse — it was never in her nature to turn down a request that appealed to both her kind heart and her quiet pride at being needed. As for Robert Moore, he had travelled on from Birmingham to London, and was still detained there on business.

As long as that east wind, sharp and dry as the breath of Asiatic deserts, parched Caroline’s lips and fed the fever in her veins, her physical recovery could not keep pace with the new calm returning to her mind. But then came the day the wind stopped its sobbing against the rectory’s eastern gable, and the old church’s oriel window. A little cloud, no bigger than a man’s hand, rose over the western horizon. Gusts of soft, damp wind from that same quarter drove it forward, spread it wide over the sky, and rain and tempest swept through for a short, wild while. When it passed, the sun broke out warm and genial: the sky regained its deep, vivid azure, the earth its lush, dewy green, that livid, cholera-like tint was gone from the face of nature. The hills rose sharp and clear all around the horizon, freed from that pale, choking, malaria-like haze.

Caroline’s youth could work its magic now, aided by her mother’s tireless, tender nurture. Both, crowned by God’s blessing, and fanned by the soft, fresh west wind blowing steadily through the always-open chamber lattice, rekindled her long-languishing energies. At last Mrs. Pryor dared to let herself hope: a genuine, solid, unshaken convalescence had truly begun. It was not merely that Caroline’s smile was brighter, or her spirits lighter — a certain dread, indescribable look had left her face and eyes, that look anyone who has sat by the bed of a dangerously ill person recognizes at once. Long before the gaunt, emaciated lines of her cheeks began to fill out, or the lost colour returned to her lips, a subtler change had taken hold: every line of her face grew softer, warmer. No longer was there the awful impression of a marble mask and glassy, unseeing eyes. Mrs. Pryor looked down to see a face pale and wasted, perhaps even more haggard than that frightening stillness had been, but far less terrible: it was a living, sick girl, not a cold white mould or rigid piece of statuary.

Now, too, Caroline no longer pleaded constantly for water. The plaintive cry of “I am so thirsty” fell silent. Sometimes, after she had swallowed a small morsel of food, she would say it had truly revived her. No longer did every kind of food taste equally loathsome; she could be induced, at times, to name something she would fancy. With what trembling pleasure, what anxious, careful hands did her mother prepare that little chosen treat! How closely she watched her while she ate it!
Nourishment brought strength. Soon she could sit up unassisted. Then she began to long to breathe the open air, to revisit her flower beds, to see how the fruit on the old trees had ripened. Her uncle, always generous, had bought a wicker garden-chair made expressly for her use. He carried her down the stairs in his own arms, settled her comfortably in it himself, and William Farren the gardener was there to wheel her round the garden walks, show her all the work he had done among her plants, and take her directions for all the little tweaks she wanted made.

William and Caroline had no end of things to talk over, a dozen shared interests that mattered not a whit to anyone else in the world, but were endlessly fascinating to them both. They shared the same soft spot for every sort of animal, bird, and insect, the same gentle doctrine about kindness to all living creatures, the same knack for noticing the tiniest, most trivial details of natural history. The little burrow of ground bees they had found tunneling in the turf under the old cherry tree was one favourite topic; the hidden nest of hedge-sparrows, and the welfare of their pearly eggs and fuzzy, newly hatched fledglings, was another. If *Chambers's Journal* had existed in those days, it would certainly have been their favourite periodical: Caroline would have subscribed for it, lent every copy faithfully to William, and both of them would have believed every word of its marvellous anecdotes about animal sagacity, and found endless joy in reading them. That digression aside, it explains perfectly why Caroline would let no one but William wheel her chair around the garden, why his quiet, earnest conversation made every air hour in the grounds feel like an adventure.

Mrs. Pryor, walking a little way behind them, wondered gently how her daughter could be so unreservedly at ease with a “man of the people”. She herself could not bring herself to speak to him with anything but stiff, distant formality. It felt to her as if a great, uncrossable gulf lay between their stations in life, that to meet him halfway would be to degrade herself. She asked Caroline softly, “Are you not afraid, my dear, to converse with that person so openly? He may presume upon your kindness, and become tiresomely garrulous.”
“William presume, mamma? You do not know him. He never presumes. He is far too proud and far too sensitive to do such a thing. William has the finest, gentlest feelings of anyone I know.”
Mrs. Pryor smiled sceptically at that naïve claim, that a rough-handed, fustian-clad working man could have “fine feelings”. For his part, Farren only gave Mrs. Pryor a sullen, set brow. He knew when he was being misjudged, and had a stubborn streak that rose up against anyone who refused to give him the respect he was due.

The evenings gave Caroline entirely back to her mother, and Mrs. Pryor loved those hours best of all: alone with her daughter, no other living soul stood between her and the thing she loved most in the world. During the day, she fell back into the stiff, formal demeanour that was lifelong habit for her. Between her and Mr. Helstone, they kept up a relationship of perfect respect, but rigid, unyielding ceremony: any trace of familiarity would have bred contempt in one or both of them at once, but by clinging strictly to civility and maintaining a careful distance, they got along very smoothly. Towards the servants, too, Mrs. Pryor was never unkind, but shy, cool, ungenial. It was diffidence far more than pride that made her seem so haughty, of course, but Fanny and Eliza could not make that distinction, and so she was thoroughly unpopular with them. She noticed the effect she had, and it left her sometimes dissatisfied with herself for faults she could not help, dejected, quiet, and cold.

But that mood melted away the moment Caroline turned to her, and only Caroline. The soft, dependent fondness of her nursling, the natural, easy affection of her own child, wrapped around her like warm sunlight. Her frost fell away, her rigidity unbent, and she grew smiling, soft, pliant. Caroline never made flowery, wordy professions of love — that would have ill suited Mrs. Pryor, who would have read such over-the-top speeches as proof of insincerity — she just hung on her arm with easy dependence, confided in her with fearless, total trust. Those small, quiet acts of affection were enough to fill the mother’s heart to bursting. She loved to hear her daughter say, “Mamma, do this”; “Please, mamma, fetch me that”; “Mamma, read to me”; “Sing a little, mamma”. No one else — not a single living soul — had ever claimed her services so naturally, looked to her for help with such total certainty. Everyone else was always as reserved and stiff with her as she was with them; everyone else noticed her odd little weak points, and let their annoyance show. Caroline was no more sharp to her about those incurable, constitutional flaws now than she had been when she was a tiny three-month-old baby at her breast.

Yet Caroline could find fault, artlessly, about the small, acquired habits that could be changed. On those points, she would gently lecture her mother, and far from being hurt, Mrs. Pryor felt a quiet, glowing pleasure to discover that the girl dared tease her, that she was so completely at home with her.
“Mamma, I am determined you shall not wear that old gown any more. Its fashion is not becoming, it is far too tight in the skirt. You shall put on your black silk every afternoon. You look so nice in that, it suits you perfectly. And you shall have a black satin dress for Sundays — real, proper satin, not cheap satinet or any of the shoddy imitations. And mamma, when you get the new one, mind you wear it.”
“My dear, I had thought that black silk would serve me as my best dress for many years yet, and I had planned to spend that money buying you several new things.”
“Nonsense, mamma. My uncle gives me more than enough cash to get everything I want. You know how generous he is. And I have set my whole heart on seeing you in that black satin. Get it soon, and let it be made by the dressmaker I recommend. Let me choose the pattern. You always try to disguise yourself like a frumpy old grandmother. You would have everyone believe you are old and ugly. Not at all! On the contrary, when you are well dressed and cheerful you are very comely indeed; your smile is so pleasant, your teeth are so white, your hair is still that pretty soft light colour. And you speak like a young lady, with such a clear, fine tone, and you sing better than any young lady I ever heard. Why do you wear such dowdy dresses and bonnets, mamma, that no other lady in the parish would ever be seen in?”
“Does it annoy you, Caroline?”
“Very much. It even vexes me. People say you are miserly, and yet you are not at all, for you give so liberally to the poor and to all the religious societies — though your gifts are conveyed so secretly that very few people know about them except the recipients. But I will be your own lady’s maid myself. When I get a little stronger I will set to work, and you must be good, mamma, and do exactly as I bid you.”
And Caroline, sitting close beside her, rearranged her mother’s crumpled muslin handkerchief, and smoothed back her soft hair.
“My own mamma,” she went on, as if wrapping herself in the sweet thought of their bond, “who belongs to me, and to whom I belong! I am such a rich girl now. I have something I can love fully, and never be afraid of loving. Mamma, who gave you this little brooch? Let me unpin it and look at it.”

Mrs. Pryor, who usually shrank from meddling fingers and anyone getting too close, allowed the familiar touch complacently.
“Did papa give you this, mamma?”
“My sister gave it to me — my only sister, Cary. I do so wish your Aunt Caroline had lived to see her namesake, her niece!”
“Have you nothing of papa’s — no trinket, no little gift of his that you kept?”
“I have one thing.”
“That you prize very much?”
“That I prize more than anything else in the world.”
“Valuable and pretty?”
“Invaluable, and sweeter to me than any gem.”
“Show it me, mamma. Is it here, or back at Fieldhead?”
“It is talking to me now, leaning against me. Its arms are round my neck.”
“Ah, mamma! You mean your teasing daughter, who will never let you alone; who, the moment you step out of your room, runs to find you, who follows you upstairs and down like a little loyal dog.”
“Whose fair features still give me a strange, soft thrill sometimes. I half fear your bright looks yet, child.”
“You do not. You cannot. Mamma, I am so sorry papa was not good. I do wish he had been. Wickedness spoils and poisons every pleasant thing. It kills love. If you and I thought the other was wicked, we could not love each other, could we?”
“And if we could not trust each other, Cary?”
“How miserable we would be! Mother, before I met you, I was afraid that you were not good, that I would not be able to esteem you. That dread dampened every bit of my wish to see you. But now my heart is so light and happy, because I find you perfect — almost perfect; kind, clever, so nice. Your only fault is that you are old-fashioned, and I am going to cure you of that. Mamma, put your sewing down. Read to me. I love your southern accent, it is so pure, so soft. It has none of that rough burr, no nasal twang that almost everyone here in the north has. My uncle and Mr. Hall both say you are a wonderful reader, mamma. Mr. Hall said he never heard any lady read with such perfect propriety of expression and such pure, clear accent.”
“I wish I could return the compliment, Cary; but really, the first time I heard your very good friend Mr. Hall preach, I could not understand a single word of his broad northern tongue.”
“Could you understand me, mamma? Did I sound rough to you?”
“Not at all. I almost wished you had, almost wished you looked a little unpolished. Your father, Caroline, naturally spoke very well, quite differently from your worthy uncle — correctly, gently, smoothly. You inherited that gift from him.”
“Poor papa! When he could be so agreeable, why was he not good?”
“Why he was as he was — and happily, child, you cannot even begin to imagine that sort of nature — I cannot tell. It is a deep mystery. The key to it is in his Maker’s hands. There I leave it.”
“Mamma, you keep stitching, stitching away. Put the sewing down. I am an enemy to it right now. It cumbers your lap, and I want it for my head to rest on; it takes up all your eyes, and I want them looking at your book. Here is your favourite — Cowper.”

These gentle, playful importunities were the greatest pleasure Mrs. Pryor had ever known. If ever she delayed complying, it was only to hear them repeated, to savour the soft, half-petulant, half-teasing urgency in her child’s voice. And when she yielded at last, Caroline would say archly, “You will spoil me, mamma. I always thought I should like to be spoiled, and now I find it is the sweetest thing in

By the time the Fieldhead house party trundled back to Briarfield through soft gray autumn drizzle, Caroline Helstone had almost recovered her strength after the weeks of wasting sickness that had nearly stolen her away. Shirley Keeldar, who had pored over every line of the postmark convalescence updates from her friend, barely shed her travelling cloak in her own hall before she had her bonnet tied tight again, determined not to let a single hour slip past before she called at the rectory.

The rain fell gentle but steady, pattering over the last of the late roses and the russet-edged bramble hedges, when the garden wicket creaked open and Caroline spotted Shirley’s familiar bright figure bobbing toward the window. When she stepped inside, the weight of her recent fear for Caroline came out not in giddy chatter, but in that quiet, intense way only Shirley had when her heart was wrung. She did not ramble or exclaim. She simply pulled Caroline straight into her arms, held her there for one long, searching look, pressed a single soft kiss to her forehead, and breathed, “You are better.”

A minute later, her voice still thick with suppressed feeling, she added, “I see you are safe now. But take care. God grant your health never has to bear another shock like that.”

She launched into bright, vivacious stories of their travels after that, but her eyes kept darting back to Caroline unbidden, every glance heavy with unspoken solicitude, quiet unease, and faint, lingering awe at how close her friend had come to the edge of the grave. “She may be mending,” that look said, “but how thin and weak she still is! What peril she walked through to get back to us.”

Suddenly her gaze snapped over to Mrs. Pryor, sharp and unwavering, piercing right through the quiet governess’s usual soft reserve. “When will my governess come home to me?” she asked.

Caroline glanced at her mother, hesitated, then asked permission to tell Shirley the whole truth. A quiet nod from Mrs. Pryor was all the sign she needed, and soon Shirley was fully apprised of the secret that had hung over the rectory for months: that Mrs. Pryor was no stranger’s widow, but Caroline’s long lost biological mother.

“Very good,” Shirley said, cool as a summer breeze, “very good. But this is no news to me.”

“What? You knew?” Caroline gasped.

“I guessed the whole truth ages ago,” Shirley told her. “I never heard it from Mrs. Pryor, of course, but I’ve picked up bits of her history from other folks. That afternoon I spent talking to Miss Mann? She told me every last detail of James Helstone’s life and character. Mrs. Yorke trots him out as one of her blood-red warning tales, too—one of the men she points to to scare young girls off of hasty marriage. I’d have thought both of them were just dramatizing the dark side of life, too, if I hadn’t asked Mr. Yorke straight out. He said to me, ‘Shirley, my lass, if you want to know about that James Helstone, all I can say is he was a man-tiger. Handsome, dissolute, soft-spoken, treacherous, courteous, cruel—’” She broke off, stroking Caroline’s hair gently. “Don’t cry, Cary. We’ll speak of him no more.”

“I’m not crying,” Caroline mumbled, wiping her cheeks, “or if I am, it’s nothing. Go on. You’re no friend if you hold back the full truth from me. I hate that cowardly habit of twisting and cutting the truth to spare people’s feelings.”

“Luckily I’ve nearly nothing left to tell,” Shirley said. “Your own uncle confirmed every word Mr. Yorke said, and he’s a man who scorns lies, never peddles those shabby little conventional evasions that are worse than outright falsehoods.”

“But papa’s dead now,” Caroline whispered. “They should leave him in peace.”

“They will. We will,” Shirley assured her, pulling her close. “Cry all you want, Cary. It’s wrong to hold back natural tears. And besides—” her voice softened, glancing over at Mrs. Pryor, whose eyes were glistening as she watched her long lost daughter weep “—every tear you cry blots out a little of that old sin. They’ve got the cleansing power the rivers of Damascus lacked. They’re like the Jordan, washing clean a leprous memory.”

She turned then to address Mrs. Pryor directly, a faint, amused smile tugging at her lips. “Did you think I could spend every day seeing you two together, marking how eerily similar you are in a hundred small ways, noticing the way you cannot hide how much you love her, whether she is in the room or not, and never put the pieces together? I did, and every last one of my guesses was correct. I do believe I’m far more sharp-witted than I give myself credit for.”

“And you never said a word?” Caroline asked, once she had steadied her breathing again.

“Not one,” Shirley said. “I had no right to. It was none of my business, so I did not make it my business.”

“You guessed such a deep secret, and you never even hinted you knew?” Caroline pressed.

“Is that so strange?”

“It’s not like you,” Caroline said, frowning. “You’re never reserved. You’re always so frank and open.”

“I can be frank and open, and still know where to stop,” Shirley laughed. “When I show off my treasures, I can still hold back a gem or two—some curious, unpolished graven stone, an amulet whose mystic glitter I rarely let even myself catch a glimpse of. Now, good day for now. I’ll come fetch you tomorrow, for a drive.”

It was a new side of Shirley Caroline had never seen before: quiet, secretive, far more careful with hidden truths than she had ever guessed. And soon, that new side of her would reveal more, far more.

As soon as Caroline was strong enough to handle a change of scene and the gentle excitement of new company, Shirley begged her to come stay at Fieldhead for as long as she liked. Whether Shirley had grown bored of her honoured relatives from the south no one could say, but she clung to Caroline’s company with an eagerness that made it plain an extra warm, sympathetic presence in that stiff, proper household was a gift she was grateful for.

The Sympsons were devout church people, of course, so the rector’s niece was welcomed with perfect, cold courtesy. Mr. Sympson was a man of spotless respectability, a fussy, worrying temper, strict pious principles, and very little patience for anything that did not fit his narrow view of the world. His wife was a good, gentle woman, patient and well-bred, but raised on a diet of small prejudices, starved of all warmth and joy, her excellent good sense trapped under a thick, indigestible crust of bigotry. She was far too submissive to ever complain about that meagre, bitter diet, or ask for a single crumb more.

Their two daughters were held up as perfect examples of young womanhood: tall, each with a stately Roman nose, educated to absolute faultlessness, their minds stuffed full of history and solid improving books, their opinions so perfectly polished there was not a single flaw to find in them. No one could have found two more regulated lives, feelings, manners, or habits anywhere. They knew by heart every tiny petty rule of the young ladies’ schoolroom code, never deviated from its rules, and regarded any small departure from it in other people with quiet, shuddering horror. To them, the great unforgivable sin, the Abomination of Desolation, was what other people called originality. Any spark of it—whether in a person’s glance, their words, their actions, even the vivid lively prose of a novel—made them recoil, as if they had stumbled on something unspeakably dangerous. If they could not understand it, it had to be bad. It had to be denounced, locked away, stamped out.

Their only son, Henry, was fifteen years old: small, lame, pale, with big eyes that were usually soft and dim, but could flare bright and blazing when his quiet emotions were roused. A flush could rise to his pale cheeks, a new sharp decision to his crippled movements, when something moved him deeply. His mother doted on him, convinced his peculiarities were a sign he was specially elected by God, a little new Samuel born to be a clergyman. Mr. Sympson and his two daughters never quite understood him, so they mostly left him alone. But Shirley made him her special pet, and he worshipped her as his dearest playmate.

Hovering at the edge of the Sympson family circle, connected but always apart, was the tutor: Louis Moore, satellite of the household, ever present, ever distant. Every member of that proper family treated him with perfectly correct, distant dignity. Mr. Sympson was austerely polite, sometimes sharp when his temper frayed; his wife was kind but formal, attentive to his needs but never treating him as an equal; the two daughters treated him as a walking abstraction, not a real man. The most vivid, witty sketch he ever drew might as well have been blank paper to them. The most clever, original thing he said fell on completely unhearing ears. Their behaviour was immaculate, and completely cold.

But nothing Caroline saw surprised her more than this: Shirley, who was always so quick to champion the underdog, did not treat Louis Moore any better. She was not haughty to him, exactly—but she barely acknowledged he existed. He came and went, spoke or fell silent, and she hardly ever so much as nodded at him. She let him keep to his quiet, isolated life under her roof as if he were no more than a shadow at the edge of the room.

As for Louis himself, he moved through the days like a man long used to this cold, distant treatment, who had long since made up his mind to bear it for as long as he must. All his sharp, brilliant faculties seemed walled up inside him, murmuring no complaint against their captivity. He never laughed, rarely even smiled, never spoke a word of grievance. He fulfilled all his tutoring duties scrupulously. Little Henry adored him, and he asked nothing more of the rest of the world than basic civility. He seemed determined to accept nothing more, here at least; for when Caroline tried to make gentle overtures of friendship, to reach out to her cousin, he avoided her instead of encouraging her. Only two living things in the whole house he showed softness to: his little crippled pupil Henry, and the great rough, ill-tempered mastiff Tartar, who was sullen and unfriendly to every other soul under the roof, but had conceived a fierce, loyal partiality for the quiet tutor. So strong was that loyalty, that once when Louis walked into the dining room for supper unacknowledged, Tartar stood up straight from his usual spot at Shirley’s feet, trotted over, and settled down right at Louis’s chair. Shirley had reached out her white hand, calling him softly to come back, but Tartar only slavered and sighed, then ignored her invitation completely, curling up at Louis’s feet. Louis lifted the dog’s great scarred, chewed black muzzle onto his knee, patted him, and smiled one tiny, private little smile, all to himself. An hour later, when Tartar had wandered back to Shirley’s feet at her footstool, one quiet word and small gesture from Louis made the dog prick up his ears, leap up, and trot back to him again for another caress, that quiet secret smile rippling across his face once more.

One day, when Caroline and Shirley were sitting alone in the little ivy-covered summer house at the bottom of the garden, Caroline turned to her and asked, “Shirley, did you know my cousin Louis was the Sympsons’ son’s tutor long before they moved down to Fieldhead?”

Shirley did not answer right away, slower than her usual quick, snappy responses. At last she said, “Yes. Of course. I knew that perfectly well.”

“I thought you must have,” Caroline said. “It puzzles me, though. How did you never mention it to me? You talk so freely, about everything—you never hold back little details like that.”

“Because it never came up,” Shirley laughed a little, a strange, breathless laugh.

“You are the most singular being,” Caroline said, shaking her head. “I thought I knew you inside out, but I keep finding new sides I never guessed at. You were silent as the grave about Mrs. Pryor’s secret, and now here is another one. I just cannot make out why you made a secret of this.”

“I never made a secret of it,” Shirley protested. “I had no reason to. If you had asked me who Henry’s tutor was, I would have told you straight away. Besides, I thought you already knew.”

“There are so many things I don’t understand about it,” Caroline said, frowning. “You don’t like poor Louis, do you? Why? Are you angry at what you see as his servile, lowly position? Do you wish Robert Moore’s brother was more highly placed, respected as he deserves?”

“Robert’s brother, indeed!” Shirley burst out, her tone sharp with sudden, unmistakeable scorn. She snatched a wild pink rose off the vine peeking through the summer-house lattice, her fingers crushing the soft petals in her sudden proud impatience.

“Yes,” Caroline said, steady and gentle, refusing to be put off. “Robert’s brother. He is closely related to Gérard Moore of Hollow’s Mill, true—nature did not give him Robert’s handsome face or his bold, noble bearing, but his blood is just as good. He is every bit a gentleman, if he only had the chance to be free.”

“Wise, humble, pious Caroline!” Shirley exclaimed, her tone thick with irony. “Men and angels, listen to her! We must never despise plain features, or honest, hard work, must we? Look at the object of your panegyric now,” she said, pointing through a gap in the ivy creepers, where Louis Moore was walking slowly down the garden path.

“He is not ugly, Shirley,” Caroline pleaded. “He is not ignoble. He is sad, his mind sealed up by all the years of silence and slights. But I know he is brilliant. And you can be certain, if he did not have something very good in his character, Mr. Hall would never seek out his company the way he does.”

Shirley laughed, once, then again, each laugh sharp with faint sarcasm. “Very well, very well,” she said. “If you say he is Cyril Hall’s friend and Robert Moore’s brother, we will tolerate his existence, won’t we, Cary? You think he’s intelligent, do you? Not quite a complete idiot? Some redeeming qualities in his character—meaning he’s not an utter ruffian? Good. Your good opinion counts for a lot with me. To prove it, if he comes this way, I will speak to him.”

Louis reached the summer house a moment later. Unaware anyone was inside, he sat down on the low stone step, Tartar settling across his feet as usual.

“Old boy,” he said, tugging gently at the dog’s torn, tattered ear, chewed to rags in a hundred old fights, “the autumn sun shines just as soft on us as it does on the richest, fairest people in the county. This garden is not ours, but we get to enjoy the green grass and the sweet flowers, don’t we?”

He fell silent, stroking the dog, while little sparrows twittered in the trees around them, hopping out onto the grass at a shy distance, as if waiting for something. “The little brown elves remember I fed them biscuit crumbs the other day,” Louis said to himself, quiet. “They want more. But I forgot to save a crumb for them today. Eager little things, I’ve got nothing to give you.”

He reached into his pocket, pulled it out empty.

“Your providence just showed up,” Shirley whispered to Caroline, quiet. She pulled a crumb of sweet cake from her reticule—she always kept the little bag stuffed full of treats for sparrows, stray kittens, hungry lambs—and crumbled it up, leaning over Louis’s shoulder to drop the crumbs right into his open palm.

“There,” she said. “There’s a little provision for the improvident.”

“This September afternoon is very pleasant,” Louis said, not even flustered or surprised, as he scattered the crumbs over the grass for the waiting birds.

“Even for you?” Shirley asked.

“Just as pleasant for me as it is for any king in the world,” he said.

“You take some harsh, solitary triumph in pulling joy out of the sun, the sky, the animals, the things no one else can take from you,” Shirley said.

“Solitary, but not harsh,” he corrected her. “With animals, I feel like the heir of Adam, the man who was given dominion over every living thing that moves on the earth. Your dog follows me. When I walk through the farmyard, the pigeons from your dovecot flutter at my feet. Your mare in the stable knows me better than she knows you, and obeys me more quickly.”

“And my roses smell sweet to you, and my trees give you shade,” Shirley said.

“And none of those things can be taken away by anyone’s silly whims,” Louis said. “They are mine.”

He walked off, Tartar trotting loyally at his heels, and Shirley stayed standing on the summer house step. Caroline saw her face then, pale as snow, as if her proud heart was bleeding somewhere deep inside.

“You see,” Caroline said, trying to explain, “people hurt his feelings so often it makes him morose and withdrawn.”

“You see,” Shirley snapped back, sharp with unmistakeable irritation, “he is a subject that you and I will quarrel over if we keep discussing it. So let us drop the subject. For ever.”

Caroline nodded, but her head was full of questions. She thought to herself: He must have snubbed her a dozen times over, that is why she is so distant with him. But Shirley is never usually so unforgiving, never so quick to be irritable and unfair.

Her good opinion of Louis only grew stronger, when two of her friends spoke up to praise him. William Farren, the poor cottager Louis had visited with Mr. Hall, swore he was a real gentleman—there was not another one like him in all of Briarfield. “I’d do anything for that man,” William said. “The children swarm around him the

Shirley Keeldar and her uncle Mr. Sympson were two sparks that could never be hammered into the same anvil, not if you heated them to a white hot fury. He was short-tempered and tyrannical, convinced every person under his roof ought to bend to his will like wet willow; she was spirited, free to her bones, ready to snap back at the first whiff of control. He measured every man and every marriage in hard, cold pounds sterling, while Shirley walked through the world chasing the kind of bright, romantic fire most people had long forgotten existed.

He had not come down to Yorkshire on a casual visit. His mission was as clear as the ink on the legal papers he kept tucked in his coat pocket: marry his niece off to a "suitable" man, hand over all the tedious responsibility of managing her and her fortune to a proper husband, and wash his hands of her wild, willful presence for good.

The only flaw in his plan was that Shirley had never, not even when she was seven years old and he tried to make her wear scratchy woollen stockings that itched, agreed that his definition of "suitable" matched hers at all. No one could say if she would break that streak now, on the most important choice of her whole life.

The test arrived far sooner than he expected.
Mr. Wynne came calling, formally requesting Shirley's hand for his son Samuel Fawthrop Wynne.
"Decidedly suitable! The most proper match imaginable!" Mr. Sympson boomed, slamming his fist lightly on the oak parlour table, so sure of his victory he was already mentally tallying the combined estates. "A fine unencumbered property, solid wealth, impeccable family connections. This will happen."
He summoned Shirley to the panelled oak parlour, shut the door behind them, laid the offer out flat, stated his own unshakable approval, and demanded she give her consent immediately.

She refused outright.
"No. I will not marry Samuel Fawthrop Wynne."
"I must have a reason!" he sputtered. "In every conceivable way he is more than worthy of you."
She stood straight on the hearth, as pale as the white marble mantel slab behind her, her eyes wide and bright, no trace of a smile in them.
"And I ask in what universe that young man could ever be worthy of me?"
"He has twice your fortune! Twice your common sense! Equal standing, equal respectability!"
"Had he a hundred times my money, I would never vow to love him."
"Explain your objections at once."
"He has spent years wallowing in cheap, despicable, commonplace profligacy. That is the first reason I will not so much as look at him."
"Miss Keeldar, you shock me to the core!"
"That behaviour alone sinks him so far below me there is no way he could climb back up to my level. His intellect could not reach the lowest standard I respect – that is the second barrier. His views are narrow, his feelings are dull, his tastes are coarse, and his manners are vulgar, through and through."
"The man is a respected, wealthy gentleman! To turn him down is pure arrogance on your part."
"I refuse, point blank. Stop pestering me about this subject. I forbid you to bring it up again."
"Do you ever intend to marry, or do you intend to live out your days a lonely old maid?"
"I deny you have any right to ask me that question at all."
"Do you expect some titled peer of the realm to come begging for your hand?"
"I doubt there is a single peer alive on this green earth I would condescend to marry."
"If there was madness in our family line, I would swear you had lost your mind. Your eccentricity and conceit are one step away from full blown frenzy."
"Maybe before this conversation ends, you will see me leap right over that line."
"I have no doubt of it! Wild, impractical girl! Take warning! I will not let you sully our family name with a cheap, disgraceful misalliance!"
"Our name? Uncle, my name is Keeldar. Not Sympson."
"Thank the good Lord you are not! But be on your guard. I will not be made a fool of."
"What, under all the laws of common sense and the land, could you possibly do if I chose a husband you disapproved of?" she asked, tilting her chin.
"Take care! Take care!" he warned, his voice and his raised hand both trembling.
"Why? What power do you hold over me? What reason have I to fear you?"
"Take care, madam!"
"I will be very careful, Mr. Sympson. Before I marry anyone, I am resolved to esteem him, admire him, and love him – with my whole heart."
"Preposterous drivel! Unbecoming! Unwomanly!"
"I know I speak a language you have never heard before. I do not care if you understand me or not."
"And if this grand great love of yours lands on a beggar?"
"I would never fall for a beggar. Mendicancy is not worthy of respect."
"On a low clerk, a play actor, a hack writer, or – or –"
"Go on, Mr. Sympson. Or what?"
"Some grubby literary scrub, some shabby, whinging artist."
"I have no taste for grubby, whinging nobodies. But I do love literature and the arts. I wonder how your precious Fawthrop Wynne would fit that life? He cannot write a simple note without spelling half the words wrong, he only reads the sporting papers, and he was the dunce of Stilbro' Grammar School!"
"Unladylike language! Great God, what is to become of her?" he cried, throwing his hands up to the ceiling.
"I will never stand at the altar beside Sam Wynne, that is certain."
"What is to become of her? Why are the laws not stricter, that I could force her to listen to reason?"
"Calm yourself, uncle. Even if Britain was a serfdom and you were the Czar, you could not force me to marry that man. I will write to Mr. Wynne myself. Do not trouble yourself over this matter any further."

---

Fortune is said to be fickle, but often her whims run in the same familiar pattern. By this point, Shirley Keeldar and her vast fortune had made such a stir across the district that three more formal marriage offers landed at Fieldhead not long after Mr. Wynne's. Each was pressed on her in turn by her uncle, and each was turned down flat – even offers from men of unblemished character and great wealth. Everyone in the village gossiped about what the stuck-up heiress could possibly be waiting for, calling her insolently fastidious.

At last, the gossips thought they had cracked the secret, and her uncle was absolutely certain he had. The revelation changed how he saw his niece entirely, and he adjusted his whole behaviour around her overnight. Fieldhead had grown far too tense for both of them to share; Shirley's soft-spoken aunt could never smooth over their fights, and her two cousins Gertrude and Isabella would huddle whispering in their dressing room for hours, chilled to the bone with decorous fear if they were ever left alone with their bold, unapologetic cousin. But now, Mr. Sympson was appeased, and the whole household grew quiet and peaceful again.

Not far away lay the village of Nunnely, with its ancient church, its sprawling forest, and its crumbling monastic ruins. It also held Nunnely Priory, a far grander stately home than any in Briarfield or Whinbury – and it had a baronet, a title no neighbouring parish could claim. For years the title had only been a name on a deed; the young new Sir Philip Nunnely had been living far away in Scotland, completely unknown on his Yorkshire estate.

While Shirley and her friends were staying at the fashionable seaside watering place of Cliffbridge that summer, they had been introduced to him. They ran into him again and again on the sands, along the cliff paths, and even at the public dances in the assembly rooms. He seemed lonely, his manners so unpretending he came off as shy rather than proud. He did not condescend to join their company – he was relieved to find it.

Shirley, who could make fast friends with any unassuming person, struck up an easy acquaintance with him immediately. They walked and talked for hours, and once her aunt and cousins even joined him for a sail on his yacht. She liked him for his kindness and modesty, and was charmed by how easy it was to make him laugh.

There was just one small flaw – no friendship is without one. Sir Philip had a literary streak. He wrote poetry: sonnets, stanzas, long meandering ballads. Shirley thought he was far too fond of reciting his own work, that the rhymes were clunky, the meter off, the metaphors stale and utterly lacking fire. She always winced when he brought up his poems, and did her best to steer the conversation elsewhere.

He would beg her to walk the moonlit pier with him at night, just to read her his longest ballad. He would lead her to quiet, hidden benches where they could hear the soft crash of the surf on the shore, the scent of rose gardens drifting around them and the tall cliffs rising at their backs, then pull out his latest batch of sonnets and read them in a voice trembling with emotion. He did not seem to realise he was writing rhyme, not poetry. Shirley would sit with her eyes downcast, her face tight with quiet mortification, because she did not want to hurt the feelings of this sweet, good man.

She tried again and again to gently wean him off his all-consuming obsession with the Muses. It was his only monomania; on every other subject he was perfectly sensible, and she was happy to talk to him about anything else. He would ask her about his neglected Nunnely estate, and she would talk for hours describing the ancient priory, the wild wooded park, the old grey church and the tiny hamlet, urging him to come home to Yorkshire, to gather his tenants around him in his ancestral halls.

To her surprise, Sir Philip took her advice exactly. At the end of September, he moved back to Nunnely Priory for good.
He came to call at Fieldhead soon after, and his first visit was far from his last. After making the rounds of all the neighbouring estates, he declared that no house felt half so warm and welcoming as the grey old Briarfield manor, even if it was far smaller and plainer than his own grand Priory.

Soon, sitting in Shirley's panelled parlour with other visitors around them no longer felt enough. He wanted her alone, walking the pastures, beside the slow still streams. Shirley avoided those private tête-à-tête rambles, so he organised large parties for her to explore his forests, the remote woodlands split by the Wharfe river, the green valleys watered by the Aire.

His constant, obvious attention made Shirley the most talked about woman in the whole district. Her uncle's greedy soul dreamed up a glorious future for her. He was already imagining the day he could casually drop references to his "nephew the baronet" to all his acquaintances, one leg propped lazily over the other. His niece was no longer a mad, reckless girl to him – she was the most sensible, superior woman he knew. He treated her with absurd deference, jumping to open and shut doors for her, getting red in the face and giving himself headaches bending to pick up her dropped gloves, her handkerchief, any small thing she happened to leave behind. He made silly little jokes about how women's wit was sharper than men's wisdom, making vague apologies for all the stupid mistakes he had made in how he handled her. He was as giddy as a rooster strutting on top of a dung heap.

Shirley watched all his silly manoeuvres and ignored his hints completely, acting like she barely understood what he was hinting at. When he outright accused her of being Sir Philip's chosen bride, she said she believed he liked her, and that she liked him back. She had never imagined a man of rank, an only son adored by his mother and spoiled by his sisters, could be so kind, and on the whole, so sensible.

Time proved Sir Philip truly did care for her. He had found in her that strange, rare charm that Mr. Hall the curate had once commented on. He sought her company more and more, until being near her felt like an indispensable habit. Around that time, a strange restlessness settled over Fieldhead – there were unspoken hopes, haggard quiet anxieties drifting through some of the upstairs rooms. Some of the household wandered the quiet surrounding fields at dusk, their nerves stretched tight with waiting.

Sir Philip was no man to be dismissed. He was gentle, if not dazzlingly intellectual, he was kind and sensitive. Shirley could never say of him what she had said of Sam Wynne: that his feelings were blunt, his tastes coarse, his manners vulgar. He had a real, if unrefined, love of the arts, and every inch an English gentleman in his deportment. His title and fortune were far grander than her own.
At first Shirley had made a few gentle, good-natured jokes about his looks – he was boyish, his features plain, his hair sandy, his stature unremarkable. But she never let anyone else tease him about it. She would flare up if anyone else made a rude comment, insisting he had a lovely face, and a heart far more valuable than a handsome profile or flowing locks. She still made the occasional soft joke about his bad poetry, but she would never let anyone else mock him for it.

Things had reached such a point that Mr. Yorke pulled the tutor Louis Moore aside one afternoon to speak plainly.
"Your brother Robert, now – he's either a fool or he's mad, far as I can see. Two months ago I would have sworn he had his whole future with Shirley Keeldar in the palm of his hand. And what does he do? Runs off to London and holes up there for weeks on end. By the time he comes back, he'll find he's been checkmated. There's a tide in the affairs of men, Louis – if you take it at the flood it leads you right to fortune, but if you let it slip it never comes back. I'd write to Robert if I were you, remind him of that."
"Robert had feelings for Miss Keeldar?" Louis asked, acting like the idea was completely new to him.
"I put the idea in his head myself, and he could have made it work. She liked him. More than liked him. I've seen her face flush, change colour, just at the sound of his name. Write to the boy, tell him to come home. He's a finer gentleman than that little soft baronet, after all."
"Does it not strike you, Mr. Yorke, that for a penniless tutor, a man with nothing to his name, to aspire to marry a rich heiress is presumptuous? Contemptible?" Louis asked, his jaw tight.
"Oh, if you want to go in for those lofty, over-refined sentiments I have nothing more to say. I'm a plain practical man. If Robert is happy to hand that grand prize over to that soft little aristocrat, that's his choice. At his age, in his position, I never would have let a baronet, a duke, a prince, steal my sweetheart without a fight. But you tutors are always such solemn fellows – talking to you is almost as stuffy as talking to a parson."

---

Flattered and fussed over as Shirley was in those weeks, she had not been spoiled. Everyone in the village had long stopped linking her name with Robert Moore, and her own apparent indifference to the absent miller seemed to confirm they had forgotten each other. But Shirley had not quite forgotten him – if not with love, then at least with deep, lingering interest. That much was clear when Louis Moore, Robert's quiet, stern tutor brother, came down with a sudden fever, and Shirley began showing him far more attention than she ever had before. She had always treated him with a strange mix of cool, distant reserve and meek, almost schoolgirlish respect – one minute sweeping past him in all the dignity of a wealthy heiress and soon-to-be Lady Nunnely, the next flushing and lowering her eyes like a shy student caught breaking the rules when he met her gaze.

Louis had caught the fever visiting the poor cottagers of the district, which he did regularly alongside Mr. Hall and his young pupil Henry Sympson. He tried to fight the illness silently for a day or two, before he was forced to take to his bed.
One evening he was tossing restlessly on his thin mattress, little Henry limping faithfully beside his bed to watch over him, when there was a soft, light tap on the door – too gentle to be Mrs. Gill the housekeeper or the housemaid.
"How is Mr. Moore tonight?" a low voice whispered from the dark gallery outside.
"Come in and see for yourself," Henry called back.
"Is he asleep?"
"I wish he could sleep. Come in, Shirley."
"He won't like me disturbing him."
But she stepped across the threshold anyway, and Henry, seeing her hesitate, took her hand and pulled her over to the bedside.
The shaded lamp did not show her fully, but it revealed she was dressed for the party downstairs. The guests were gathered in the drawing room, including Sir Philip Nunnely, and Shirley had slipped away from the other ladies to visit her old tutor. Her white silk dress, her bare fair arms and neck, the thin gold chain around her throat that trembled as she breathed, glowed softly in the dim sickroom light. Her face was gentle, pensive, her voice soft as a sigh.
"Mr. Moore, how are you feeling tonight?"
"I was never very ill. I am better now."
"I heard you were thirsty. I brought you some grapes. Won't you try just one?"
"No, thank you. But thank you for remembering me."
"Just one." She plucked a single ripe berry from the full basket she held and held it to his lips. He shook his head, turning his flushed face away.
"What else can I bring you? You don't want fruit, but your lips are dry. What would you like to drink?"
"Mrs. Gill brings me toast and water. That is all I want."
Silence stretched for a few minutes.
"Do you have pain?"
"Very little."
"What made you sick?"
Silence.
"I wonder what caused this fever? What do you think brought it on?"
"Miasma, probably. Autumn is always a bad season for fevers around here."
"I know you visit

Shirley must have enjoyed every moment of her previous evening with Sir Philip Nunnely, for she drifted down to breakfast the next morning glowing in the kind of unguarded, unselfconscious joy that graced her only on her very best days.
"Who’ll come for a walk with me?" she called cheerfully once the plates were cleared. "Isabella? Gertrude? Care to join?"
Such an invitation from Miss Keeldar to her two cousins was so rare that the girls hesitated, half-convinced they’d misheard. When their mother nodded her approval, they scrambled for their bonnets and cloaks, and the three set off together.
The trio had never been particularly suited to one another’s company. Shirley counted almost no ladies as friends, her only genuine, unreserved affection given over entirely to Mrs. Pryor and Caroline Helstone. She was always polite, kind, even attentive to her cousins, but usually she had little to say to them. That sunlit autumn morning, though, she wove even the most mundane small talk into something bright and captivating, her sparkling wit skittering across every casual phrase until the two quiet girls found themselves laughing, hanging on her every word, wondering how they’d never noticed how much fun she could be.
No obvious cause fed that joy. The day itself was dim, a pale, fading late autumn afternoon, the paths through the dark wood damp underfoot, the air heavy, the sky streaked with heavy overcast. It didn’t matter. Shirley carried all the blue, sun-drenched light of Italy in her chest, all its warm, unruly laughter glinting out of her grey English eyes.
On their way back to Fieldhead, she fell a few paces behind the cousins to pass a handful of quick, practical instructions to her foreman, John. Twenty minutes slipped by before she made her way up the drive again. By the time the bell for luncheon rang, she’d wandered lingering in the lane by the gate, and when the maid called her in to eat, she excused herself entirely and went straight up to her room.
"Isn’t Shirley coming down for luncheon?" Isabella asked, confused. "She said she was ravenous an hour ago."
When an hour passed and she still hadn’t left her chamber, Gertrude went to check on her. She found Shirley perched on the floor at the foot of her bed, head resting on one hand, pale, thoughtful, almost sad.
"You’re not ill, are you?" Gertrude fussed.
"Just a little queasy," Shirley replied.
The change in her from the bright, laughing girl who’d led them on the walk two hours earlier was staggering. This shift didn’t pass like a wisp of summer cloud. She joined them for dinner, chatted as usual, stayed with them the whole evening, laughed off all questions about her health as nothing more than a passing faintness, a silly momentary sensation not worth dwelling on. But everyone in the house could feel it: something fundamental had shifted in Shirley.
That strange, soft shadow lingered on her face, in her movements, even in the tone of her voice, for days, then weeks, then a full fortnight. A strange quietude settled over her, not deep enough to let anyone press her on it too often, but impossible to miss, heavy as a cloud no wind could ever blow away. It soon became clear that mentioning the change would annoy her. She’d shrink away from gentle concern at first, and if people persisted, her old sharp hauteur would flare up to push them back.
"Are you ill?" they’d ask, and she’d snap back sharp and firm: "I am not."
"Is something weighing on your mind? Did something happen to upset you?"
She’d scoff, mock the very idea. "What do you even mean by ‘spirits’? I don’t have a stash of black or white or blue spirits floating around my head waiting to get upset."
"You must be different for a reason, you’re not yourself at all."
"I’m allowed to change if I please. I know I look plainer. If I want to let myself grow less pretty, why on earth should anyone else lose sleep over it?"
"What’s the cause, Shirley? Tell us what’s wrong."
And then she’d order them sharply to leave her alone.
She’d force herself to be bright and gay around everyone else, and seethe at herself when she couldn’t pull it off perfectly, muttering sharp, angry little insults at herself when she was alone. *Fool! Coward!* she’d hiss. *Poltroon! If you’re going to tremble, tremble where no one can see you. Don’t quail where any eye can catch you.*
"How dare you," she’d berate her own reflection, "show your weakness, betray your silly, silly anxieties? Shake them off. Rise above them. If you can’t do that, at the very least hide them."
And hide them she did. She threw herself back into being resolutely lively in company, and when the effort wore her too thin, she fled out of the house instead of moping locked in her bedroom. She’d mount her mare Zoë and ride out for hours at a time, half-day long treks over the moor. Her uncle disapproved, but he never dared argue with her. Facing Shirley’s anger had never been pleasant, even when she was well and bright, but now that her face was growing thin, her large eyes hollowed out, the dark flash of her temper was as unsettling as it was achingly sad.
To every casual stranger who commented on how gaunt she looked, she gave the same flat reply: "I’m perfectly well. I haven’t a single thing wrong with me."
And she had to be tough as nails to weather the exposure she put herself through. Rain or shine, gale or calm, she rode daily across Stilbro’ Moor, her tartan terrier Tartar loping at her side with his endless, wolf-like gallop.
Twice, three times, gossips’ prying eyes spotted her veering away from her usual route up Rushedge ridge, riding straight for the little town below. It didn’t take long for busybodies to track her destination: she dismounted every time at the office of Mr. Pearson Hall, a solicitor whose family had handled all of the Keeldar estate’s affairs for generations. Rumours flew. Some said she’d sunk money into bad speculations for Hollow’s Mill and lost a fortune, forcing her to mortgage Fieldhead. Others whispered she was secretly engaged, drawing up marriage settlements.
* * * * *
Louis Moore and young Henry Sympson were alone in the schoolroom. The tutor waited for his pupil to finish preparing his lesson, but the boy sat hunched over his book, not turning a single page.
"Henry, hurry up," Moore said gently. "The afternoon’s getting away from us."
"Is it, sir?"
"Certainly. Are you nearly done with your translation?"
"No."
"Not even nearly?"
"I haven’t construed a single line."
Moore looked up. There was a tight, strange tremor in the boy’s voice.
"The work isn’t hard, Henry. If you’re stuck, show me. We’ll work through it together."
"Mr. Moore, I can’t work at all."
"Are you feeling unwell, my boy?"
"I’m no more sick in body than usual, but my heart’s so full I can barely breathe."
"Shut the book. Come over here to the fireside."
Henry limped across the room. Louis pulled a chair out for him, and the boy’s lips were already quivering, his eyes brimming with tears. He set his crutch on the floor, dropped his head in his hands, and sobbed.
"This grief isn’t from physical pain, you say? Tell me what’s weighing on you."
"Sir, it’s the worst grief I’ve ever had. I wish I could make it go away. I can barely stand it."
"Maybe if we talk it through together, we can lighten it. What’s the cause? Who does it concern?"
"It’s Shirley, sir. It’s all about Shirley."
"You think she’s changed, don’t you?"
"Everyone who knows her notices. You do too, Mr. Moore."
"Not seriously," Moore said, even as his chest tightened. "I’ve not seen anything that a few good weeks of rest couldn’t fix. She swears herself she’s perfectly well, after all."
"That’s the trouble!" Henry cried. "As long as she kept saying she was fine, I believed her. I’d be sad when I was away from her, but the second I stepped up to her I’d feel light again. But now…"
"Go on, Harry. What happened? The two of you were talking in the garden for two hours this morning, I saw you. If she told you she was sick and made you swear to secrecy, don’t keep that promise. For her sake, tell me everything. Speak, my boy."
"Her say she’s ill! I’m convinced, sir, that if she was lying on her deathbed she’d still smile and say nothing at all ails her."
"Then what have you found out? What new thing did she tell you?"
"She made her will. Just a few days ago."
Louis froze, and the two of them sat silent for long minutes.
"She told you that?" Moore asked at last.
"She told me like it was the most ordinary, unthreatening thing in the world, though I felt every word like a punch to the chest. She said only her solicitor Mr. Hall, Mr. Helstone, Mr. Yorke, and I know about it, and she wanted to explain every part of it to me, specially."
"Go on, Harry."
"‘Because,’ she said, looking down at me with her beautiful eyes—oh Mr. Moore, they’re the most beautiful eyes in the whole world! I love her, she’s my star! Heaven can’t take her! She’s made for this world, she’s not some distant angel, she’s a real woman who belongs here with us. Even if an archangel with wings as wide as the sky came down to claim her for heaven, I’d fight him off, boy and cripple as I am."
"Henry Sympson, go on when I tell you to."
"‘Because,’ she said, ‘if I died without a will before you came of age, all my property would pass straight to you, and I won’t let that happen, even though your father would be over the moon about it. But you’ll inherit your father’s whole estate one day, it’s larger than Fieldhead, you’ll never want for anything. Your sisters, though, will get nothing from their father, so I left them a sum of money each, even though I don’t love the two of them half as much as I love a single lock of your fair hair.’ She called me her darling, and let me kiss her hand, Mr. Moore. She said she left Caroline Helstone a nice little legacy too, that this old manor house, all its books and furniture, would pass straight to me, because she didn’t want to take the old family home away from her own blood line. And all the rest of her property, twelve thousand pounds after all the other legacies are paid? She didn’t leave it to me, because I’m already rich enough. She left it to a good man, she said, who would use it better than any other person alive: a man gentle and brave, strong and merciful, who might not shout about his piety to the whole parish, but who lives a pure, unshowy kind of faith that cares for widows and orphans, who never lets the world turn him hard. Then she asked me ‘Do you approve of what I’ve done, Harry?’ I couldn’t answer. I was crying too hard to speak."
Louis let the boy catch his breath for a minute before he spoke again. "What else did she say?"
"When I told her I thought every part of her will was perfect, she said I was a generous boy, that she was proud of me. ‘And now,’ she said, ‘if anything ever happens to me, you’ll know what to say when people whisper lies about Shirley, saying I wronged you, that I never loved you. You’ll know I did love you, Harry—no sister could have loved you better than that, my own treasure.’ Mr. Moore, just thinking about her voice, her face that morning, my heart feels like it’s going to burst out of my chest. She might go to heaven before me, if God calls her home, and that’s His right—but every day of the rest of my life, and I know my life won’t be long, I’ll walk straight down the path her feet already trod. I always thought I’d be buried in the Keeldar family vault before her. If it turns out the other way, promise me you’ll lay my coffin right by her side."
Moore spoke slow, calm, steady, a deliberate contrast to the boy’s wild, aching devotion.
"You’re both being silly, you’re scaring yourselves for no reason. When young people get caught in the grip of a shadowy terror, they convince themselves the sun will never shine again. You think this one little shadow will last forever. What else did she say? Anything else?"
"We settled a few little family things between us."
"I’d very much like to hear what they were."
"But you’re smiling, sir. I couldn’t even crack a smile seeing Shirley look like that."
"Harry, I’m not excitable, I’m not prone to poetic fancies, and I’m far older than you. I see things as they really are, which you don’t yet. Tell me those little family points."
"Only, sir, she asked me if I considered myself more a Keeldar or a Sympson, and I told her I was Keeldar all the way down to my bones. She was so happy to hear it, she said I was the only other Keeldar left in all of England besides her. Then we agreed on a few things."
"Go on."
"If I live long enough to inherit my father’s estate and her house, I’ll change my last name to Keeldar, and make Fieldhead my permanent home. I’ll be Henry Shirley Keeldar. Her name, her home, they’re ancient, the Sympsons and Sympson Grove are nothing new, nothing compared to that."
"Neither of you are going anywhere near heaven any time soon. I’ve got very high hopes for both of you, you pair of half-fledged eaglets. Now, tell me what you make of all this. Say it out plain."
"That Shirley’s convinced she’s going to die."
"She never mentioned her health once to you?"
"Not once. But look at her, sir! She’s wasting away. Her hands are thin, her cheeks are sunken."
"Has she ever complained to your mother or your sisters?"
"Never. She laughs them off when they ask if she’s unwell. She’s such a strange, wonderful creature—soft, girlish, nothing like a loud, domineering Amazon, and yet she’s too proud to take help or sympathy from anyone."
"Do you know where she is right now, Henry? Is she in the house, or riding out?"
"Surely she’s not out, sir. It’s pouring rain."
"It is pouring, but that’s never stopped her before. Lately, no weather, no matter how wild, has kept her from riding."
"Remember last Wednesday? The storm was so bad she wouldn’t even let Zoë be saddled, the wind was that brutal. But the gale she thought was too rough for her mare she walked straight out into on foot. She tramped all the way to Nunnely and back. I asked her if she wasn’t scared of catching a terrible cold. ‘Not I,’ she laughed. ‘Catching a good fever and drifting off like any normal Christian would be far too lucky for me. I don’t know, Harry, but the best thing that could happen to me right now would be to fall ill and slip away.’ She’s reckless, sir."
"Reckless doesn’t even cover it. Go find her. If you can ask her to come here for a minute without drawing anyone else’s attention, do it."
"Yes sir."
He grabbed his crutch and started for the door.
"Harry!"
He turned back.
"Don’t make the message sound formal, like I’m summoning her for a meeting. Say it like you used to, the same way you’d call her to the schoolroom for a silly French reading lesson. She’ll be far more likely to agree."
"I understand, sir."
"And Harry—"
"Yes?"
"I’ll call you if I need you. You’re excused from the rest of your lessons today."
Henry left. Louis stood up from his desk the second the door clicked shut.
"I can act all cool and superior with Henry," he muttered to himself, pacing the room. "I can act like his silly little fears are nothing, like the pair of them are just overexcited children. Let’s see if I can keep that act up with her. I’ve come so close to slipping before, nearly letting my composure shatter, nearly letting her see that I’m not her calm, stoic tutor at all, that I’m just a man so wrapped up in her I can barely think straight. I can’t afford to be a fool. Sir Philip Nunnely can blush every time he meets her eye, he can tremble when their hands brush, no one would think less of him for it. But I’m poor. If I let myself show how soft I am for her, everyone would think I’m just a grasping fortune hunter, after her money. I’ve sat right next to her a hundred times and never shaken, never broken composure, not once. I’ve never even touched her hand, I’ve never put myself to that test. I’m no servant of hers, I’m no serf—but I have to guard my self-respect, not give up an inch of it. Those little comments she used to make about cold people who turn flesh to marble? They made my chest feel light, for no reason I’d ever dare admit. I don’t let myself dwell on what she means, or stare too long at her face, because if I did I’d stop believing in plain sense and start believing in romance. Sometimes a silly, secret joy runs right through my veins. I don’t encourage it. I refuse to even remember it. I’ll keep my head, I swear it."
He paused, listening.
"Is she coming? Or is she going to refuse me?" he wondered. "Will she laugh it off, or turn me away haughtily? She’s half wild child, half queen, anything could happen."
"If she does come, what on earth am I going to say to her? How do I

Louis Moore had long grown accustomed to the hush of a quiet life. A man of few words by nature, he bore its constraints far better than most men would have managed, for he carried a whole vivid, thrumming world of thought and feeling locked inside his own head and heart. Confinement to the small, still corner of the real world he occupied as a paid tutor never chafed him long—until now.

How ghostly quiet Fieldhead hangs this evening. Every soul but Moore is gone to Nunnely: Miss Keeldar, all the Sympsons, even young Henry. Sir Philip Nunnely had insisted they all come to meet his mother and sisters, newly arrived at the priory, and in his good nature he had extended an invitation to the tutor too. Louis would sooner have struck a tryst with the ghost of the old Earl of Huntingdon and his band of shadowy merry men, under the gnarled blackest oak deep in Nunnely Forest, than sit through that stiff, polite supper. He would far rather wander the weedy, crumbling ruins of the old abbey, keeping company with mist-pale phantom nuns, than suffer the benevolent but stern baronet’s mother, or his patrician, icy sisters, or a single one of the fussy Sympson clan.

Tonight he longs for someone, something, to be close beside him—but none of them.

The equinox still rages through its storms. The wild lashing rains of the day have ebbed, and a great single cloud has torn apart and rolled back from the sky, not to leave a flat sapphire sea of heavens in its wake, but to reveal a long, high, roaring tempest of moonlight. The moon rides glorious, wild with gale, as if she has thrown herself gladly into the wind’s fierce, hungry caress. No Endymion will wait for his goddess on the hills tonight; no flocks graze the high pastures, and it is well, for tonight she embraces Æolus himself.

Sitting in his cramped schoolroom, Louis hears the storm howl around the far gable and batter the hall front, but his little corner is tucked sheltered, quiet. He wants no shelter. He craves no soft muted sounds, no screened, safe little nook.
“Every parlour in this house is empty,” he murmurs to the dark. “This cell makes me sick at heart.”

He slips out of the schoolroom, and wanders to the front rooms, where the casements are wide and unobstructed, no leafy branches blocking the view of the wild autumn night sky: deep bruised indigo, streaked with silver-fleeced clouds tearing past, the whole world thrumming with gale. He carries no candle—no lamp, no fire is needed. The broad, wavering, moonbeam spills silver over every floor and wall, bright enough to see by.

He drifts from parlour to parlour, as if he is following a ghost through the empty rooms. He stops in the old oak room. Unlike the formal, chilly salon, this space is warm. The hearth glows ruddy hot, cinders tinkling in the intense clear glow of the embers, and pulled up near the rug is a little work table: a desk sits atop it, and a chair drawn close. It looks as if the ghost he has been tracking has just risen from that chair a minute before. Anyone who saw him standing there, leaning forward, would see the bright sharp interest flare in his eyes, the soft, unguarded weight of feeling on his face, and think he had just found a living companion in the silent house, and is about to speak.

He spots the small satin bag hanging off the back of the chair. The desk is open, the keys still dangling in the lock. Scattered across the top are tiny, charming trifles: a pretty carved seal, a slim silver pen, a couple of bright crimson bramble berries resting on a green leaf, a small, soft, delicate kid glove. Order would tuck all these little messy things away out of sight, but these little unplanned details make the whole room feel alive.
“Her mark,” he whispers. “Here she was—careless, bright, bewitching thing—called away in a rush, and forgot to come back and tidy up. Why does she leave fascination in every footprint she leaves behind? How is it that she can be heedless, and never once offend? There is always some little thing to chide her for, but that reprimand never hardens into true displeasure in the chest. For the man who loves her, for her husband, any sharp word would melt right off his lips into a kiss after a moment. Half an hour spent gently remonstrating with her is better than a whole day of admiring any other woman alive. Am I muttering to myself? Stop that.”

He stills, standing quiet for a minute, then sets about making himself comfortable for the evening. He draws the heavy curtains shut over the wide window, shutting out the regal moon, the gale, all the starry sky. He heaps more logs onto the fast-dying fire, lights one of the pair of beeswax candles on the table, pulls a second chair up opposite the one she had been sitting in, and sinks down. From his pocket he pulls a small, thick blank book, hidden away for years, and a pencil, and begins to write in a tight, neat, cramped hand no one else would easily read.
Lean in, reader. Do not be shy. Look over his shoulder, fearless, and read every word:

It is nine o’clock. The carriage will not return before eleven, I am certain. I have two full hours of freedom. Two hours where I may occupy her room, sit opposite her chair, rest my elbow on her table, have all her little sweet mementoes scattered around me.
I used to love solitude, once. I used to imagine it a quiet, fair nymph, an Oread coming down to me from lonely mountain passes, her robes stitched through with blue hill mist, her breath sharp with high cold wind, her whole bearing steeped in solemn beauty. I used to court her calmly, think my heart was lighter when I held her to me, all silent and majestic.
But ever since that day I called S. to me in the schoolroom, and she came and sat close at my side, ever since she opened the trouble weighing on her mind, asked for my protection, appealed to my strength—ever since that hour I have abhorred solitude. It is a cold abstraction, a fleshless skeleton, daughter, mother, and mate of Death.
It is such sweet relief to write of what is as dear to me as the core of my own heart. No one can take this little book from me. Through this pencil, I can say what I dare not utter to any living soul, what I dare not even let myself think aloud when anyone else is near.
We have barely crossed paths since that evening. Once, when I was alone in the drawing room looking for one of Henry’s books, she walked in, dressed all in silk for the concert at Stilbro’. It was her shyness, not mine, that drew a soft silver veil between us. I have heard so much empty cant about “maiden modesty” in my life, but those words, when they are not hackneyed, are perfect for her. When she passed the window, after greeting me with that quiet, graceful little nod of hers, I could think of no name for her but “stainless virgin.” I could see a soft, delicate splendour wrapping around her, the shyness of girlhood glowing as a halo around her head. I am no handsome man, no clever charmer, but that little shyness of hers touched me deep, flattered the finest, softest parts of me. I must have looked like a stupid block standing there, but I was thrumming with a kind of paradise life when she turned her gaze away from mine, and softly averted her head to hide the flush rising in her cheeks.
I know this is the talk of a dreamer, a rapt romantic lunatic. But I do dream. I will dream, now and then. If she has woven romance into my staid, prosaic bones, how can I help it?
What a child she can be, sometimes! What an unsophisticated, untaught, warm thing she is. I can see her now, looking up into my face, begging me to make sure no one smothers her, to give her a strong narcotic to bear it if they try. I can see her confessing that she is not half so self-sufficient, so independent of other people’s sympathy, as the whole world thinks she is. I see the secret tear drop soft and quiet from her eyelash. She told me once she thought I saw her as childish. I did. She thought I despised her. Despised her! It was unutterably sweet to feel myself at once close to her and able to hold her up, to feel that natural right and power to sustain her, the way a husband ought to sustain his wife.
I worship her perfections, but it is her little foibles, her tiny faults, that bring her close to me, nestle her right into my heart, wrap her up in my love. For a selfish, deeply natural reason: those little faults are the steps by which I climb to a place of belonging in her life. If she was a trimmed, artificial, flat mound with no uneven ground, what foothold would there be for me to climb?
