She is a wild natural hill, all mossy breaks and soft hollows, her slope inviting ascent, her summit the sweetest pleasure to reach.
To drop the metaphor: It delights my eyes just to look at her. She fits me, as if she was made for me. If I were a king, and she the scullery maid sweeping my palace stairs, across all that gulf between our stations, my eyes would still pick her out, my pulse would still beat true for her, even if we could never speak. If I were a gentleman, and she waited on me as a servant, I could not help but like that Shirley. Take away all her education, her fine gowns, all her privilege, all grace but the natural ease that comes of her own fine, straight frame. Show her to me at a cottage door, in a plain stuff gown, offering me a cup of water with that same smile, that same warm good will she uses to dispense grand manorial hospitality now. I should like her. I should linger an hour to talk to that rustic girl, leave her with regret, and come back again whenever I could.
How recklessly careless of her to leave her desk open, where I know she keeps money! The keys in that lock open every one of her repositories, even her jewel casket. I can see the silver bead tassel of her purse sticking out of that little satin bag. My brother Robert would be furious if he saw it. All her little failings would irritate him no end. But if they vex me, it is the softest, most pleasurable vexation imaginable. I delight to find her at fault. If I lived here full time, I know she would never stint to give me just that little thing to correct, a theme for my quiet tutor’s lectures. I never lecture Henry, never want to. If he does something wrong, which is very rarely, a single word is enough. Most times I just shake my head. But the moment I catch that mutinous little pout on her face, all the words for gentle expostulation crowd to my lips. I, who am so taciturn, think she would turn me into a man who talks for hours. The more mischievous, teasing, unmanageable she gets, the more reason I have to gently disapprove of her, the more I want to seek her out. She is never wilder than when she is kitted out in her riding habit and hat, never less governable than when she and her dog Zoë come tearing back fiery from a race with the wind across the hills. I can confess it to this mute page: I have waited an hour in the courtyard just for the chance to watch her ride back, and the far sweeter chance to catch her in my arms as she dismounts from the saddle. I have noticed, and I would never say this to anyone but this book: she will never let any other man help her down. I have seen her politely turn down Sir Philip Nunnely’s offer, be so gentle to his thin, sensitive pride that he never even noticed the refusal. I have seen her haughtily reject Sam Wynne’s outstretched hand. But she lets me lift her down, unresisting, unhurried. Does she know how my strength sings to serve her? I am no slave to her, I swear it, but all my knowledge, all my prudence, all my calm, all my power, gather at her presence like genii summoned by the glisten of a lamp, waiting humbly for a task. How glad they are when she gives them one! What joy they take in the smallest toil she assigns! Does she know that?
I called her careless earlier. It is remarkable how that carelessness never dims her refinement. The holes in her character prove how genuine that refinement is, not just a polished facade. A whole, perfect garment can hide meagreness and malformation, but a torn sleeve reveals a fair, round, unblemished arm. I have handled so many of her stray belongings, left scattered all over the house, and I have never found a single thing that does not mark her as a lady—nothing sordid, nothing soiled. She is as unthinking in some ways as she is scrupulous in others. Even as a peasant girl, she would always be trim, spotless, neat. Look at the pure, unmarked kid of this little glove, the fresh, unsullied satin of her bag.
What a world of difference there is between S. and that sweet pearl Caroline Helstone! Caroline is the very soul of punctuality and exactitude, the kind of woman who would suit my fastidious brother Robert down to the ground—delicate, dexterous, quiet, every task done to the minute, every possession arranged to a straw’s breadth. But what on earth would I do with something so nearly perfect? She is my equal in station, poor as I am, pretty too, a little Raphael-like head of hers, all English softness and purity, a lily of the valley, no tint needed, no flaw to fix. What could I possibly change about her? What pencil would dare to add a single stroke? My sweetheart, if I ever have one, must be far closer to a rose: sweet, vivid, guarded by sharp, exciting thorns. My wife, if I ever marry, must stir my big strong frame with a sting now and then, must give my vast reserve of patience something to use itself on. I was not made to endure a lamb for a mate. I would find far more joy, more purpose, in the care of a young lioness or leopardess. I like few sweet things that are not also pungent, few bright things that are not also hot. I like the summer day where the sun makes the fruit blush and the corn ripen golden. Beauty is never so beautiful as when, if you tease it, it wriggles back at you with spirit. Fascination is never so powerful as when, half roused, half angry, it threatens to turn fierce. I fear I would tire very quickly of the mute, unchanging innocence of a lamb. I would find the nestling dove that never stirred in my arms burdensome after a while. But my patience would revel in stilling the flutters, training the wild energy of a restless merlin, taming the instincts of a wild, untamed creature. My powers would feast on that work.
Oh my pupil! My Peri! Too mutinous for heaven, too innocent for hell, I will never do more than see you, worship you, wish for you. Alas, to know I could make you happy, is it my doom to watch you fall into the hands of men who have no power to do that at all?
However kind a hand may be, if it is feeble, it cannot bend Shirley. And she must be bent. It cannot curb her. And she must be curbed.
Beware, Sir Philip Nunnely. I never see you walk or sit at her side, and watch her lips press tight, her brow knit, as she resolutely endures some trait of yours she does not admire, tolerates some weakness she thinks is atoned for by your kindness, but that annoys her all the same. I never see that grave flush rise on her face, the unsmiling sparkle in her eye, the slight recoil of her whole frame when you lean too close, gaze too intensely, whisper too warmly, without thinking of the fable of Semele, reversed.
It is not the daughter of Cadmus I see, longing to look on Jove in his full godhead. It is a priest of Juno, keeping lone, late vigil at an Argive temple. For years of solitary ministry he has lived on dreams. A divine madness is on him. He loves the idol he serves, prays day and night for his frenzy to be fed, for the Ox-eyed queen to smile on her votary. She has heard his prayer. She will be gracious. All Argos sleeps. The temple doors are shut. The priest waits at the altar.
A shock rips through heaven and earth, unheard by the sleeping city, felt only by that lone watcher, brave and unshaken in his fanaticism. Out of the silence, no warning, he is wrapped in sudden, blinding light. Through the rent roof, the wide, yawning, vast white blaze of the heaven above, a wondrous force pours down, dread as the rush of falling stars. He has exactly what he asked for. Withdraw. Do not look. I am blinded. I hear an unspeakable sound ringing through the shrine. I wish I could not hear it. I see an unendurable glory burning terribly between the pillars. Gods, be merciful, and quench it!
At dawn, a pious Argive comes to the temple to leave an early offering. There was thunder in the night. The lightning struck here. The shrine is shattered, the marble pavement split and blackened all around. Juno’s statue rises pure, grand, completely untouched. At her feet, pale ashes lie heaped. No priest remains. The man who kept the vigil will never be seen again.

The distant rumble of carriage wheels rolls up the drive.
Louis starts, locks the desk, pockets the keys, tucks the glove, the pen, the seal, the satin bag into his coat pocket too.
The carriage will pull up to the door any second. She will be looking for her keys tomorrow. She will come find me, he thinks, already smiling soft to himself. I can hear her clear voice now, half reluctant, a little sheepish: “Mr. Moore, have you seen my keys?”
She will say it, aware this is the twentieth

Every soul in Briarfield agreed: it was high time Mr. Robert Moore came home. The whole parish buzzed with curiosity over his unaccountable long absence, and the neighbouring villages of Whinbury and Nunnely each added their own wild, embellished theories to the pile of gossip.

No one was short of a reason for his delay – forty plausible ones circulated at the last count, all of them wrong. The gossips were unanimous that it could not be business, for everyone knew that long before, he had run down the four Luddite ringleaders who had targeted his mill, attended their trial, seen them convicted, and overseen their safe loading onto a transport ship bound for the colonies. The *Stilbro' Courier* had printed every last detail, padded out with extra dramatic flourishes, and the local mill-owners quietly celebrated, hoping the show of legal force would crush the violent unrest that threatened their livelihoods. But the disaffected mill workers still muttered in the corners of alehouses, swore bitter oaths over drugged beer, and raised fiery toasts in raw British gin, and no one was stupid enough to believe the trouble was truly over.

One loud rumour claimed Moore dared not set foot back in Yorkshire: his life, people whispered, would not be worth an hour's purchase the second he crossed the county line.
"I'll go tell him that," Mr. Yorke announced dryly when his foreman brought him the story. "And if that doesn't send him galloping home full tilt, nothing will."

Whether it was that threat, or some other pull, Moore finally yielded. He sent word to his foreman Joe Scott that he would arrive in Stilbro' on market day, and ordered a hackney carriage sent to meet him at the George Inn. Yorke heard the news, and made a point of heading into town to meet his young friend himself.

The market was in full swing when Moore arrived, and he took his usual seat at the manufacturers' shared market dinner. As a well-known man of action, recently returned from chasing down the mill rioters, he was received with no small measure of deference. Men who would never have dared acknowledge his acquaintance in public, lest the workers' hatred for him rub off on their own businesses, pulled him aside privately to hail him as their champion. When the wine circulated, their respect would have bloomed into full-throated enthusiasm if not for Moore's cold, unshaken nonchalance, which kept their praise smouldering low, damp and unenthused.

Yorke, who presided over these dinners by default, watched his young friend's bearing with huge, quiet delight. Nothing riled him faster than seeing a man made a fool of by flattery, puffed up by cheap popularity. Nothing smoothed his rough temper faster than seeing a public man who could not bear the limelight – disdain would have only angered him, but quiet indifference soothed him completely. Robert leaned back in his chair, quiet and almost surly, as the clothiers and blanket-makers around him bragged about his courage, recited his deeds, and laced their praise with coarse insults against the working class. To Yorke, it was a perfect sight. He could tell those cheap, vulgar eulogiums shamed Moore deeply, made him half-scorn himself and all he had done. It was easy to laugh at threats and insults, but the praise of people you despised stung sharper than any lash. Moore had stood on a hustings facing howling hostile crowds before, breasted the storm of unpopularity with his head high and his spirit elate, but now he drooped, chagrined, shrinking back from the congratulations of half-educated tradesmen.

Yorke could not resist teasing him. "How do you like your new band of supporters? Don't you think they do your cause proud? Pity you didn't hang those four rioters, lad. If you'd pulled that trick, the gentry round here would have unharnessed the horses from your coach, yoked a dozen asses to it, and dragged you through Stilbro' like a conquering general."

Moore soon pushed his wine aside, broke away from the party, and headed for the open road. Yorke followed him less than five minutes later, and the two men rode out of Stilbro' side by side.

They had left early for home, but the day was far spent. The last sliver of sun had already faded from the edges of the clouds, and the dark of the October night was creeping over the moors, casting long soft shadows across the heather. Yorke, pleasantly warmed by a moderate amount of wine, and glad to have young Moore back in Yorkshire as his companion for the long ride, talked almost nonstop. He passed over the trials and convictions with a scoffing, brief remark, moved on to the neighbourhood gossip, and before long, turned his full attention to Moore's personal affairs.

"Bob, you're well and truly beaten, and you deserve it. Fortune was head over heels for you, you know. She handed you the grandest prize in her lottery – twenty thousand pounds, a whole new life, your honour restored, your mill saved – all you had to do was reach out and take it. And what did you do? You hopped on a horse and rode off hunting rioters in Warwickshire. Your sweetheart – Fortune, I mean – was generous. She waited, patient as a monument, till the chase was done, till you'd run down your vermin prey, expecting you to come thundering home and lay your laurels at her feet. Instead, you coolly hopped a coach to London. God knows what you did there. I'll warrant you just sat and sulked. You were never pale as a lily, but now you're olive green. You're a far sight less handsome than you used to be, man."

"And who is getting this prize you go on about?" Moore asked, gruff.

"Only a baronet. That's all. I've not a doubt in my mind you've lost her. She'll be Lady Nunnely before Christmas."

"Hem. Quite probable."

"And she never needed to be. You fool of a lad! I swear you could have had her for the taking."

"What makes you so sure of that, Mr. Yorke?"

"Every sign there is. The way her eyes lit up when you walked in a room, the way her cheeks flushed bright red when your name was mentioned – and her cheeks are usually pale, you know that well enough."

"So my chance is quite over, then?"

"It ought to be. But it's still worth trying. This Sir Philip fellow is nothing but milk and water. And they say he writes verses, tags silly rhymes. You're above all that, Bob, at the very least."

"Would you advise me to propose even now? At the eleventh hour, when it's far too late?"

"You can only try, Robert. If she ever had a soft spot for you – and on my conscience, I believe she did, once – she'd forgive a lot. But lad, you're laughing. Are you laughing at me? You'd better grin at your own stubbornness. Though I see you're laughing on the wrong side of your mouth right now. You look as sour as any man I've ever seen."

"I've quarrelled with myself, Yorke. Kicked against pricks till my boots are worn through, struggled in a strait waistcoat till my wrists are raw, bashed my thick skull against a wall harder than stone till it aches."

"Ha! I'm glad to hear that. Sharp exercise, that! Hope it knocked some of your self-conceit out of you?"

"Self-conceit? What even is that? Self-respect, self-tolerance – do you sell those, anywhere? Do you know anyone who trades in them? Point me the way. I'd spend my last guinea this very minute to buy them."

"Is that how it is, Robert? I like that. I like a man who speaks his mind plainly. What went so wrong?"

"Every part of me is broken. The whole engine of my nature, the human mill that is me. The boiler I call my heart is fit to burst."

"That should go in a book. It's almost poetry. You'll be spouting verses any second now. Don't mind me, give in to the feeling."

"It was a hideous, unforgivable, base blunder. A mistake you make in one moment, that you rue for years – that no life can cancel out."

"Go on, lad. This is better than sugar candy. I love to hear it. Talking will do you good. There's no one else within miles on this moor, no one to overhear."

"I will talk. I'm not ashamed to tell. There's a wild cat tearing at my chest right now, and I want you to hear it yell."

"To me it's fine music. You and your brother Louis both have grand voices. When Louis sings, his tones are deep and soft as a distant bell, I've felt the hair on my arms stand up. The night is still, it's leaning down over you like a dark priest to a penitent. Confess. Don't smooth anything over. Be candid as a sanctified Methodist at an experience meeting. Make yourself as wicked as Beelzebub. It'll lighten your mind."

"As mean as Mammon, you mean. Yorke, if I got off this horse and laid myself across the road, would you gallop back and forwards over me twenty times?"

"With all the pleasure in life, as long as no coroner's inquest finds me out."

"Hiram Yorke, I truly believed Shirley Keeldar loved me. I saw her eyes sparkle bright when she spotted me in a crowd. She flushed crimson every time she offered me her hand and said 'how do you do, Mr. Moore?' My name had some strange magic over her – I saw her face change every time someone else said it. She said my name herself, in the prettiest, softest tone she had. She was warm with me, interested in me, anxious for my success. She sought out every chance to help me. I watched, and weighed, and wondered, and I could only come to one conclusion: this is love.

"I looked at her, and I saw youth, and a kind of rare beauty. I saw power in her. Her wealth could have redeemed my honour, saved my mill, pulled me out of all my debt. She'd already lent me five thousand pounds, that substantial, life-saving sum that kept the gates of Hollow's Mill open. I told myself how stupid I would be to turn away from that, to dismiss every good fortune placed right in front of me. Young, graceful, gracious – my benefactress, attached to me, pining for me. I repeated that to myself over and over, puffed up with the idea that a woman as bright as her could love me, and I smiled to myself at her sweet naivete, that she was the first to fall and show it. That whip of yours has a heavy handle, Yorke. You can swing it and knock me right out of the saddle if you like. I wouldn't even mind a hard thrashing right now."

"Have patience, Robert, till the moon rises and I can see your face clearly. Speak plain. Did you love her or not? I'm curious. I truly want to know."

"Sir – she's pretty, in her own way, very attractive. Sometimes she looks like something made of fire and air, and I stand and marvel at her, but I never once felt the urge to reach for her, to kiss her, to clasp her close. She stirred my vanity, my self-interest, but she never felt like my other half, my better self. When that thought crossed my mind, I pushed it away brutally, told myself I'd be rich with her, ruined without her, that I'd be practical, not foolishly romantic.

"That sensible resolution is what made me ride up to Fieldhead one night last August, the very eve of my trip to Birmingham to chase those rioters. I'd sent her a note asking for a private interview. I found her home, and alone. She greeted me without embarrassment, assuming I had come on business about the mill. I was so nervous I could barely breathe, but I was determined. I don't even remember how I got the words out, but I did it, cold and hard – I offered myself up, my fine person and all my debts, as if it was a business settlement.

"It infuriated me when she did none of the things I expected. She didn't blush, didn't tremble, didn't look down at the floor. She said, 'I doubt whether I have understood you, Mr. Moore.' I had to repeat the whole proposal twice, word it as plain as the alphabet, before she fully took it in. And then she stood up, walked fast twice across the room, in that way no other woman walks, and cried out, 'God bless me!'

"I leaned back against the mantelpiece, ready for anything. I knew my doom the second I saw her face, heard her shocked, indignant, sad voice. She stopped and looked right at me. 'God bless me,' she said again. 'That was a very strange proposal, stranger still coming from you. If you knew how coldly you spoke it, how stern you looked, you would be startled at yourself. You sounded like a brigand demanding my purse, not a lover asking for my heart.'

"It was a queer thing to say, wasn't it Yorke? And I knew instantly it was all true. Her words were a mirror, and I saw myself exactly as I was. I stared at her, dumb and sullen, half enraged, half shamed. 'Gérard Moore,' she said, 'you know you do not love Shirley Keeldar.' I could have lied, sworn up and down I loved her more than life, but I could not lie to her pure face. I could not perjure myself in front of her truthful eyes. She would no more have believed me than she would have believed the ghost of Judas. Her wits were far too sharp to mistake my cold, coarse admiration for the real, throbbing love a man should have for a woman he asks to marry.

"She sat down in the window seat then, and cried. Passionately. Her eyes rained tears, but they flashed too, large and dark and haughty, accusing me. They said: you have hurt me, outraged me, deceived me. 'I did respect you,' she said. 'I did admire you, I did like you – as much as I would like a brother. And you – you want to turn me into a business speculation. You would sacrifice me to that mill, your Moloch.'

"I had the good sense to not make a single excuse, to not try and wriggle out of it. I just stood there, ready to be scorned. I was such a fool, so infatuated with the idea of saving my mill, that when I finally spoke, what do you think I said? 'Whatever my own feelings were, I was persuaded you loved me, Miss Keeldar.'

"Isn't that beautiful? She sat there, completely stunned. 'Is that Robert Moore speaking?' I heard her mutter. 'Is it a man, or something lower?' 'Do you mean,' she asked out loud, 'that you thought I loved you the way we love people we wish to marry?' I said yes, that was exactly what I thought.

"'You have conceived an idea that is deeply insulting to every woman,' she said. 'You announced it in a way that makes my soul recoil. You are implying all the frank kindness I showed you was a complicated, bold, immodest trick to catch a husband. You are saying you have come here out of pity to offer me your hand, because I chased you. Let me tell you this: you saw wrong. You judged wrong. I never loved you. Rest easy on that. My heart has no passion for you any more than your heart has any real affection for me.'

"I think I got my answer, Yorke. 'I seem to be a blind, besotted fool,' was all I could say. 'Loved you!' she cried. 'I was as frank with you as a sister. I never shunned you, never feared you. You cannot make me tremble when you walk in a room, cannot make my pulse race for you.' I tried to argue, told her how she blushed when she talked to me, how my name moved her. 'Not for your sake,' she said, and would not explain more. She asked me, one by one, if I thought she loved me at the school feast, when she stopped me in Maythorn Lane, when she visited me at the counting house, when we walked on the pavement together. And I said yes, every time.

"By the Lord, Yorke, she rose up then, and she seemed to grow taller, lit from within like hot live coal. 'That means you think the worst of me. You deny me every thing I value most. You say I am a traitor to all my sisters, that I acted in a way that no decent woman ever would, chasing after a husband like that.' We were silent for minutes. 'Lucifer, Star of the Morning,' she said, 'thou art fallen. You, who I once held so high in my esteem, are cast down. Go.'

"I didn't go right away. I saw her lip tremble, and I knew another wave of tears was coming, and I thought after that, there would be calm, and we could fix it. The tears came fast, softer this time, full of regret. She looked up at me, her gaze more mournful than angry. 'O Moore,' she said. It hurt worse than 'Et tu, Brute!' I groaned, the ache of it sitting heavy in my chest, like Cain's own loneliness. 'What I did was wrong,' I said, 'and it has earned me bitter wages. I will go and spend them far away from you.'

"I picked up my hat. I did not want to leave like that, and I thought she would stop me. She would have, too, if the mortal wound I had dealt her pride had not silenced her, made her forget her compassion for a moment. I had to turn back of my own accord, walk up to her, and beg her to forgive me. 'I could forgive you,' she said, 'if I did not have to forgive myself too – to think I misled a sensible man so far, I must have done something wrong.' I stammered out apologies, desperate to make her see it was all my fault, no fault of hers, and finally she held out her hand. For the first time, I wished I could take her in my arms and kiss her, and I did kiss her hand, over and over. 'Some day we shall be friends again,' she said. 'When you have time to see my actions and my motives clearly, and stop misinterpreting me so horribly. Time will give

The die was well and truly cast. Sir Philip Nunnely carried the weight of his unspoken question thrumming under his skin; Shirley Keeldar felt its faint, strange echo at the back of her consciousness; and Mr. Sympson preened, utterly convinced the glittering, advantageous match he’d schemed for was as good as signed, sealed, and settled. That evening, when every last member of the Fieldhead household dined at Nunnely Priory beneath the family’s crested tapestries, had tipped every scale.

A handful of small, perfect turns had conspired to push the soft, poetic baronet to act. He’d watched Shirley all night, quiet, a faint rose flush high on her cheek, a faraway pensive air that tugged hard at his most romantic, unguarded heart. A sonnet was half-written in his head before he knew it, lines tangled like wild ivy, when one of his sisters coaxed Shirley over to the piano, begging her to sing a ballad Philip had penned himself: the least flowery, most unforced, and by far the finest of all his verses, an ode to faithful love that refused to break through disaster, that clung tighter through poverty and storm.

Just minutes before, Shirley had been staring out the tall sash window, watching that wild silver storm-light spill over the park—the same gale-tousled moonlight Louis Moore was no doubt contemplating from their oak parlour lattice back at Fieldhead. She’d seen the gale wrench at the gnarled ancient oaks, send tall, proud beeches bowing like knights before their queen, heard the low thunder of the forest roaring far below, watched clouds tear past the moon so fast the whole sky seemed to race. When she sang, she poured all that roused, wild feeling into the notes. Her voice curled soft around the tender verses, then swelled with unyielding passion, dramatic, bright, so alive that every person in the long shadowy saloon sat still, holding their breath.

Afterwards, she slipped to sit on a low half-stool, half-cushion by the hearth, and all the ladies clustered around her, staring like plain barnyard fowl gawking at a wandering egret they had never seen the like of. The Misses Sympson and Misses Nunnely could not fathom how she sang so: so much fire, so much individuality, nothing like the meek, decorous schoolgirl performance propriety dictated. Old Lady Nunnely, ensconced in her great carved armchair, stared at her stonily, her gaze spelling plain as day: This woman is not for my son. She is not one of us. Philip caught that look, his heart jolting hard in his chest—he was about to lose the prize he’d dreamed of, if he did not act fast.

The old saloon had once been Sir Monckton Nunnely’s picture gallery, long and shadowy, stretching far back, with a deep window recess tucked at one end that held nothing but a couch, a small table, and a little carved fairy cabinet: so cut off from the rest of the room that two people could speak in low tones and no one would overhear a single word. Philip sent two of his sisters off to pluck at a tuneless duet at the far end of the room, occupied the Misses Sympson with idle small talk, left the elder ladies gossiping by the fire, then drifted over to Shirley where she lingered, gazing at the portraits on the wall. He told her a soft tale of one of his ancestresses, a dark southern beauty whose portrait hung there, and when she leaned down to peer at the old missal and rosary laid out on the inlaid shelf of the cabinet in the recess, he leaned down too, and whispered his proposal, fast and breathless. Shirley froze stock still for a heartbeat, as if that soft whisper had turned her to marble, then she lifted her head, and answered him. A few moments later they parted, Shirley back to her seat by the fire, Philip lingering behind his screeching, unmusical sisters, and only Mr. Sympson, hovering by the door, had spotted the whole quiet exchange.

He raced home that night giddy, cock-a-hoop, certain the engagement was sealed. He could barely keep the secret, and the next morning, he puffed himself up before Louis Moore, his son’s tutor, announcing in grand, pompous tones that he would soon pack the household back for the south, because his long, hard work out here in Yorkshire had finally borne fruit: they were about to welcome a most eligible new relation into the family.
“Sir Philip Nunnely?” Louis had conjectured, quiet as ever, and Sympson chortled so hard he nearly choked on his snuff, waving the man back to his papers.

For days after that, Sympson fidgeted, hovering by every window, listening for the crunch of chariot wheels on the drive, waiting for the formal follow-up letter, the lawyers, the whole grand worldly ritual of arranging settlements for the marriage. At last, the letter came—he pulled it straight out of the post bag, recognising the baronet’s handwriting and crest at a glance. Shirley took it straight to her room, read it, locked herself away for nearly the whole day writing her reply, and when she came back down, simply said it was done. Sympson did not dare press her, not with that sharp, unreadable look on her face, as inscrutable to him as the writing on Belshazzar’s wall. He half wanted to drag Louis Moore in to decode it for him, but his pride would not let him, and Louis himself looked like a scholar staring at a page of blank grammar, no clue what was going on behind Shirley’s bright, steady eyes.

Then one afternoon, Sympson came back early from a visit to De Walden Hall, his face flushed, his boots stamping hard across the oak parlour floor, and called Shirley aside. He wanted a strictly private interview, he said, and Shirley rose without a single word of protest, laying down her sewing and thimble on the windowsill, following him to the drawing room with the same calm resignation someone might show following the dentist to pull a long aching tooth they’d been dreading for weeks.

They sat opposite each other in armchairs, a few yards apart, the pretty white and green carpet stretching between them. Sympson said he’d been to De Walden, and learned the most shocking news: Nunnely Priory was shut up. The whole family, Lady Nunnely, the daughters, Sir Philip himself, had left for their estate in another shire, without a word of warning.
Shirley only said “Indeed.”
Sympson fumed, demanded to know if this was news to her, and she said yes, it was. He lost his temper then, all pomp falling away into fidgety, irritable wordiness, ranting that he would not be trifled with, that he insisted on a full explanation.
“Calm down, uncle,” Shirley said, steady as a rock. “Ask what you want to know. I’ll give you truthful answers.”
He demanded to know if Sir Philip had made her an offer.
“He has,” she said, unflinching.
Sympson gaped, stammered out that he’d seen it all, the proposal in the picture gallery recess by the cabinet, that he knew she’d got the letter after. He demanded to see the letter, and Shirley told him flatly he could not. He sputtered that he was her guardian, and Shirley pointed out she was no longer a ward—she had no guardian.
Then he shouted the question he’d been dying to ask: had she accepted him?
“I refused him,” Shirley said, plain as day.
Sympson bounced right out of his chair, trotting back and forth across the rug, nearly apoplectic. “Refused him! Shirley Keeldar, you refused Sir Philip Nunnely, one of the most eligible baronets in the shire?” He raved that she was ungrateful, that her mind was poisoned by French novels and wild French ideas, that this would end in infamy. Shirley cut through his rant, calm still, and laid out her reasons: Philip was too young, little more than a boy to her. His mother and sisters would never accept her, and she would not marry a man she did not respect enough to obey, a man who could not match her fire, who would let her lead him around by the nose. She wanted a master, she told him, a man of thirty, with the wisdom of forty, whose approval was a prize, whose displeasure could sting, someone she could both love and fear. Not a soft poetic boy who wrote sonnets.

Sympson jeered, asked her if she’d pictured that husband from real life, and Shirley’s cheeks flushed bright rose. She teased him that she’d loved Socrates, Leonidas, Washington, all sorts of heroes, before she dragged the joke out long enough to exclaim that her present idol was none other than the great Arthur Wellesley, Lord Wellington. Sympson was so furious he bounced out of the room then bounced right back again, slamming the door, and then he dropped the ugly rumour he’d heard all over the neighbourhood: everyone was whispering that she was carrying on an improper entanglement with her bankrupt foreign tenant, Louis Moore’s brother Robert, the mill-owner.

Shirley’s eyes flashed bright, her temper snapping at last. She defended the Moores fiercely, saying they were all honour and courage, far worthier than any petty baronet with a handful of verses to his name. She told Sympson plainly she was done with his meddling, his petty small-town prejudices, his narrow rules that she would never bow to. “Your god is not my god,” she told him, bright and unyielding. “I walk by a different creed, a different light, a different hope than yours.”
Sympson gasped, scandalised to his very bones.
“An infidel! You’re an infidel!” he shrieked.
Shirley smiled, sharp and bright. “An infidel to your religion. An atheist to your god.”
The word hung in the air, ringing through the drawing room, as Mr. Sympson stared at her, aghast, and realised for the first time that he had not the slightest power over his fierce, brilliant, headstrong niece.

Briarmains stood far closer than the Hollow Mill that night, so gruff, warm-hearted Mr. Yorke had hauled his wounded young comrade straight to his own door, laying him in his finest guest bed with the same careful gentleness he would have shown one of his own sons. The sight of bright welling blood oozing from that treacherous, unprovoked blow sealed the tall, proud mill-owner a place right at the core of the Yorkshire gentleman’s affections. To see that straight, stalwart figure which had walked so unyielding across the highroad fall prostrate in the dust, that fine southern brow go deathly pale and slack, that youth in the prime of his strength reduced to a pale, lifeless heap at his feet—this exact set of circumstances was all it took to win Mr. Yorke’s most fierce, unwavering interest.

There was no other hand there to lift him, no other soft voice to ask after his hurts, no other clear head to plan for his care. Mr. Yorke did it all, and the utter, helpless dependence of that bleeding, silent youth on his goodwill tied that loyalty tight as a rope. Mr. Yorke had always loved holding power, and using it well: here he held a fellow creature’s life in his two hands, and it suited him perfectly.

It suited his saturnine, sharp-tongued wife no less. Some women would have screamed and fallen into hysterics to find a gory, half-dead man carried over their threshold at the dead of night, but not Mrs. Yorke. She only ever flew into hysterics when Jessie refused to leave the garden to do her knitting, or when Martin announced he was off to Australia to escape Matthew’s overbearing rules. A near-murder on her own doorstep, a half-murdered man in her best bed? It perked her right up, tied a jaunty little turban-like angle to the edge of her cap, and set her about her work like a heroine. She was exactly the sort of woman who could make a overworked little housemaid’s life a drudging misery one hour, and nurse an entire ward of plague patients through the worst fevers without a flinch the next. She almost loved Robert Moore, tough as her heart was. The second she saw him left in her arms, as small and helpless as her own youngest son in his cradle, she chased Jessie and Rose straight out of the upper floors of the house, forbade all the maids from stepping foot near the sick chamber, and guarded her charge like a ferret guarding its nest. If she caught any of them so much as handing him a sip of water or smoothing his pillow, she would box their ears before they could open their mouths.

Strangely enough, they never would have pitied him at all if he’d been attacked outside the rectory gates and taken in by old Helstone. They’d have called it well-earned payback for his stubborn mill rules and meddling. But as things stood, Robert was for the moment the apple of their eye. Even Louis Moore, his quiet, gentle brother, was allowed to sit for hours on the edge of his bed, holding his hand and pressing soft kisses to his pale forehead, without Mrs. Yorke protesting. She let him sit up all night with Robert once, even got herself up at five o’clock on a drizzly November morning to light the kitchen fire herself, cook the two brothers a hot breakfast, and serve it to them in her giant flannel wrapper and frilled nightcap, watching them eat as complacently as a hen watching her chicks peck at grain. That same day, though, she gave the cook formal warning for sneaking a basin of sago gruel up to the sickroom without her say-so, and stripped the housemaid of all her favours for airing Louis’s overcoat for him when he left, helping him into it, and accepting his thanks and a shilling tip. When two pale, anxious ladies (Caroline and Shirley, of course, whom she’d barred without a second thought) called and begged just for one single minute to see Robert, she turned them straight out the door with more than a few sharp words for their presumption.

Hortense Moore’s arrival was far more welcome. The two women found an endless, endlessly satisfying shared topic in the lazy, scheming propensities of servants, matching each other’s suspicion and severity perfectly, and Hortense never once tried to steal the role of head nurse from Mrs. Yorke. Instead she busied herself patrolling the kitchen, reporting every bit of gossip from the staff, and making herself generally useful, the two of them united in ferociously barring every last visitor from the sickroom, keeping their mill-owner captive so carefully not even a stray gust of cold air could touch him unplanned.

Surgeon Mr. MacTurk, a gruff, no-nonsense man with a short temper, declared the wound dangerous but far from hopeless, and offered to send one of his own trained nurses to sit with Moore. But neither Mrs. Yorke nor Hortense would hear of it, promising to follow every single one of his instructions to the letter. Left to their own devices, though, the bandages got shifted and tampered with somehow, and Robert lost a dangerous amount of blood. MacTurk came galloping over, his horse lathered in foam, his temper frayed to breaking point, and let loose a stream of sharp, blistering complaints that heaped first on his stony young assistant Graves, then on his own son who worked alongside him, before landing full on the two meddling women. He and his two assistants worked straight through most of the winter night, huddled at the bedside on one side, fighting Death itself on the other, the struggle sharp and brutal, lasting until the first pale streaks of dawn broke over the hills.

At sunrise he left Graves and his son to watch the patient, then rode straight out to fetch the best nurse on his entire staff: Mrs. Horsfall. A giant of a woman, broad, strong, and unyielding as a granite boulder, she obeyed MacTurk’s orders to the letter more strictly than she followed the Ten Commandments. The moment she stepped across the threshold of the sickroom, her reign began. Hortense and Mrs. Yorke, both women who thought themselves rather imposing in their own right, were completely cowed by her bulk and sharp eye, and retreated meekly to the back parlour to grieve at being ousted. Mrs. Horsfall sat wherever she pleased, drank her daily dram three times a day, and smoked her pipe of tobacco four, and no one dared step in her way. She turned the six-foot tall, once-powerful Robert Moore in his bed as easily as a nursemaid turns a squalling infant, calling him “honey” when he did as he was told, and shaking him sharp when he fussed or tried to speak over MacTurk’s examinations. When Robert once weakly complained to the surgeon that she was a heavy drinker, MacTurk just laughed and told him not to fuss: drunk or sober, she followed his orders better than any nurse alive.

November dragged on, short, grey, dreary days melting into long dark nights, and Robert Moore lay stuck in that upper bedroom, weak, in constant pain, his only society the three sharp-edged surgeons and his dragon keeper.

Then the fogs of autumn lifted. The rains stopped, the wind swept far off to sigh over other lands, and deep clear winter settled over the hills, sharp with frost and stillness. One calm day bled into a crystalline, glittering evening, the whole world tinted like fragments of gemstone: the hills lilac-blue, the setting sun streaked purple through its red, the sky a sheet of silvered pale ice, the stars when they rose white as clear crystal, no warm gold to their light.

Down in the wood, all its green long faded to soft neutral brown and grey, a small dark blue figure wandered away from the highroad where the rest of the grammar school boys trudged home. It was fifteen-year-old Martin Yorke, sharp, prickly, tall for his age, the sort of boy ushers learned not to push around with severity or flatter with soft words. He pretended to hate all poetry and sentiment, rolling his eyes whenever anyone talked of nature or fairy tales, but he’d snuck away from the group on purpose to sit on a mossy mound at the root of his favourite old oak tree. He pulled a tattered contraband fairy tale book out of his satchel, settled in, and lost himself first in tales of green-robed fairy ladies riding white palfreys through misty mountains, then of foam-white sea nymphs dancing on sharp black reefs out in the tide.

A soft crunch of dead leaves on the path made him jump. He snapped the book shut and stuffed it back in his satchel, watching the trees part to reveal a lady in dark silk, a thin veil pulled over her face. He’d never seen a lady in this wood before. She passed him by without a word, and he was just sneering to himself that it was silly, stuck-up Caroline Helstone, when she stopped, turned back, and lifted her veil to look at his face.

“Are you one of Mr. Yorke’s sons?” she asked, soft and a little shy.

Martin would never have admitted he blushed bright red to the ears, but he did. He told her he was Martin, and the simple, unceremonious way she used his name, no “Master Martin” stuffy formality, no silly babying familiarity, made him soften immediately. She asked after his parents, his sisters, then asked after Hortense, then finally, hesitantly, after Mr. Moore.

Martin thought it would be great fun to test how much she cared, so he told her, deadpan, that Moore was dying, all hope lost, that the meddling women had messed up his bandages and finished him off.

Caroline went deathly pale. Her veil slipped from her fingers, and she stood perfectly still, as if the breath had been knocked clean out of her. Without a goodnight or another word, she turned and started walking slowly away.

The fun died in Martin’s throat immediately. He ran after her, apologizing, spilling that he’d lied, that Robert was eating mutton chops for dinner and arrowroot for supper, that there was always some sweet delicacy simmering on the kitchen fire for him, that he’d even eaten half a basin of the sago gruel once. Caroline was so shaken she could barely stand, leaning against a shivering frost-filigreed tree, trembling, and Martin’s sharp, urchin heart felt a strange twinge, like the time he’d found a blackbird crying over her nest Matthew had smashed. He offered to guide her out of the wood, leading her through a messy little short cut that made him help her climb over thorny hedges and low walls, grumbling about how helpless she was the whole time, secretly giddy to feel useful.

Before they parted at the Briarmains gate, Caroline made him promise on his honour that Robert was really recovering. Martin, feeling suddenly protective of her, promised he would sneak past Mrs. Horsfall the dragon nurse tomorrow, get a proper look at Robert himself, and meet her right here in the wood at the same hour the next evening to bring her news, so she wouldn’t have to keep walking around with that heavy, worried weight on her chest. He walked her all the way back to her cottage, ignoring the fact he’d be late and get nothing but dry bread and a long lecture for tea. The adventure was far better than muffins by the fire.

When he got home, the scolding and dry tea were duly doled out, and he was sent up to bed early. Before he climbed the stairs, he ducked into the quiet, unused formal dining room, held his candle up to the two female portraits hanging above the mantel, one soft and happy, the other sad and forlorn, and nodded at the latter.

“That’s exactly what she looked like,” he murmured to himself, thinking of Caroline leaning white and shaking against the tree, “when she thought he was dead.”

Fifteen-year-old Martin Yorke had a very specific, very mischievous plan to pull off that day—and the first rule of his scheme was that he could not, under any circumstances, attend grammar school.

So he picked at his breakfast until his plate sat practically untouched, and by the time Mark was slinging his schoolbag over his shoulder to leave for class, Martin was clutching his chest, face pale enough to sell a dramatic story of a sudden, stabbing pain around his heart. It was a very convincing performance, convincing enough that his mother fussed over him, and his father waved him off to claim the best armchair by the parlour fire and the morning paper, rather than tramping through the cold to Mr. Summer’s classes.

By the time the front door clicked shut behind Mark, and Matthew and Mr. Yorke had headed off to the counting house, Martin had four tasks to tick off his list. The first, and most urgent for a growing boy who’d skipped his morning meal on purpose, was to make up for that missed breakfast. The next three? Get his mother, Hortense Moore, and the housekeeper Mrs. Horsfall well and truly out of the way before four o’ clock that afternoon.

He slipped quiet as a cat to the larder, the servants all behind closed kitchen doors eating their own late breakfast, his mother and Miss Hortense gossiping on the lawn. He took his time picking out the very best treats: three blushing rosy apples resting on a bed of straw, a buttery apricot puff, a small damson tart, a currant tea-cake still slightly warm from the hearth, a tender wing of cold fowl and a slice of cured ham he sliced free with his clasp knife, and a thick wedge of cold custard pudding to round it all out. His arms loaded high, he was halfway across the hall, three steps from the back parlour where he intended to eat his feast in peace, when the front door opened.

It was Matthew. The older boy had never bought Martin’s fake chest pain for a single second, had been muttering about him “shamming Abraham” ever since breakfast, and the sight of Martin staggering under that mountain of forbidden snacks was all the proof he needed to prove he was right.

Martin froze for all of one heartbeat—then a bright, devious little grin spread across his face. This accident wasn’t a disaster. It was an opportunity.

He knew exactly how his elder brother picked fights: dry snide laughs, taunts about being a lazy little faker, sneers at his fake illness. Martin snapped back with a cutting, deliberately provocative retort, and before ten seconds were up the two Yorke boys were rolling and shouting in the hall loud enough to rouse the whole house. The servants came running, his mother and Hortense darted in from the lawn, no one could pry them apart, and Mr. Yorke stormed out of the counting house to threaten to throw whichever of the two started it out of the house for good.

When they finally pulled Martin off Matthew, he was completely calm, not a scratch on him, smiling to himself. He’d known their fight would send his mother straight into one of her well-known hysterics, and that by mid-afternoon she’d be tuckered out enough to spend the rest of the day napping in her bedchamber. That was the hardest part of his plan, already done.

He lounged on the sofa pretending to be a delicate invalid for the rest of the morning, at one point half-convincing himself this whole scheme to play matchmaker for Caroline Helstone wasn’t worth the hassle—until he glanced at the half-finished portrait of Robert Moore Hortense had left on the dining room easel, the one with wild unbound hair and eyes dim with grief, and shook his head. No, he wasn’t going to quit. He wanted to see that sad girl smile, and he wanted to outwit every single stuffy grown woman in the neighbourhood while he did it.

After dinner, just as he’d predicted, his mother dragged herself up the stairs to her bed, declaring the scene with the boys had worn her out completely. Next on his list: Hortense. He found her settled in the back parlour mending stockings, and launched into a long, rambling story about Sarah the maid at the Hollow, the one who was now running the cottage all by herself. He casually dropped the news that Sarah supposedly had three suitors tripping over themselves to court her, and that the neighbours had seen her feeding them lavish meals every night. Hortense, who could not resist a chance to snoop on a household scandal, grabbed her shawl within five minutes and set off for the Hollow to see the state of things for herself.

That left only Mrs. Horsfall. Martin slipped to his mother’s work basket, fished out the key to the sideboard cupboard, pulled out a bottle of sweet spiced cordial and a small glass, left them out on the parlour table, then knocked on the door to Robert Moore’s bedchamber where the nurse sat keeping watch. He told her the family had all stepped out, and she was welcome to go sit by the back parlour fire and help herself to a nice glass of refreshment with no one to disturb her. He watched her go in, then shut the door softly behind her. The nurse was safely occupied for hours.

Martin grabbed his cap and ran for the wood. It was only half past three, but the sky had turned dark and heavy, soft fat snowflakes starting to drift down, the wind cutting sharp through the bare branches of the old twisted oaks. He loved it, the wild, spectral hush of the snow coming down. He paced the narrow path back and forth, the snow falling faster, the wind picking up from a soft moan to a low howl.

“Took her long enough,” he muttered, when he finally heard the quick, light footstep on the path, barely making a sound over the dead leaves. It was Caroline Helstone, her shawl wrapped tight around her, her cheeks pink, not even seeming to notice the bitter cold or the blinding snow swirling around her.

“Martin—how is he?” she burst out, the first words from her mouth.

Martin’s chest did a strange little soft flip. She didn’t care a bit about the snow, or the cold, or what anyone would say if they saw her, all she cared about was him. He almost felt bad for not having brought a spare cloak to wrap around her.

He teased her a little, told her he wasn’t going to run messages for her anymore, that he was far too “delicate” after his terrible chest pain that morning to traipse through the snow, and before long he’d talked her into trusting him, into letting him lead her down the quiet lane to Briarmains, telling her he could get her inside to see Robert herself. She hesitated for only a second before nodding, and slipping her hand through his arm.

The whole house was quiet, exactly as Martin promised. He kicked his shoes off on the mat at the door, so no sound of their steps would carry, and Caroline followed him barefoot up the staircase, her feet light as a shadow. He tapped on Robert’s bedchamber door three times before a low, weak, familiar voice called out for them to enter.

Martin stepped in first, telling the wounded man that a lady had come to call on him, that none of the staff had been around to turn her away. Robert, surprised, ordered Martin to send her in—and then he froze, wide-eyed, when Martin stepped aside and said “Come in, Miss Caroline.”

Caroline stepped into the middle of the room, and the tall, thin, wasted figure of Robert Moore stumbled forward to take both her hands. Outside, the storm raged so hard the whole house seemed to rattle, but neither of them heard a single howl of wind, or saw the snow piling up against the window panes. They only saw each other. They talked of all the times Caroline had tried to visit before, turned away at the door, of the long, bleak weeks the wounded man had spent alone, of the quiet, sick despair Caroline had suffered through when she thought she would never see him again. When she leaned down to brush her soft lips over his thin, cold, work-worn fingers, a great, trembling tear rolled down his hollow, gaunt cheek, and he swore he would hold that gentle kiss close in his heart for as long as he lived.

Martin slipped out, shutting the door behind him, grinning to himself like he was the most powerful king in the shire. He’d never pulled off a scheme half as good as this one. He waited in the gallery, keeping watch, making sure no stray servant or family member wandered up to disturb them, until he heard a faint cough from his mother’s bedchamber down the hall. Panicked, he threw open the door to Robert’s room. They’d had twenty whole minutes, way more than the quarter of an hour he’d promised.

“Come on, now, out you go!” he hissed. Caroline protested for half a second, but when he reminded her his mother was up and moving around, she let Martin wrap her shawl tight around her, and hurried down the stairs, slipping out the front door just as Mrs. Yorke’s footsteps creaked on the gallery above them. She skimmed across the snowy lane, her feet barely sinking into the drifts, gone before Martin could even work up the nerve to ask her for the kiss he thought he’d earned for pulling off the best secret reunion the two of them had been dreaming of for months.

He leaned against the doorframe, watching her go, the snowflakes landing on his rosy cheeks. He told himself he’d only missed claiming his reward because he’d been in a rush, not because a soft, boyish shyness had held him back. But even without the kiss, he knew, somewhere quiet in his heart, he’d done a far kinder, far more wonderful thing than he’d ever planned to do that day.

# CHAPTER XXXIV. A CASE OF DOMESTIC PERSECUTION--REMARKABLE INSTANCE OF PIOUS PERSEVERANCE IN THE DISCHARGE OF RELIGIOUS DUTIES
Martin Yorke had tasted the bright, buzzing spark of excitement once, and he ached for another sip. He’d felt the quiet weight of power for the first time, and he had no intention of letting it slip back through his fingers. Miss Helstone— the girl he’d always written off as plain, whose face now haunted every corner of his days and nights, glowing in sunlight and lingering in shadow— had once stepped into his small, self-made world. The thought she might never cross its threshold again niggled at him like an itch he couldn’t scratch. Though still a schoolboy, he was no ordinary one: he was marked to be a soul all his own. In years to come he would spend endless hours sanding and polishing his edges to fit the rest of the world’s expected mould, but he would never quite manage it. That unique, unmistakeable stamp stayed on him for life.
He sat idle at his grammar school desk that week, turning over every idea he could think of to add a new chapter to the little half-spun romance he’d begun to weave around Caroline. He still had no idea how many such half-written life stories are doomed to never make it past the first, or at most the second page. He spent his Saturday half-holiday wandering the bare winter woods with his tattered book of fairy legends, the unwritten story in his head far more vivid than any tale on the pages.
Martin had always hated the approach of Sunday, an ungodly sort of drag to him. His parents, for all their distaste for strict loyalty to the Established Church, never failed to drag their whole brood of blooming sons and daughters to Briarfield Church once every sacred week, to fill their large cushioned pew to bursting. Theoretically, Mr. Yorke held every sect and denomination on equal ground. Mrs. Yorke swore the Moravians and Quakers were the best of the lot, for the crown of gentle humility they wore. Neither of them, however, had ever been known to set foot in a dissenting conventicle.
This Saturday afternoon, his woodland daydreams unveiled a new, thrilling charm to the coming Sunday.
When dawn broke, the snow lay deep enough to swallow a man’s ankle. At breakfast, Mrs. Yorke announced her firm conviction that none of the children should step outside, and decided they would all spend two quiet hours in the back parlour instead of going to church, taking turns reading selections of John Wesley’s sermons aloud. Wesley, a reformer and agitator, was a favourite of both hers and her husband’s.
“Rose will do as she pleases,” Martin said, not lifting his eyes from the book he pored over even as he ate his bread and milk, a habit he kept all his life.
“Rose will do as she is told, and so will you,” his mother shot back.
“I’m going to church,” Martin replied, with that unshakable quiet steadiness unique to the Yorkes, who know their own will and mean to get their way, who will let themselves be crushed to a wall before they surrender, no matter what.
“The weather’s not fit for a dog,” his father objected. No answer. The boy read on studiously, breaking his bread slow, sipping his milk one tiny mouthful at a time.
“Martin hates church, but he hates obeying even more,” Mrs. Yorke sighed. “I suppose this is just pure perverseness on your part?”
“Yes,” he said.
“Mother, I am not.”
“Then what on earth is motivating you?”
“A whole tangled mess of motives. I might as soon turn myself inside out to show you all the machinery inside my body as try to untangle them for you.”
“Listen to the lad!” Mr. Yorke laughed, slamming his palm on the table. “I’m sending this one to the bar, mark my words. Nature made him to earn a living by his tongue. Hesther, our third son will be a lawyer, no two ways about it— he’s got all the stock in trade: brass, self-conceit, and words, words, words.”
“Pass the bread, Rose, please,” Martin requested, perfectly grave, serene, phlegmatic as ever. His voice was naturally low, soft, almost plaintive, and in his stubbornest moods it rose scarcely above a lady’s whisper: the more inflexible his will, the gentler, sadder his tone. He rang the bell, and asked for his walking boots in that same quiet, unshakable voice.
“But Martin,” his father urged, “the drifts are waist deep all the way to church. A man could barely wade through them. But lad,” he went on, seeing the boy stand just as the first church bell began to toll, “I won’t balk you of your will, not for the world. Go on to church, if that’s what you’d rather do than sit by a warm fire. There’s a pitiless wind, sharp frozen sleet, and snow thick underfoot that will freeze your socks solid in ten minutes. Go out into it, then.”
Martin pulled on his thick wool cloak, his fuzzy wool comforter, and his cap pulled low over his ears, and stepped deliberately out into the storm.
“My father has more sense than my mother,” he muttered to himself as the wind bit his cheeks. “How women fumble things! They hammer away at what they think is stone, driving their nails right into the soft flesh of the person they’re trying to move.”
He reached church long before the service started. If the storm scared her off, or if that Mrs. Pryor forbade her from leaving the house, and he missed her after all, he’d be furious— but tempest or tornado, hail or ice, she ought to come. If she had half the spark her face promised, she would come. She’d be here for the chance to see him, just as he was here for the chance to see her. She’d want a word about that confounded sweetheart of hers, just as he wanted another taste of that sharp, bright spark he’d come to think was the very essence of real life: adventure, the thing that makes stagnant, boring everyday life feel like champagne next to flat porter.
He glanced around the cold, silent, empty church. Only one old woman sat in the free seats. As the chimes faded and the single bell tolled slow, other elderly parishioners trickled in one by one, taking their humble spots on the bare oak benches. It is always the frailest, oldest, poorest souls who brave the worst weather, to prove their devotion to the old stone mother church they love. Not one affluent family turned up that wild morning, not one carriage clattered up the path: all the lined, cushioned, well-kept pews were empty. Only the gray-haired elders and feeble paupers sat ranged along the bare wooden seats.
“If she doesn’t come, I’ll scorn her,” Martin muttered under his breath, sharp and bitter.
The rector’s shovel hat passed the porch. Mr. Helstone and his clerk vanished into the vestry. The bells stopped. The rector took his place at the desk. The doors shut. The service began.
The rectory pew stood empty. She was not there.
Martin scorned her. Worthless thing! Vapid thing! Commonplace humbug! Just like all other girls— weak, selfish, shallow! That was his whole litany of silent, angry insults. She was nothing like the ideal he’d built up in his head. Her eyes weren’t large and expressive, her nose wasn’t straight and delicate and Hellenic, her mouth didn’t have that soft charm he’d imagined could chase his worst sullen moods away. What was she, anyway? A paper doll, a trivial toy, a girl, nothing more.
He was so caught up in his angry thoughts he forgot to stand up from his knees when the rest of the congregation did, and he was still kneeling in what looked like exemplary devotion when the litany ended and the first hymn was announced. He jumped up bright red, as sensitive to ridicule as any young girl, embarrassed to be caught daydreaming. To make matters worse, the church door creaked open, and the aisles began to fill: patter, patter, patter, a hundred little feet trotted in. It was the Sunday scholars. Per Briarfield’s winter custom, the children had been kept by the warm stove in the parish hall until that point, only led into church right before the communion and sermon. The tiny ones were settled first, the boys and younger girls arranged after them— and just as the organ swelled loud and the choir and congregation stood to sing, a tall class of young women filed quietly in at the back of the procession. Their teacher saw them seated, then slipped into the rectory pew.
Martin knew that soft French-gray cloak and neat little beaver bonnet instantly, the exact costume his eyes had ached to catch. Miss Helstone had not let the storm stop her. She had come after all. Martin hid his face behind his hymn book for two whole minutes, to hide the stupid giddy grin spreading over his face.
Satisfied or not, he had time to get very cross with her again before the sermon ended. She never once looked his way— at least he never caught her glancing at him. “If she takes no notice of me, if she acts like I’m not even on her mind, I’ll think worse of her than ever,” he fumed to himself. “It would be the most pathetic thing in the world if she only came here to look after those sheep-faced Sunday school children, and not for my sake, or that long lanky skeleton Moore’s.”
The sermon ended. The benediction was pronounced. The congregation spilled out of the church. She never came near him.
As Martin turned his face for home, he realized the sleet was stinging sharp, and the east wind was bitter enough to cut through his cloak. His shortcut lay through untrodden fields, dangerous in the deep snow, but he didn’t care. He would take it anyway.
Near the second stile stood a clump of bare winter trees. Was that an umbrella waiting there? Yes, an umbrella, held with clear effort against the blast, a French-gray cloak flapping behind it. Martin grinned to himself as he trudged up the steep, snow-drifted slope, as hard to climb as the side of a mountain. There was a glint of unmistakeable mischief in his face when he reached the stile, sat down coolly right on top of it, and opened a conversation he was perfectly willing to drag out for hours.
“I think we ought to strike a bargain. Exchange me for Mrs. Pryor.”
“I wasn’t sure you’d come this way, Martin,” Caroline said, laughing a little, “but I decided to take the chance. There’s no way to get a quiet word in church or the churchyard, what with everyone milling about.”
“Will you agree? Give Mrs. Pryor over to my mother, and tuck me into her skirts instead?”
“As if I could understand you! What on earth put Mrs. Pryor into your head?”
“You call her ‘mamma,’ don’t you?”
“She is my mamma.”
“Not possible— no real mother would be so inefficient, so careless as to let her daughter traipse out in weather like this. I’d be a far better guardian for you, five times over. You can laugh all you like. I don’t mind seeing you laugh. I hate ugly teeth, you know— but yours are as pretty as a pearl necklace, all fair, even, perfectly matched.”
“Martin! The Yorkes never pay compliments, I thought?”
“They never did until this generation. I’m fated to be a new variety of Yorke, I think. I’m tired of all our boring old ancestors. We’ve got family traditions going back four whole ages: tales of Hiram son of Hiram son of Samuel son of John son of Zerubbabel Yorke. Every single one of them, from Zerubbabel down to the last Hiram, was exactly like my father. But before that, there was a Godfrey Yorke. We’ve got his portrait, hanging in Moore’s bedroom. He looks just like me. We don’t know a thing about his character, but I know it was different from all the rest of them. He’s got long dark curling hair, he dresses like a cavalier. And since I said he looks like me, I don’t need to add he’s handsome.”
“You’re not handsome, Martin.”
“No, not yet. But give me a little time. I’m going to start cultivating and polishing myself from this very day, and we’ll see.”
“You’re a very strange, unaccountable boy, Martin. But don’t go imagining you’ll ever be handsome. You won’t.”
“I’m going to try. But we were talking about Mrs. Pryor. She’s the most unnatural mother alive, to calmly let her daughter go out in this storm. My mother was so furious when I said I was going to church, she was fit to hurl the kitchen brush at my back.”
“Mamma was very worried about me. But I’m afraid I was obstinate. I would go.”
“To see me?”
“Exactly. I could think of nothing else. I was so afraid the snow would stop you from coming. You have no idea how pleased I was to spot you sitting all alone in your pew.”
“I only came to do my duty, set the whole parish a good example,” he said, feigning primness. “So you were obstinate, were you? I’d love to see you properly obstinate. Wouldn’t I keep you in good order if you were mine? Let me take that umbrella for you, you’re struggling with it.”
“I can’t stay two minutes. Our dinner will be ready any second.”
“So will ours. We always have a hot dinner on Sundays. Roast goose today, with apple pie and rice pudding. I always manage to know exactly what’s on the menu. I love all those things enormously, but I’ll make the sacrifice to stay out here in the cold as long as you want, if you will.”
“We have a cold dinner. My uncle won’t allow any unnecessary cooking on the Sabbath. I have to go back. The whole house will be in an uproar if I don’t turn up soon.”
“So will Briarmains, believe you me! I can almost hear my father sending the overlooker and five of the dyers out to search in six directions for his prodigal son’s frozen body in the snow, and my mother repenting every little petty thing she ever did to me, now she thinks I’m lost.”
“Martin— how is Mr. Moore?”
“That’s what you came out here for, isn’t it? Just to say that one word.”
“Go on, tell me quickly.”
“Hang him. He’s no worse, but he’s still being treated abominably— locked up, kept in solitary confinement. They’re going to make an idiot or a maniac out of him at this rate, then get a lunacy commission signed to take over all his property. Horsfall is starving him, you saw how thin he was.”
“You were so good to him the other day, Martin.”
“What other day? I’m always good. A perfect model, that’s me.”
“When will you be good again?”
“I see exactly what you’re after. But you won’t wheedle me. I’m no one’s cat’s-paw.”
“But it has to be done. It’s the right thing, and the necessary thing.”
“How pushy you are! Remember, I did that favour entirely of my own free will last time.”
“And you will again.”
“I won’t. That whole business was far too much trouble. I like my comfort.”
“Mr. Moore wishes to see me, Martin, and I wish to see him.”
“I don’t doubt it,” he said, cool as ever. “It’s terrible of his own mother to exclude all his friends.”
“Go tell her that, then.”
“His own relations.”
“Go confront her about it.”
“You know that won’t help a single thing. Fine, I’m sticking to my point. I will see him. If you won’t help me, I’ll manage it all on my own.”
“Go on then. Nothing beats self-reliance, self-dependence.”
“I don’t have time to argue with you now. You’re being so provoking. Good morning.”
She hurried away, closing her umbrella tight because she couldn’t hold it against the tearing wind.
“She’s not vapid. She’s not shallow,” Martin murmured to himself, watching her go. “I can’t wait to sit back and watch her work her way through this, all on her own. If the storm was fire instead of snow, the kind that rained down on the cities of the plain, she’d walk straight through it just to get five minutes of speech with that Moore. All the little disappointments and stupid angry fits I went through this morning just made that short little chat at the end taste all the sweeter. She thought she’d coax me into helping her right away. She won’t manage that in one go. She’ll come back again, and again, and yet again. I’d love to push her far enough to make her angry, make her cry. I want to see how far she’ll go— what she’ll do, what she’ll dare, to get what she wants. It’s so strange and new, to see one human being care that much about another. But it’s time to head home. My stomach’s telling me what the hour is. Won’t I tuck into that roast goose? And we’ll see who gets the biggest slice of apple pie, Matthew or me.”

Martin’s clever little scheme woven for his own private amusement had been strung with all the deft precision of a spider spinning its web, but the unforgiving, red-armed housewife we call Fate has never spared even the most finely spun plans of schemers far older and wiser than him. This time, her broom was hewn straight from the tough, stubborn fibres of Robert Moore’s own iron will, newly sharpened through weeks of slow recovery, and nothing could stop it sweeping all other carefully laid arrangements out of his path.

He defied the formidable Mrs. Horsfall first, to her endless astonishment: one morning he refused to let her dress him, the next he turned away her specially brewed coffee and declared he would take breakfast with the rest of the Yorke family, and by week’s end he had barred her entirely from his bedchamber. The very same day, over the squawking protests of every woman in the house, he stuck his head out the front door into the crisp, frosty air. The next morning, he limped after Mr. Yorke to his counting house, and calmly requested an envoy to fetch a chaise from the Red House Inn. He was returning home to Hollow’s Cottage that very afternoon, he said, and no amount of arguing would shift him.

Far from opposing him, Mr. Yorke laughed and aided him, even as Mrs. Yorke wailed the foolhardy act would be his death. Moore spoke scarcely a word all the while, but his purse did all his talking for him: he pressed generous coins into every servant’s palm, and handed Mrs. Horsfall a sum so handsome it wiped every last memory of his earlier defiance clean away. The pair parted the best of friends.

He soothed the kitchen staff first, then made his way to the parlour to appease Mrs. Yorke, a far trickier task. She sat huffing in her chair, sunk deep in grievances over man’s base ingratitude, until he leaned over her, his pale, gaunt face still holding traces of quiet worn beauty, his hollow eyes soft with a shy, earnest smile.
“Goodbye,” he said, and the smile glistened and melted a little, for he was still too weak to master every small stirring of emotion.
“And what do you mean to leave us for?” she grumbled. “We’ll nurse you back to full strength, do anything in the world for you, if you’ll only stay a little longer.”
“Goodbye,” he repeated, and added gently, “You’ve been a mother to me. Won’t you give your wilful stray son one embrace?”
Half protesting, she let him lean in to kiss first one cheek, then the other, in his foreign fashion, and found herself kissing him back. “What a burden I’ve been to you,” he murmured.
“Worse than a wilful boy at that,” she sniffled. “Who on earth will nurse you at that tiny cottage? Your sister Hortense knows no more about tending an invalid than a five year old child.”
“Thank God for that,” he laughed low. “I’ve had more than enough of being coddled to last me a lifetime.”

The two little Yorke girls — Jessie red-eyed with tears and Rose quiet and grave — came in then, and he herded them out to the hall to kiss them, to pet their soft hair, knowing full well Mrs. Yorke would be annoyed to see him lavish affection on anything that wasn’t her. The boys were loitering by the chaise waiting for him when he stepped outside; he had no farewell words for them, only nodded to Mr. Yorke before he climbed in. “You’ll be glad to be rid of me,” he said. “That shot of mine turned your fine house into a hospital for weeks. Come visit me soon at the cottage.” He drew the carriage glass shut, and the chaise rumbled away down the lane.

Half an hour later, he alighted at his own garden wicket, paid off the driver, and leaned against the wood for a long minute, catching his breath and thinking. Six months ago, he had walked out this very gate a proud, angry, bitterly disappointed man, convinced his whole life was unraveling at the seams. Now he came back sadder, wiser, still weak on his feet, but untormented. The world around him was cold and grey, but quiet — and now that he no longer dreaded the thought of ruin, he had nothing left to fear. He knew the bankruptcy he had spent so many sleepless nights dreading would come, in six months’ time at the latest, but it no longer felt like dishonour. He could work beside Joe Scott, earn an honest living with his own two hands, and if the worst came, he and Louis would sail for America, plant a new life in the woods there. A quiet, unnameable thought tugged at the back of his mind: would anyone else come with them? He had no right to ask, not yet.

He pushed open the door and stepped inside. It was late afternoon, the sky heavy with frozen clouds, the frost biting sharp enough to freeze the mill dam over, the whole Hollow wrapped in stillness. Hortense came bustling over the second she saw him, ready to help him off with his heavy cloak. “I’m glad to be home at last,” he told her, the words soft — words he had never spoken of the small, cramped cottage before, that had always felt to him like a prison.

He sat by the fire, then got up and walked to the window, then paced back again. “This parlour looks so clean, so bright,” he said.
“I had the whole house scrubbed top to bottom while you were gone,” Hortense told him proudly.
“Then we ought to have a guest for tea,” he said, “to see all your hard work. Not Miss Mann — it’s too cold for her to walk all this way. Not Miss Ainley, she lives too far. Send Harry Scott to the rectory, tell Caroline Helstone we’d be honoured if she’d come spend the evening with us.”

Hortense fussed a little that it was too late, but he was firm, and ten minutes later Harry was off with a hasty pencil-scrawled note. Before Sarah had time to fret about her overdone toast, Caroline was through the kitchen, stopping to pet the new tortoiseshell kitten curled on the hearth, to murmur to the canary that the firelight had startled on its perch, to chat a minute with the cook before she tripped up the stairs to take off her bonnet and furs. She came back down with her soft curls neatly smoothed, her merino dress spotless, her little work bag hanging from her wrist, and the two cousins greeted each other with the quiet, gentle warmth of people who had ached to see each other for months. The new lamp burned bright, the singing tea urn hummed, and a soft, delicate pleasure, like the scent of wild rose, hung light in the small parlour.

“I am glad to be home,” Moore said again, as they all sat round the table. Hortense chattered most of the meal, gushing over how Caroline’s cheeks had filled out, how her colour had come back — it was true. There was a new lightness to her, all the old heavy depression and fear and loneliness melted away, as if she had drunk deep of a cordial of perfect heart’s ease.

After tea, Hortense suddenly remembered she had not sorted through her dress drawers in a month, and trotted off upstairs to tend to that long deferred task, leaving the two of them alone. Caroline took up the conversation easily, her voice softer than ever, with a new, sweet lilt to it, her face lighting up with animation he had never seen there before.
“Caroline, you look like you’ve heard nothing but good tidings lately,” he said, after watching her for several quiet, enchanted minutes.
“Do I?” she smiled.
“I sent for you this evening thinking you’d cheer me up,” he admitted. “But you’ve cheered me far more than I ever dared hope.”
She told him all about her mother, Mrs. Pryor — how the weeks of quiet nursing had bound them closer than she had ever dreamed possible, how she spent all her days sewing for her, walking with her, listening to all her mother’s quiet stores of wisdom, falling more in love with her every single day. Then she told him she was glad he was well, and glad they were friends again, after she had once feared they never would be.
